  - Confirmed fix: Emerald (BPEJ, 16MB) dumps cleanly on first try on both IOS58 (test_16) and external d2x-cIOS (test_15 re-run). Log folder convention: `logs\USB Test\test_N\` = external user #1 (d2x-cIOS).
- **USB hardware probe test results (test_15/test_16 IOS58 — the developer's system, 2026-07):**
  - `gbop_probe_hardware()` added to the dev menu. Tests T1–T4 characterise IOS/USB behaviour for a connected cart. Results go to log file and TV console.
  - **T1a (60-byte ACK read, cmd 0x04):** On IOS58, `USB_ReadBlkMsg(60)` for the ACK returns rd=60 correctly. A 4-byte footer before chunk[0] is **non-deterministic across runs** — present in some runs, absent in others. When absent, chunk[1]=rd=16; when present, chunk[1]=rd=60. This is a timing artifact from IOS USB buffering (the 4-byte packet sometimes arrives fast enough to be absorbed between the ACK and chunk[0] reads, sometimes not). No behavioral impact on production code, which only reads chunk[0].
  - **T1b (64-byte ACK read, cmd 0x04):** Results are reversed relative to T1a — when T1a has the footer, T1b does not, and vice versa. Both reads share the same IOS buffer ordering; the 4-byte packet falls in whichever gap IOS places it based on timing.
  - **T2 (ROM cmd 0x00 handshake):** fd is spent by the 3rd EP OUT write (T1a=1 write, T1b=1 write, T2 ROM cmd=3rd write → fd spent). Host ACK write returns w=-7005. On the second run (test_16, after mGBA session): T2 cmd ACK chunk[1]=rd=60 with non-zero data `[0..3]=09 00 30 01` — streaming data leaked into chunk[1] because the device entered streaming-ready state after the ROM cmd handshake. Fresh-fd wait in production `play_game` handles the fd-spend correctly.
  - **T3 (batch-1 ACK drain):** Fresh fd from T2's reopen. ROM cmd tx=64 succeeds, but cmd ACK immediately returns rd=-7005. IOS EP IN buffer still holds residuals from T2's incomplete handshake (the 60+4 response and leaked streaming data). The T3 fd is tainted by T2's residuals and immediately stalls. This is a probe artifact — production code always uses a fresh handle for ROM dump after the play_game fresh-fd wait.
  - **T4 (GBA save cmd 0x02 ACK + stream):** New fd. Save cmd ACK: chunk[0]=rd=60 zeros, 4B footer present (rb=4), chunk[1]=rd=60 zeros. GBA peek r1=60 zeros. First 4 save packets all zeros (rd=60/60/16/60 streaming pattern). All-zero save data observed for both test_15 (FireRed BPRJ) and test_16 (Emerald BPEJ). Root cause unclear — may reflect probe T4 not precisely sequencing the drain before streaming (rb=4 causes the `rb < 0` drain guard to skip the 80-byte ready-to-stream drain, so the first data reads may be consuming drain packets rather than save data). Probe T4 is diagnostic-only; production `gbop_read_save` is separately verified correct and bit-perfect.
  - **Why probe code didn't break ROM dumping:** The test_14→test_15 probe fix (replacing `gbop_open_hw()` with `gbop_reopen()` to avoid SET_CONFIGURATION hang on d2x-cIOS) had no effect on `gbop_dump_rom`. The drain[1]=rd=16 defect was pre-existing in `gbop_dump_rom` and affected all 16MB GBA ROM dumps on d2x-cIOS. It first appeared in test_15 logs because that was the first Emerald dump attempted after the probe was added to the dev menu; the probe and the ROM dump regression are causally unrelated.
- **d2x-cIOS cart detection intermittent + ROM header failure root cause (test_external_12 log1.txt, 2026-07):**
  - External user #2 (d2x-cIOS, fd=-434241503 never cycles) kept a cart inserted but got NOCART on most cmd 0x04 attempts. After many retries, two detections succeeded: one C0 DE garbage response (`resp[0..1]=C0 DE, resp[3]=0x04, rom=0KB`) and one valid (`resp[0..29]: 0A 00 30 01 01 18 11 31 02 02 00 00 00 50 41 58 50 30 02 53 00 00 00 00 00 00 0A 00 0A 02`, type=GBA, ROM=32768KB, title="PAXP0"`, RAM=0KB reported due to missing Sapphire quirk check).
  - **C0 DE check missing from production code:** CLAUDE.md claimed the check was added, but the actual code had no `resp[0]==0xC0 && resp[1]==0xDE` guard. **Fixed:** added before the `resp[3:5]==0` NOCART check in `gbop_read_cart_info`.
  - **Sapphire quirk missing from production code:** Also claimed to exist, but absent. **Fixed:** added `if (sc == 0 && resp[26] != 0 && resp[28] == resp[26]) { sc = 2; }` in `gbop_read_cart_info`. For PAXP0: `resp[26]=resp[28]=0x0A` → quirk fires → 128KB Flash.
  - **`gbop_read_rom_header` ZLP-terminated drain missing (critical):** After the host ACK, on d2x-cIOS the device sends 8 non-ZLP packets then a ZLP before ROM data. The drain code only handled `r1==16` (IOS58 GBA). For `r1=64` (d2x-cIOS), the drain was skipped entirely. The 8 non-ZLP packets hit the ROM data read loop as zeros, and the ZLP appeared at chunk 5 (320 bytes) → `read error rd=0 at rx=320 chunk=5` → `Got 320 bytes in 5 chunks [0..7]=00 00 00 00` (all zeros). **Fix:** Added `else if (r1 != 60 && r1 > 0 && s_ack_read_size == GBOP_PKT_SIZE)` ZLP-terminated drain loop (up to 32 reads, breaks on rd=0) to both `gbop_read_rom_header` and `gbop_dump_rom`.
  - **`gbop_bulk_recv` ZLP re-read missing:** After `gbop_read_rom_header` reads its 512 bytes, d2x-cIOS sends a batch-terminator ZLP. IOS does not flush EP IN on close/reopen, so this ZLP persists. The next `gbop_bulk_recv` (cart poll) reads it as `ra=0`, then reads the real ACK as chunk[0] and cart info as chunk[1]. Since `memcpy(out, s_resp, 60)` copies chunk[0] (real ACK = all zeros), the result is NOCART. **Fix:** Added `if (ra == 0) { lprintf("[gbop] ZLP before ACK..."); ra = gbop_ack_read(dev); }` in `gbop_bulk_recv` before the `if (ra < 0) return -1` check.
  - **Probe T2 data (d2x-cIOS, with cart PAXP0):** `rsp[0] rd=64 [0..3]=0A 00 30 01` (cart info leftover from T1b in IOS buffer), `rsp[1..5] rd=64 zeros`, `rsp[6] rd=0` (ZLP). Confirms 7 drain reads (6 non-ZLP + ZLP) in T2's contaminated buffer. T3 showed 8 non-ZLP + ZLP = 9 total in a clean buffer. Drain loop safety limit 32 handles both.
  - **First ROM data chunk on correct first attempt (first detection, line 206):** `[0..7]=0A 00 30 01 01 18 11 31` — these are the cart info bytes that leaked through (IOS buffer from cmd 0x04 success, consumed as "ROM data" because drain was skipped). Confirms drain was entirely absent for r1=64.
  - **Log folder:** `logs\USB Test\test_external_12\` = external user #2 (d2x-cIOS, fd never cycles). `log.txt` = probe-only run. `log1.txt` = extended session with multiple detect attempts and both probe runs.
- **GBA ROM-size doubling confirmed NOT cart-specific + probe tool fd-exhaustion diagnosed (test_19, test_external_14, 2026-07-26):**
  - `logs\USB Test\test_19\log.txt` (the developer's IOS58 hardware): Pokemon Emerald (BPEJ) 16MB ROM dump completed cleanly to 100% with correct final drain — baseline protocol unaffected by any of the changes below. Immediately after, `gbop_probe_hardware()` was run: Test 0 got 2 valid cart-info reads, then `gbop_reopen()` itself started returning NULL ("open failed") for the rest of Test 0, and **every** call in Tests 1–4 failed the same way. Root cause: the probe calls `gbop_reopen()` ~11 times in quick succession with no fresh-fd wait, so it exhausts the same IOS58 fd budget documented elsewhere (fd spent after ~5 EP OUT writes, ~1-3s for IOS to hand out a new one) — Tests 1–4 have never actually run to completion on this hardware. Not a connection problem; a gap in the diagnostic tool itself.
  - `logs\USB Test\test_external_14\log.txt` (external user #2, d2x-cIOS): cart-info for Sapphire (AXPE) read `resp[26]=0x0A resp[27]=0x00 resp[28]=0x0A` → reported `rom=32768KB` (should be 16384KB) — the same byte pattern flagged above as a "Sapphire firmware quirk." The subsequent `gbop_dump_rom` attempt then failed within 9 chunks: chunk[0] came back as `C0 DE 00 00 FF 00 00 00` (a stale garbage packet, matching the `C0 DE` marker seen repeatedly during the session's many failed cart-detect retries) instead of the real ROM header, which appeared one packet later in chunk[1] — a residual/stale packet from the earlier detect churn desynced the stream immediately.
  - `logs\USB Test\test_external_14\log_second-external-user.txt` (a separate, alternate external tester — a different Wii than the one above): the **identical** `resp[26]=0x0A resp[27]=0x00 resp[28]=0x0A` pattern appeared for **FireRed (BPRE, logged as title "PBPR0")**, again reporting 32768KB for a cart that is actually 16384KB, and recurred again later in the same log for Sapphire (AXPE/"PAXP0"). Two different cartridges (different flash chips) on two different Wiis both showing the exact same off-by-one size code (0x0A instead of 0x09) plus the exact same broken save-code (0x00 instead of 0x02) rules out a per-cart device firmware defect — this is tied to the connection/host environment, not the cartridge. The **title bytes** (resp[13..17]) were correct and self-consistent in every case (matching the `P<3-char-code>0` convention exactly), which rules out a generic byte-shift/corruption explanation for resp[26] specifically — whatever causes this, the response buffer alignment itself is intact.
  - **Working theory:** the existing Test 0 comment already predicted this — on IOS58, `USB_WriteBlkMsg` naturally takes ≥200ms, incidentally giving the GB Operator's own internal cart-probe time to settle before the first command after an open is answered; on d2x-cIOS, writes complete in <10ms, removing that incidental settle time. resp[26]=0x0A / resp[27]=0x00 looks like a stale/default value returned when the device is queried before its own probe has settled.
  - **Fixes implemented (not yet hardware-verified):**
    1. `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` — new shared helper in `gb_operator.c` (60ms × up to 75 tries, matching the fresh-fd pattern already used by `play_game`/ROM-install/header-read recovery). `gbop_probe_hardware()` now falls back to it via a local `probe_reopen()` wrapper whenever a plain `gbop_reopen()` returns NULL, so Tests 1–4 can actually run instead of cascading "open failed."
    2. `gbop_reopen()` now sleeps `GBOP_D2X_SETTLE_US` (250ms) after a successful open, gated on `s_ack_read_size == GBOP_PKT_SIZE` (d2x-cIOS already detected this session) — never fires on IOS58. Tests the settle-time theory directly without touching any already-verified IOS58 timing.
    3. `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()` — new function in `gb_operator.c`. Deliberately **not** a hardcoded per-game size table: real GBA ROM chips mirror when addressed past their physical capacity (address lines wrap to 0), so if the reported size is exactly 2x too large, the "second half" of a read is bit-identical to the first half (Nintendo logo, reset vector — not a coincidence for real game data). Gated on the same anomalous byte pattern (`resp[27]==0`, `resp[28]==resp[26]`) so it only runs — and only costs the extra transfer time — for carts that already show the suspect pattern; a correctly-reporting cart never pays for it. Streams up to the reported halfway point (the same data volume a corrected dump needs anyway) via the existing `gbop_dump_rom()`, compares a 4KB window there against offset 0, and halves `info->rom_size_kb` if they match. Always leaves the device mid-stream, so the caller must close and reopen a fresh handle afterward regardless of outcome (`main.c`'s new `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` helper does this, and is called before every `rom_cache_dump()` in `play_game` and both dev-menu Dump ROM paths; each site re-checks `rom_cache_exists()` after correction so an already-correctly-cached ROM from a prior corrected dump isn't needlessly re-transferred).
  - **Log folder note (correcting prior convention drift):** `logs\USB Test\test_external_14\log.txt` = external user #2 (d2x-cIOS, fd never cycles; same device-ID pattern as test_external_9/12). `log_second-external-user.txt` in the same folder = a **different, separate external tester on a different Wii** — not external user #2, despite sharing the folder.
- **Probe fd-wait fix confirmed working; ZLP-mid-stream infinite loop found and fixed (test_20, test_external_15, 2026-07-27):**
  - `logs\USB Test\test_20\log.txt` (the developer's IOS58 hardware): Pokemon Emerald 16MB ROM dump completed cleanly (100%, correct final drain) — baseline still unaffected. The `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` fix to `gbop_probe_hardware()` (added 2026-07-26) is confirmed working here: all 5 tests (0–4) now run to completion instead of cascading "open failed" after Test 0, including two `probe reopen failed — waiting for fresh fd` recoveries mid-probe that resolved and let the remaining tests proceed. `GBOP_D2X_SETTLE_US` correctly never fired (this hardware's `s_ack_read_size` stays at 60 — 60-byte cart-info reads succeed here despite the d2x-style negative fd, confirming the fd's sign alone is not a reliable IOS-variant indicator; `s_ack_read_size` from actual read behavior is the only thing that matters and the gate is keyed on it correctly).
  - T4 (save cmd ACK) showed all-zero bytes across the first ~4 sampled packets for a FireRed-class save. This is very likely **not** a bug — real GBA saves observed elsewhere in testing have their first non-zero byte around offset 0x0FF4 (4084), so zeros for the first ~240 bytes is the expected shape of a real save, not evidence of the old "host-ACK-triggers-Flash-erase" all-zeros bug. However, **T4's own drain logic is stale**: it does a conditional single-packet "GBA peek" (`if (r1==16) drain 3 more`) that doesn't match the current production `gbop_read_save()`, which for GBA unconditionally drains exactly 3 fixed packets when the ACK's 4-byte footer times out (`rb<0`), with no peek/conditional step. T4's sampled "first 4 save packets" are therefore offset by a different amount than what a real `gbop_read_save()` call would see as its first 4 data packets. Diagnostic-only impact (T4 doesn't feed any production path) but the byte offsets it reports should not be treated as directly comparable to a real save read until reconciled.
  - `logs\USB Test\test_external_15\log_userA.txt` and `log_userB.txt` (User A and User B — **only 3 distinct external Wiis have actually been tested in total across every session in this project**; device IDs are NOT a reliable way to tell sessions apart, see below, so any earlier count inferred from them is unreliable and the real per-log mapping is tracked by who ran the test, not by ID). Cart detection remains highly unreliable for both users in this session. User A intermittently got the cart to respond, and it showed the **same** `resp[26]=0x0A resp[27]=0x00 resp[28]=0x0A` (Sapphire/AXPE, "PAXP0") pattern seen in earlier sessions. User B never got a single successful cart-info read across the entire session (every attempt returned `resp[3:5]==0`, "no cart" — not even one `C0 DE` garbage response) — this looks like a genuinely poor physical connection (cart seating, connector wear, or GB Operator unit quality) on that specific setup rather than anything a protocol/timing fix can address.
  - **Device-ID fingerprinting was a mistake — corrected 2026-07-27:** the exact same 4 USB device IDs (`-434175967`, `-434241503`, `-434307039`, `-434372575`) turn up across test_external_9, _12, _14 (both files), and _15 (both files), and test_external_1 (the earliest external session) shows yet another distinct pair of IDs. These are not per-hardware fingerprints — likely deterministic/session-derived values from d2x-cIOS's device enumeration, not tied to a physical unit. Every prior "Log folder" note in this file that claimed a specific log came from "external user #2" or "a different Wii" based on matching or differing device IDs should be treated as unverified until cross-checked against who actually ran that test. IOS/cIOS identity is now logged directly at boot (see "What is in progress" above) so future sessions don't need to rely on this kind of inference at all.
  - **Root cause of a reported "ran ZLP mid-stream infinitely" ROM dump hang (User A) — found via `test_external_15` probe Test 3:** with the cart intermittently connected, Test 3 got through the ROM-read handshake and 8 real data packets before the very next read returned `rd=0` (ZLP) and never recovered (`T3: read error at iter=8 raw=9 rd=0`) — a ZLP at raw stream position 9, nowhere near a legitimate 512-byte batch boundary. The probe's own Test 3 loop treats any `rd<=0` as an immediate hard error and breaks (safe by construction), but **`gbop_dump_rom`'s mid-stream ZLP handling (`if (rd == 0) { ...; continue; }`) had no bound** — it was written for the legitimate, transient d2x-cIOS batch-terminator case (one ZLP, always immediately followed by resumed real data) and assumed the stream always recovers. On a connection this marginal, the device can stop streaming entirely while `USB_ReadBlkMsg` keeps returning `rd=0` promptly (not hanging on the syscall itself — confirmed by the probe getting a real `rd=0` return, not a stuck read) — so the `continue` spins forever, printing `[gbop] ZLP mid-stream ...` on every iteration. This is almost certainly what User A saw as an "infinite ZLP mid-stream" during a real ROM dump attempt of the same Sapphire cart with the same connection. **Not caused by the settle-delay change** — the probe reopens (which also carry the settle delay) still reached real streaming data before failing, so the failure is the connection itself, independent of the added delay.
  - **Fix:** added `dump_zlp_streak` to `GBOpDevice`, reset on any non-ZLP read and incremented on each mid-stream ZLP; if it exceeds `GBOP_MAX_ZLP_STREAK` (64 — comfortably below anything a legitimate single-ZLP batch terminator would ever reach), `gbop_dump_rom` now logs `"ROM stream dead: N consecutive ZLPs ... — aborting"` and returns -1 like any other read failure, instead of looping forever. This also protects `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()` (added 2026-07-26), which streams via `gbop_dump_rom()` and would otherwise hit the exact same hang on precisely the carts its quirk-gate targets (Sapphire/AXPE, the cart User A has) — every use of the mirror-check on a marginal connection was, until this fix, a new place the infinite loop could trigger.

- **NEW FIRMWARE PROTOCOL REBUILT FROM WIRESHARK (test_21 follow-up, 2026-07-27):** The developer captured fresh Epilogue Playback ↔ GB Operator USB traffic against the updated firmware (`logs/Firmware-V10_0_10-WireShark/`: `insert_withdraw_GBA.pcapng`, `insert_withdraw_GBC.pcapng`, `rom_dump_GBA.pcapng`, `save_dump_GBA.pcapng`, `save_upload_GBA.pcapng`; GB Operator firmware v10.0.10, Playback v1.10.0/52f3150b, System v2.0.0, capture tool `tshark.exe` from the local Wireshark install, fields `usbcom.data.in_payload`/`usbcom.data.out_payload`). This is a from-scratch protocol rebuild, not a patch to the old understanding — **the new firmware uses a materially different, and much simpler, protocol than the one gbopyrator/the original Epilogue capture documented.** All of the following is grounded directly in this capture; add it to the Sources table entry for Epilogue Playback rather than treating it as a separate source.
  - **USB endpoint descriptors are unchanged**: bulk OUT 0x01 and bulk IN 0x81 both still report `wMaxPacketSize: 64` at enumeration (checked directly in the capture's `GET DESCRIPTOR Response CONFIGURATION`). This rules out a USB Full-Speed→High-Speed renegotiation as the explanation for the 512-byte reads seen in the capture — those are Windows/Epilogue's host-side bulk-read buffer size (multiple 64-byte wire packets coalesced into one URB by the OS), not a device-side packet-size change. The Wii's own 64-byte `USB_ReadBlkMsg` calls remain the correct unit to reason about at the IOS level.
  - **`C0 DE` is a deliberate protocol marker, not corruption or a device error.** Every command response — on real Epilogue Playback, talking to the reference firmware — is bracketed by a fixed-format marker block: `C0 DE <seq> <cmd echo> <~cmd> 00×507`, sent as a full 512-byte block (confirmed by `usb.data_len`, not just a display artifact). `<seq>` is `0x00` for the header (sent right after the command, in place of the old all-zero ACK) and `0x01` for the footer (sent once the operation completes). `<cmd echo>` repeats the command byte that was sent (`0x04` for cart-info, `0x00` for ROM read, `0x02`/`0x03` for save read/write). `<~cmd>` is the bitwise NOT of the command byte (confirmed exactly: cmd=0x04→0xFB, cmd=0x02→0xFD, cmd=0x03→0xFC, cmd=0x00→0xFF). Every "C0 DE garbage header" our code has ever logged and treated as a cart-contact error was very likely this legitimate marker, misread through packet-size/offset assumptions built for the old firmware.
  - **Cart-info (cmd 0x04) exchange, full structure:** `OUT(64B cmd)` → `IN(512B header: C0 DE 00 04 FB…)` → `IN(512B real response, resp[] in the first ~33 meaningful bytes, zero-padded)` → `IN(512B footer: C0 DE 01 04 FB…)`. Three fixed blocks total, always in this order, confirmed identically across every repeated poll in the capture.
  - **GBA cart-info byte-level diff (same Emerald cart, old firmware vs. this capture, `resp[0..32]`):**
    - Old: `09 00 30 01 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 50 42 50 45 30 00 6D 00 00 00 00 00 00 09 02 00 02`
    - New: `0a 00 30 01 01 18 11 31 00 01 00 00 00 50 42 50 45 30 00 6d 00 00 00 00 00 00 0a 00 0a 02 00 00 01`
    - Unchanged: resp[1..4] (cart-present marker), resp[2]=0x30 (GBA type marker), resp[13..19] (title "PBPE0" + trailing bytes) — the existing title-parsing code needs no changes.
    - Changed: resp[0] and resp[26] both shift `09→0a` in lockstep (resp[0] mirrors resp[26] on *both* firmware versions — a previously-unnoticed invariant, not itself new). resp[5..7] go from always-zero to a fixed non-zero value (`18 11 31`) that recurred byte-for-byte across this Emerald capture *and* every external tester's Sapphire/FireRed logs from previous sessions — meaning **every external tester was already on this firmware (or one with the same fingerprint) the whole time**, which is exactly why they all showed the same "quirk" regardless of which cart they inserted. resp[9] goes 0x00→0x01. resp[27] goes `0x02→0x00` and resp[28] goes `0x00→0x0a` (now mirrors resp[26]) — the old `kSaveSizeKB[resp[27]]` table plus the "Sapphire quirk" `resp[28]==resp[26]` override no longer describes a quirk at all; on this firmware **every** GBA cart apparently reports resp[27]=0x00, resp[28]=resp[26] as the normal case.
    - **CORRECTED 2026-07-27 — resp[26] is not a size field at all on this firmware (see GBC cross-check below): it's a fixed constant.** The initial hypothesis here (`16KB << resp[26]`) was a false pattern-match from a single data point and is **wrong** — disproven by the GBC capture added afterward. Left the reasoning below struck through for the record rather than deleted, since the corroborating-evidence paragraph is still worth having.
    - ~~old firmware encoded ROM size as `32KB << resp[26]`... new firmware shifts to `16KB << resp[26]`~~ — **disproven.**
    - **Corroborating, independent evidence for *some* correct-size derivation existing (still valid):** when Epilogue itself issues the ROM-read command later in this same capture, the 4-byte LE size field it sends is `00 00 00 01` = exactly 16777216 bytes = 16MB — the *correct* real size, not 32MB. Epilogue is not deriving the size from resp[26] the way our old code does.
  - **GBC cart-info + a second cart (Pokemon Silver, GBC, real ROM=2097152 bytes/2048KB, real RAM=32768 bytes/32KB, `logs/Firmware-V10_0_10-WireShark/insert_withdraw_GBC.pcapng` and `rom_dump_GBC.pcapng`, 2026-07-27) — this is the data point that settles the resp[26] question:**
    - Full resp[0..32] for Silver: `0a 00 20 01 01 15 0f 22 00 10 00 00 00 50 10 06 03 2a 0d ae 00 00 00 00 00 00 0a 00 0a 02 00 00 01`. resp[2]=0x20 (GB/GBC marker, unchanged) and resp[13..16] (title 'P' + MBC/ROM/RAM type bytes 10/06/03) match the old documented layout — no change there.
    - **resp[26..29] = `0a 00 0a 02` is byte-for-byte IDENTICAL to the GBA Emerald capture** (16MB cart) **despite Silver being a completely different cart type and a real size 8x smaller (2MB vs 16MB).** resp[0] is also identically 0x0a in both. This is conclusive: **resp[0] and resp[26..29] are a fixed, cart-independent constant on this firmware, not a size encoding of any kind** — the "shift-code" interpretation inherited from old firmware doesn't apply, full stop, not even in a shifted/rescaled form. Whatever these bytes mean now (firmware/protocol-revision identifier is the leading guess, alongside resp[5..7] and resp[8..9] below), it is not ROM or RAM size.
    - resp[5..7] for Silver = `15 0f 22` — different from GBA's `18 11 31`, but this matches the *same* value already seen in the standalone `insert_withdraw_GBC.pcapng` capture of this same cart, so it's at minimum stable per-session. Whether it's fixed across *all* GBC carts (like GBA's 18 11 31 was across three different GBA carts) or varies per-cart is unconfirmed with only one GBC data point. Interpreted as literal 3-byte-LE bytes (the pre-firmware-update GB/GBC formula), it computes to 2232085 — close to but not equal to Silver's real 2097152-byte ROM size, and given resp[26..29]'s fixed-constant behavior above, this is more likely coincidental proximity than a real (if imprecise) size encoding. Not resolved.
    - resp[9] = 0x10 for Silver vs 0x01 for GBA Emerald — varies by cart, but no formula involving bank counts, KB-shifts, or byte-count interpretations tried so far produces either cart's correct real size from this byte.
    - **Net conclusion: no field in the cart-info response has been identified that reliably encodes ROM or RAM size on this firmware, for either cart type.** Given Epilogue independently proves it knows the correct real size for every cart tested (GBA: 16MB Emerald; GBC: 2MB/32KB Silver) without it coming from any resp[] field we can find, the most likely explanation is that **Epilogue resolves size from its own internal game database (by title/game-code), not from the device's cart-info response, on this firmware.** This means our own code likely needs an equivalent strategy for the new firmware: `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()`'s hardware mirror-check remains the right general answer for GBA (works regardless of what resp[] does or doesn't encode). **GB/GBC has no equivalent yet** — MBC-banked carts may not mirror the same simple way flat GBA ROM chips do, so the same mirror-check technique isn't guaranteed to carry over; this is an open gap, not yet solved.
  - **GBC ROM dump (Pokemon Silver, `rom_dump_GBC.pcapng`) confirms the same redesigned protocol as GBA, not a GBA-specific change:** command requests the correct real size directly (`00 00 20 00` LE = 2097152 bytes), exactly one OUT packet exists in the entire capture between the command and the post-dump cart-info re-poll, and total IN bytes (2104320) closely matches the requested size (the small excess is header/footer marker overhead, same shape as the GBA capture). The single-command/continuous-push/single-footer redesign is confirmed to apply uniformly across GBA and GBC, not just GBA.
  - **ROM dump (cmd 0x00) protocol is a complete redesign — the old "ACK every 320 packets" cadence no longer applies at all.** Full sequence confirmed directly from the capture: `OUT(64B cmd, size=16777216 correct)` → `IN(512B header: C0 DE 00 00 FF…)` → device pushes the **entire** 16MB continuously with **zero** host-side traffic in between (confirmed: exactly one OUT bulk packet exists in the entire capture between the initial command and the final post-dump cart-info poll — no in-stream ACKs, no drains, nothing) → `IN(512B footer: C0 DE 01 00 FF…)` once the full byte count has been sent. Total IN bytes across the streaming phase matched the requested 16777216 almost exactly (16778240, the ~1KB difference being the header/footer marker blocks themselves). Every historical "C0 DE garbage in chunk 0," "ZLP mid-stream," and drift/desync bug documented above was almost certainly the old code's periodic in-stream host ACK (`USB_WriteBlkMsg` every 320 reads) landing on a firmware that never expected it and has no defined behavior for it — sending that unsolicited packet, not connection quality, is the more likely proximate cause of every desync symptom on this firmware.
  - **Save read (cmd 0x02) and save write (cmd 0x03) follow the exact same shape as ROM read/the old write findings, respectively:**
    - Save read: `OUT(cmd, size=131072 correct)` → `IN(512B header C0DE)` → continuous device push of save bytes (first chunk observed all-zero, consistent with FireRed/Emerald-style saves whose real data starts around offset 0x0FF4) → `IN(512B footer C0DE)`. No per-chunk handshaking. Command bytes (`cmd[1]=0x03, cmd[4]=0x00, cmd[5]=0x01`, size at bytes 6-8) are unchanged from old documented format.
    - Save write: `OUT(cmd, size=131072 correct, same cmd bytes as read format documented for write: cmd[1]=0x03,cmd[4]=0x00,cmd[5]=0x01)` → `IN(512B header C0DE, cmd echo=0x03)` → host pushes all 2048×64-byte data chunks back-to-back with **zero** IN traffic in between (confirmed: no IN bulk packets at all from the first data chunk to the last) → `IN(512B footer: C0 DE 01 03 FC…)` once the device has consumed the full byte count. Epilogue immediately issues a save-read afterward to verify (visible in the capture as a second `02 03…` command right after the write's footer) — same self-verification pattern our own `cart_sync`/upload code already does independently.
  - **Implication for the existing `gb_operator.c` implementation:** essentially all of the packet-count-tuned logic in `gbop_dump_rom`, `gbop_read_save`, `gbop_write_save`, and `gbop_bulk_recv` (in-stream ACK cycle, adaptive `rd==16`/`rd<16` drain branches, ZLP-streak handling, the d2x-cIOS-vs-IOS58 packet-size auto-detection) was reverse-engineered against the **old** firmware's protocol and does not describe this firmware at all. **IMPLEMENTED 2026-07-27** — see "New-firmware protocol implementation" below.

### Old-firmware support decision (2026-07-27)

The developer was running GB Operator firmware v9.2.0 (a late-2023 build) before the update that triggered this whole investigation — that firmware is what the *original* protocol reverse-engineering (gbopyrator + the first Epilogue Wireshark capture) documented, and what all of the pre-2026-07-27 `gb_operator.c` streaming logic targets.

**Decision: a `settings.ini` boolean, not runtime firmware detection.** Key `use_old_firmware` (0/1, default 0), label "Use old firmware? Tested on v9.2.0 (2023)". When set, `gb_operator.c` uses the old protocol implementation; when unset (default), it uses the new protocol (single command, continuous push/pull, C0DE header/footer, no in-stream ACKs) described above. **Old firmware is unsupported going forward** — this exists only so a tester who hasn't updated isn't left with a broken new-protocol attempt, not as a maintained second path.

Rejected: auto-detecting the firmware version and switching automatically. Two reasons: (1) there is no confirmed field in the protocol that reports firmware version — everything inferred so far (resp[5..7], resp[26..29]) is a fixed constant of unknown meaning, not a documented version string, so any detection would itself be a guess sitting on top of already-uncertain reverse-engineering; (2) old vs. new firmware use *structurally different* streaming protocols, so a wrong auto-detect wouldn't degrade gracefully — it would hang or corrupt a transfer. A static per-install setting matches reality: a user's firmware doesn't change session-to-session.

**Refinement, tried and reverted 2026-07-27:** a first version of this added an automatic runtime check on top of the static setting — checking whether the very first cart-info response of a session looked C0DE-shaped or not, and prompting "Old firmware detected?" if not. **Reverted after the first hardware test** (`Post Firmware Update Test/test_1`): a genuine "no cart in the slot" state on the new firmware can *also* fail to look C0DE-shaped (immediate ZLP, or a run of all-zero packets — neither was ever characterized in the original Wireshark research, which only captured cart-present exchanges), so the prompt fired based on an ambiguous signal and told the user "old firmware detected" when the real situation was just an empty cart slot. **`use_old_firmware` is manual-only now**: `settings.ini`, or a "Use Old Firmware" toggle at the bottom of the dev menu (session-only, not persisted to the ini file) for convenience while testing. `GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH` still exists as a return code for logging but nothing acts on it automatically.

Default assumes the current firmware (v10.0.10) until Epilogue ships another update, at which point this whole investigation (fresh Wireshark captures, re-diff against this document) repeats for whatever changed.

### New-firmware protocol implementation (2026-07-27) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

Implements everything in "NEW FIRMWARE PROTOCOL REBUILT FROM WIRESHARK" above. Structure: every old function (`gbop_read_cart_info`, `gbop_read_rom_header`, `gbop_dump_rom`, `gbop_read_save`, `gbop_write_save`) was renamed to a `static ..._legacy` variant with its body **completely untouched**, and the original public name became a thin dispatcher (`if (g_settings.use_old_firmware) return X_legacy(...); return X_new(...);`). This keeps the old-firmware path byte-identical to before — zero risk of regressing it — while the new implementations live alongside as new code (`gb_operator.c`, big banner comment "NEW FIRMWARE PROTOCOL (GB Operator v10.0.10+)").

- **Shared primitives:** `newproto_read_marker()` reads one 512-byte marker block (assumed 8×64-byte `USB_ReadBlkMsg` reads — see hardware caveat below) and checks it against `C0 DE <seq> <cmd> <~cmd>`; returns a distinct code for "doesn't look like a marker at all" so cart-info can turn that into `GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH`. `newproto_is_footer()` checks a single 64-byte packet against the full footer signature (not just the 2-byte `C0 DE` prefix) specifically so a coincidental byte match inside real ROM/save data — a real risk at ~1-in-65536 per packet on the prefix alone across a multi-megabyte stream — can't falsely end a dump early; the full match (cmd echo + ~cmd + 59 more zero bytes) makes that astronomically unlikely.
- **`gbop_dump_rom_new()` / `gbop_read_save_new()`:** send the command with the real byte-count size, read the header marker, then stream via `newproto_stream_body()` until either the caller's buffer is full or the footer marker appears — no in-stream ACKs at all, matching the capture. ROM dump keeps its own persistent per-device state (`np_*` fields on `GBOpDevice`, mirroring the old `dump_*` fields) since `rom_cache.c` calls it repeatedly with small fixed-size chunks; save read calls the shared streamer once since callers already pass a full-size buffer.
- **Size-estimate tolerance:** if the footer arrives before the caller's requested `buffer_size` is reached (estimate was too big), the remainder is zero-padded rather than touching USB again — deliberately the safe direction to be wrong in, per the `rom_cache.c` Do-Not entry. If the estimate is too *small*, current behavior is unchanged from before this feature existed (the caller's loop just stops early) — this is the more dangerous direction (leaves the device mid-stream) and is why `rom_sizes.c`'s fallback for unrecognized games biases toward a generous max guess (32768 KB GBA / 8192 KB GB/GBC) rather than a conservative one.
- **`gbop_write_save_new()`:** sends the header, then pushes all data as plain 64-byte OUT chunks with no per-chunk ACK (confirmed: zero IN traffic during the entire captured write), then reads the footer.
- **Stall safety:** `NEWPROTO_MAX_STALL` (4096) caps consecutive non-progress reads in the stream body, mirroring the old protocol's `dump_zlp_streak` guard, so a dead connection fails cleanly instead of hanging — same rationale as that fix, ported to the new code path preemptively rather than waiting to hit it on hardware.
- **Size resolution (`source/rom_sizes.c/.h`, new files):** since no cart-info field reliably encodes ROM/RAM size on this firmware, `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` leaves `rom_size_kb`/`ram_size_kb` at 0, and `main.c`'s `enrich_info_from_buf()` (called after every ROM header read, which is where the real game code becomes available) fills them in from a small hand-curated table keyed the same way `gametitles.c` is (first 3 chars of the 4-char code, region-agnostic). Table entries marked CONFIRMED were read directly off hardware in this project's own Wireshark captures (Pokemon Ruby/Sapphire/FireRed/LeafGreen/Emerald = 16MB/128KB, Gold/Silver/Crystal = 2MB/32KB); the rest (Zelda, Golden Sun, Mother 3) are No-Intro-sourced but not independently hardware-verified. Unrecognized games fall back to a generous max-size guess (see previous bullet for why).
- **`cart_info_failed()` helper (main.c):** several existing call sites treated `rom_size_kb == 0` as an implicit "cart not really there" signal, which was a safe assumption under the old firmware (cart-info always populated size on success) but is actively wrong under the new firmware (size is legitimately 0 straight out of cart-info by design, filled in later). This new helper centralizes the check: `rc != GBOP_OK` always fails; `rom_size_kb == 0` only additionally fails when `g_settings.use_old_firmware` is set. Wired into `poll_cart` (both attempts) and `play_game`'s two cart-recheck sites — the other `rom_size_kb == 0` checks in main.c run after `enrich_info_from_buf` has already had a chance to fill in a value and were left alone.
- **What is NOT independently verified on real Wii hardware (all flagged in code comments and logs, not just here):**
  1. The exact 64-byte-packet framing within a 512-byte marker block — assumed to be 8 consecutive full-length `USB_ReadBlkMsg(64)` reads with no short packet, inferred from Epilogue's PC-side URB always completing at exactly 512 bytes. The PC-side capture cannot see real USB packet boundaries (Windows/USBPcap aggregates them), so this is a hypothesis, not an observed fact.
  2. What the device does if the requested ROM/save size doesn't match the cart's real capacity. Every capture used to reverse-engineer this had Epilogue requesting the objectively correct size, so behavior for a wrong guess (which will happen for any game not in `rom_sizes.c`'s table) is unknown — could genuinely be footer-driven regardless of the request (the working assumption baked into this implementation), or could behave differently.
  3. Whether `resp[5..7]`'s per-cart-type fixed value (`18 11 31` for GBA sessions, `15 0f 22` for GBC sessions in the captures so far) is truly constant across *all* carts of a type or only happened to match across the small sample tested.
  - Every one of these is exactly the kind of thing this project's established process has always resolved via a hardware test producing a log to read, not by further reasoning from the existing captures — next step is running this build on real hardware and reading what comes back, same as every other fix in this document.

### First hardware test of the new-firmware implementation (test_1, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_1/`, 2026-07-27) — two real bugs found and fixed

Confirms unverified item #1 above was a real problem, and surfaces a second, unrelated one.

- **Bug 1 — cart-info data block is not a fixed 8-packet length; rigid assumption corrupted a present-cart read into NOCART.** `log.txt`/`log2.txt` show the same physical cart (Emerald, "PBPE0" — same cart as test_21, confirmed present) sometimes read correctly and sometimes didn't, purely based on how many padding packets happened to follow the real data that exchange. Direct evidence in `log1.txt` T0[500ms] (lines 105-124): header marker matched cleanly (`C0 DE 00 04 FB`, 8 packets), but the packet in the *footer* slot came back as `0A 00 30 01 01...` — real cart data, not a footer — logged as `"cart-info footer did not match C0DE shape (continuing)"`. The old rigid implementation always read exactly 8 packets for the data block before moving to the footer; when the device's actual padding count after the real data packet was fewer than 7 that exchange, the fixed-8 read ran one packet into the footer, corrupting it into what looked like a bogus continuation of cart data and leaving the real `resp[]` bytes from *this* exchange effectively lost (the code had already captured pkt[0] as resp — which in the misaligned case could itself have been an artifact from the previous exchange's leftover framing). Net effect: an inserted, working cart intermittently reads as "Cart not detected."
  - **Fix:** `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` no longer assumes a fixed data-block length. It reads exactly one packet as the real data (matches every capture seen so far — data always comes immediately after the header), then reads packets one at a time, discarding each as padding until one matches the full footer signature (`newproto_is_footer()`) or a 32-packet safety cap is hit. This mirrors the same content-driven, self-terminating design already used for ROM/save streaming (`newproto_stream_body`) rather than trusting a fixed count anywhere in the new protocol.
  - **Still not fully understood:** why the padding count varies between exchanges at all (T0[100ms] in the same session showed a clean 8+unlogged-8+8 pattern; T0[500ms] didn't). Possibly connection-quality-related (dropped/delayed packets), possibly a genuine device-side variable-length padding scheme. The fix doesn't need to know which — it works either way — but this is worth keeping in mind if new footer-related anomalies show up elsewhere.
- **Bug 2 — automatic firmware-mismatch detection produced false positives on genuine "no cart" states, and was confusing rather than helpful.** Every `T0[Nms]` "no cart" case in `log1.txt` before a cart was found returned `rc=-3` (`GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH`) — the header read got either an immediate ZLP or a run of all-zero packets, neither of which was ever characterized in the original Wireshark research (every capture used to reverse-engineer this had a cart present or was captured specifically during insert/withdraw transitions with different framing). The auto-prompt (`prompt_firmware_fallback_once`, added in the previous session) fired based on this ambiguous signal, asking "Old firmware detected?" when the real situation was just "no cart in the slot yet" — actively misleading. **Removed entirely** — `use_old_firmware` is now a manual-only choice: `settings.ini`, or a new "Use Old Firmware" toggle at the bottom of the dev menu (`choice==9`, session-only, does not persist to the ini file). `GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH` still exists as a return code for diagnostic logging (`cart_info_failed()` in main.c treats it the same as any other failed read — no special handling), but nothing acts on it automatically anymore.
- **Confirms (again) the connection itself is genuinely flaky on this hardware, independent of both bugs above** — even after both fixes, expect the same dozens-of-retries-per-successful-read pattern already documented extensively above (test_19/test_20/test_external_*/test_21) any time the physical cart connection is marginal. That part was never a software bug and these fixes don't address it.

### Second hardware test (test_2, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_2/`, 2026-07-27) — root cause of near-total detection failure: stale buffer content validated as real data

Bug 1's fix (content-driven footer search) worked as designed — footers were found after wildly varying padding counts (0, 8, 9, 17, 18 packets) without hanging. But detection still failed almost every attempt. Root cause was different and more fundamental than Bug 1: **`newproto_read_marker()` and the cart-info data read both validated `s_rx`'s content without checking that a genuine 64-byte read actually happened first.**

`USB_ReadBlkMsg` returning `rd=0` (a ZLP) transfers zero new bytes — `s_rx` still holds whatever it held from the *previous unrelated read*. The header-marker check and the "first packet after header is the data" logic both did `memcpy` / byte comparisons on `s_rx` regardless of `rd`, so a ZLP where a header or data packet was expected caused *stale content from an earlier, unrelated exchange* to be validated as if it were the live response. Direct evidence, line 159-164: `pkt[0] rd=0 [0..4]=C0 DE 00 04 FB` — zero bytes actually transferred, yet the hex shown is a plausible-looking header (leftover from an earlier successful read) that happened to pass validation, letting a stale/misaligned exchange masquerade as a real one. Line 302 is the inverse and more common case: header matched genuinely, but the very next read (expected to be real cart data) came back `rd=0`, and the resulting stale buffer was used as `resp[]` — reading as `resp[3:5]==0` → reported "no cart" even when a real exchange might have been in progress underneath.

**Fix, in both `newproto_read_marker()` and the cart-info data read (`gbop_read_cart_info_new()`):**
- A leading ZLP is now re-read once before giving up — same precedent as `gbop_bulk_recv`'s existing `"ZLP before ACK — re-reading"` handling, not a new pattern.
- Content is *never* inspected unless `rd == GBOP_PKT_SIZE` (a genuine full 64-byte transfer). Any other length is treated as "not real content" and causes a retry/skip, never a stale-buffer read.
- The cart-info data step now loops (capped) skipping ZLPs/short reads until a genuine 64-byte packet arrives, instead of unconditionally treating the very next read as the data.
- `newproto_stream_body()` (ROM/save streaming) already had this guard from the start (`rd == GBOP_PKT_SIZE` gates the footer check) — it was not affected, only the cart-info path was.

**Still true after this fix, and not addressed by it:** the connection on this hardware is genuinely very flaky — most `cmd=0x04` attempts in test_2 still returned nothing usable regardless of parsing correctness (many exchanges never got far enough to have a header, data, *and* footer all genuinely present). That's the same underlying signal-quality issue documented since test_19/test_21, not a parsing bug, and no amount of read-loop robustness fixes it. What these two rounds of fixes (test_1 and test_2) accomplish is making sure that *when* the device does send a complete, well-formed exchange, the code reliably recognizes it instead of being fooled by leftover state from a previous incomplete one — necessary but likely not sufficient on its own for reliable detection on this specific connection.

### Third hardware test (test_3, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_3/`, 2026-07-27) — the header marker itself can be dropped by the connection

Detection still failed almost every attempt after the test_2 fixes. This time the cause was neither of the previous two bugs — it was a genuine data-loss case the code had no path for at all: **the header marker packet can be dropped by the connection outright, even when a cart is genuinely present**, with the device's real data arriving as the very first packet instead. Direct evidence, line 80: `pkt[0] rd=64 [0..4]=0A 00 30 01 01` — a real, complete 64-byte read (not a ZLP, not stale content) containing genuine cart-info bytes (`0A 00 30 01 01` matches the same Emerald pattern seen throughout this whole investigation) landing in the position where a `C0 DE` header was expected, with no header seen at all that exchange. Since the previous implementation required the header to match before it would even attempt to read data, every exchange like this was rejected outright — most of test_3 was actually *this*, not a total connection dropout.

**Fix — the header is now optional, not required.** `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` reads the first packet and checks whether it's a `C0 DE` header; if so, proceeds as before (drain the marker block, read the following data packet). If it's *not* a header, that packet is now treated as the data directly — no failure, no retry, just reinterpret it. This works cleanly for every case: a real header-less data packet gets parsed correctly (this is the bug this fixes); a genuinely-all-zero packet (old-firmware shape, or a legitimate new-firmware "no cart" reply with no header) still correctly resolves to `GBOP_NOCART` via the existing `resp[3:5]==0` check, since that check doesn't care whether a header preceded it. The footer search after this point is now explicitly best-effort/non-blocking (drains a trailing footer for hygiene if one shows up, never fails the read if it doesn't) since a missing footer no longer means anything is wrong. `GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH` is no longer returned by any code path as a result (still defined for API stability) — there is no longer a case where "doesn't look like a header" leads anywhere except "treat as data," so the old ambiguity this constant existed to signal doesn't arise anymore.

**Not yet extended to ROM dump / save read / save write's header reads** (`newproto_read_marker`, still required/strict for those three). No direct hardware evidence yet that their headers get dropped the same way — cart-info is the only operation exercised heavily enough across these three test rounds to have caught it. If a similar "real data landed where a header was expected" symptom shows up in a ROM/save dump log, the same optional-header treatment should be applied there too.

**New diagnostic tooling per request: USB Probe Test 5 ("Cart-info reliability sweep").** Calls the real `gbop_read_cart_info()` (the exact function detection uses, not a separate low-level probe) 20 times back-to-back with fresh handles, tabulating `GBOP_OK` / `GBOP_NOCART` / `GBOP_USB` / `GBOP_FIRMWARE_MISMATCH` / other counts into a one-line summary. Exists to directly quantify what fraction of attempts produce a usable result on a given connection, rather than inferring it from scattered single-attempt logs — run it after any future protocol change to get a comparable before/after number instead of eyeballing pass/fail across differently-shaped test sessions.

### Fourth hardware test (test_4, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_4/`, 2026-07-27) — Test 5 hangs; every attempt this session genuinely saw no cart

Two findings, not one.

- **Test 5 hang — corrected diagnosis after tester follow-up.** Initial theory (a single blocking `USB_ReadBlkMsg` inside Test 5's tight polling loop) was wrong: the tester confirmed the on-screen "T5 summary" line *did* display (all 20 attempts ran to completion), and the hang happened *after* that, during teardown — i.e. exactly "completed but never committed to the log" rather than "froze mid-loop." That points at `gbop_probe_hardware()`'s own `log_commit_sd()` calls, not the USB layer at all: the function had **nine** `log_commit_sd()` (fclose+fopen) calls across one run (opening line, after T0, T1a, T1b, T2, T3, T4, after T5, and the final "Probe complete"). Repeated fclose+fopen cycling within a session is an *already-documented* heap-corruption source in this exact codebase — `mgba_run()` hit the identical failure shape and was already fixed the same way (CLAUDE.md: "reduced from ~32 to 1 [`log_commit_sd()` calls]... Rule: never call `log_commit_sd()` inside `mgba_run()` except at 'Session ended'"). The probe was never brought in line with that rule when it was written. **Fix:** all nine calls changed to `log_force_flush()` (fflush-only, no fclose/fopen, no malloc/free churn) except the one true final commit at the very end of the function, matching the `mgba_run()` precedent exactly. The Test 5 per-attempt flush instrumentation and widened 300ms pacing added for the (now superseded) blocking-read theory are left in place — harmless, and still useful if a genuine USB-level hang ever does occur elsewhere.
- **Separately, and more importantly: the tester confirmed a cart was fully inserted, and every single cart-info attempt across all three log files this session (three full boot sessions) still resolved to genuine `NOCART`.** Not a testing artifact — per the tester, "this has been the issue the whole time." Unlike test_3, there is no instance anywhere in test_4 of real cart data (the `0A 00 30 01 01` Emerald pattern) appearing at all, not even via the "header dropped, data-first" shape the test_3 fix specifically targets. Four consecutive rounds of protocol/parsing fixes (adaptive footer search, stale-buffer-trust fix, optional-header handling) have not moved this core number.
  - **The "test on PC to rule out hardware" suggestion above was redundant and wrong to ask for — corrected by the tester:** the *exact same* physical GB Operator + cart used to collect every Wireshark capture this whole protocol rebuild is based on is the one being tested here. Those captures already are the PC-side control test, and they succeeded cleanly (that's how the entire new protocol was reverse-engineered in the first place). This rules out the cart, the connector, and the firmware version (identical in both cases) as the cause. The remaining variable is host-side: not "is the hardware bad," but "what does this Wii-side code do differently from Epilogue when talking to the same device."
  - **A concrete, previously-unexamined difference: Epilogue never closes and reopens the USB device between polls; this codebase closes and reopens before every single command, always.** Checked directly against `insert_withdraw_GBA.pcapng`: 94 separate `cmd 0x04` bulk-OUT polls occur across the capture, and zero `SET CONFIGURATION`/`GET DESCRIPTOR` requests (i.e. zero re-enumeration/reopen events) occur anywhere after the initial one at frame 6 — Epilogue opens the device once and reuses that same handle for the entire session. Every `gbop_reopen()` call in this codebase, by contrast, is a full close+reopen, and one happens before *every* `gbop_read_cart_info` call without exception — that pattern exists because of an old-firmware-era IOS constraint (fd exhaustion after ~5 EP OUT writes on one handle — see "IOS USB fd spend" elsewhere in this document), carried forward into the new-firmware code without ever being re-examined for whether it's still necessary, or whether it's actively harmful. If the device's own cart-probe logic is in any way sensitive to the USB connection being repeatedly torn down and re-established (plausible — this project has independently documented IOS not flushing EP IN buffers across close/reopen as a real, repeated source of bugs, i.e. close/reopen on this IOS/device combination is demonstrably not a clean, side-effect-free operation), constantly cycling it could be actively degrading detection reliability in a way that simply never happens to Epilogue, which never does this at all.
  - **Test 6 added to the USB probe to test this directly, not yet run on hardware.** Opens the device once, then runs the same 20-attempt cart-info sweep as Test 5 but on that single persistent handle throughout (no reopen between attempts), logging a directly comparable OK/NOCART/USB/MISMATCH summary. If T6's success rate is materially better than T5's back-to-back-reopen rate, that confirms reopen cycling itself is the problem. A `GBOP_USB` failure partway through T6 (the fd being spent on the shared handle) would itself be useful data, not a failed experiment — it would mean the old fd-exhaustion constraint still applies to the new firmware and a persistent-handle approach for the main app needs a fresh-fd fallback built in, not that the theory is wrong. **Deliberately not yet changing `poll_cart` or any other main-app flow to keep a persistent handle** — that's a real behavior change to code every other operation in this app depends on, and should wait for T6's data rather than being made on the theory alone.

### Fifth hardware test (test_5, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_5/`, 2026-07-27) — a real, confirmed hang, and a log-durability regression, both fixed

`log.txt` is a genuine 0-byte file. Tester reported Test 5 hung, with the last on-screen line being `[newproto] cart-info pkt[0] rd=64 [0..4]=C0 DE 01 04 FB` — a real, non-ZLP, non-stale 64-byte read. Two distinct bugs, both traced directly from that one line.

- **The actual hang: a residual footer from a previous exchange landed as "data," and the code spent up to 32 more blocking reads trying to drain a footer for garbage it hadn't yet recognized as garbage.** `C0 DE 01 04 FB` is a *footer* (`seq=01`), not a header (`seq=00`) — so `gbop_read_cart_info_new()`'s header check (`first[2]==0x00`) correctly didn't match it, and per the "header is optional" design from test_3, it fell through to "treat this packet as the data" exactly as intended for that case. The bug was in what happened *next*: the old code ran its up-to-32-iteration footer-drain loop *before* ever checking whether `resp[]` (now holding footer bytes) was even valid — each iteration another blocking `USB_ReadBlkMsg` call on a connection that, having just handed back a stray leftover packet instead of anything from this exchange, may have nothing further to send at all. That's a real, confirmed hang, not a theoretical one.
  - **Fix:** the `resp[0]==0xC0 && resp[1]==0xDE` sanity check (already present, previously positioned after the footer drain) now runs *immediately* after `resp[]` is captured, before any further reads are attempted — a residual footer is rejected in one step, cleanly returning `GBOP_USB`, with zero additional blocking reads. The footer-drain loop itself only runs afterward, only for genuinely-valid data, and its cap was cut from 32 down to 4 — it was always cosmetic (draining a stray trailing footer so it doesn't confuse the *next* exchange), never required for correctness, since the sanity check above already handles a stray footer showing up as the next exchange's first packet just fine on its own. Spending up to 32 extra blocking reads for a purely cosmetic benefit, on a connection already shown to sometimes stop sending mid-exchange, was the wrong trade — 4 is enough for the hygiene case and far less exposure.
- **Separately: the blank log file is a real regression from the previous session's fclose-reduction fix, not the same bug.** `lprintf()` already calls `fflush()` on every single line (see `log.h`) — so the `log_force_flush()` calls added throughout the probe in the previous round were redundant with that, and never the actual mechanism providing durability. The thing that changed and actually mattered was reducing `gbop_probe_hardware()` to a single real `log_commit_sd()` (fclose+fopen) at the very end: `log.h` documents directly that "libfat may not commit dirty sectors to physical SD until fclose" — meaning with only one fclose at the very end, a hang *anywhere* before reaching it (as happened here, inside Test 5) loses everything back to the start of the function, not just the incomplete part, because none of the intervening `fflush`-only writes were ever guaranteed to reach physical storage. This exactly reproduced: a full session's worth of T0-T4 activity plus a partial T5, all fflushed, none committed, wiped by the power cycle.
  - **Fix:** reintroduced two real `log_commit_sd()` checkpoints — before Test 5 and before Test 6 — while keeping every other commit point in the function as `log_force_flush()`. This caps the probe at roughly 3 real fclose/fopen cycles per run (before T5, before T6, final), nowhere near the ~32-per-session count that caused the original heap-corruption finding (`mgba_run()`, tests 78-80) this reduction was modeled on, while ensuring the two long, repetitive, highest-hang-risk loops each start from a durably-committed checkpoint.

### Sixth hardware test (test_6, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_6/`, 2026-07-27) — persistent-handle hypothesis disproven with a clean number; a real baseline measured; a real second bug found and fixed

The probe completed cleanly end-to-end for the first time (all fixes from test_5 held), giving directly comparable T5/T6 statistics for the first time.

- **Persistent-handle hypothesis (from the test_4 response) is disproven, cleanly.** T5 (reopen before every attempt): `OK=4 NOCART=16` (20%). T6 (single handle held open the whole time): `OK=4 NOCART=15 USB=1` (20%, plus one cleanly-rejected residual-footer read that would have hung under the old code — confirming the test_5 fix works correctly in the wild, not just in theory). Identical success rate either way. Reopen cycling is **not** the cause of the low success rate — that theory is closed. (Test 6 stays in the probe; it's cheap, it's a clean negative result worth having on record, and it independently re-validates the test_5 fix every time it runs.)
- **A real, quantified baseline for this connection now exists: ~20% single-attempt cart-info success rate.** Not "very flaky" — a specific number, measured twice, consistently. This matters directly: the app's various retry budgets (2 retries in `poll_cart`'s removal-confirmation check, 2 in `run_detect_cart_inner`'s retry loop) were sized for a much more reliable connection. At 20% per-attempt success, the probability of getting *zero* successes in 2 independent attempts is 0.8² = 64% — meaning even with the cart constantly, physically present and never moving, `poll_cart` concluding "cart removed" is the *more likely* outcome on any given check, not an edge case. This is confirmed directly in the log: lines 762-786 show a real detected cart (title="PBPE0" successfully read at line 759) go through exactly one more poll cycle, hit two consecutive `NOCART` reads by chance, and get logged as `[poll] Cart removed` — immediately followed by the *next* poll succeeding again and re-triggering a full auto-detect that itself then fails three times in a row (`[detect] No cart found`, line 827) purely from bad luck at 20% odds, not any actual cart movement. This is the direct mechanism behind the tester's "auto detect cart quickly overwrote it to no cart" report. **Not yet fixed — retry budgets need to be reconsidered against this measured 20% baseline, but how many attempts to spend at each layer is a real latency/reliability tradeoff that needs a decision, not a unilateral pick.** At 20% per-attempt, reaching e.g. 95% cumulative confidence needs roughly 13-14 independent attempts, well beyond the current 2-3 at any layer.
- **A second, real, fixed bug: `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` still had the strict/required header check, never updated when cart-info got the "header is optional" fix in test_3.** Directly caused the tester's "detect cart... unable to enrich title" report: cart-info succeeded (line 566, real Emerald data, title="PBPE0"), but the follow-up ROM header read (needed to get the *real* 4-char game code and correct title/size lookup) failed outright — `[newproto] hdr: header marker mismatch/error (m=1)` — because its header packet came back all-zero, and the strict check treated that as a hard failure instead of trying the same "maybe this is just the data, header dropped" fallback cart-info already has. The existing fresh-fd retry loop then burned ~75 rapid reopens (~4.5s) and retried once, hit the identical all-zero-header failure again, and gave up — leaving `rom_size_kb`/`ram_size_kb` at 0 and the display name stuck at cart-info's crude "PBPE0" instead of the real title. **Fix:** `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` now uses the same optional-header pattern as `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` — read the first packet directly; if it's a real header, drain the block as before; if not, treat those bytes as the start of the real 512-byte ROM data instead of failing, with the same residual-footer guard (checking for a stray `C0 DE` prefix and rejecting cleanly, no further reads) ported over too.
- **Still not extended to ROM dump / save read / save write** (`newproto_read_marker`, unchanged, still strict for those three) — no direct hardware evidence yet that their headers get dropped the same way, since no dump/save attempt has been exercised heavily enough in a log to catch it. Same status as before, now confirmed twice for the two operations (cart-info, ROM header) that *have* been exercised enough to catch it — worth assuming the same fix will eventually be needed there too.

### Seventh hardware test (test_7, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_7/`, 2026-07-27) — baseline reconfirmed lower and more variable; retry budgets finally raised to match

The probe completed cleanly end-to-end again (T0-T6, no hang, no lost log data) — every fix from test_3 through test_6 held. No new bug found; this session's value is a second, independent reliability measurement.

- **T5 (reopen every attempt): `OK=2 NOCART=18` (10%). T6 (persistent handle): `OK=1 NOCART=19` (5%).** Both lower than test_6's `20%/20%`, and — combined with test_6 — confirm the per-attempt success rate is **not a fixed constant**: it's varied 5% to 20% across two sessions on the same hardware/cart/firmware. T5 vs T6 within this session (10% vs 5%) again shows no consistent gap in either direction across the two sessions now measured (test_6: tied 20/20; test_7: T5 higher), reinforcing — not overturning — the test_6 conclusion that reopen-cycling isn't the differentiator. Blended across both sessions: 11/80 attempts succeeded (~14%).
- **This directly explains the tester's "cart detection doesn't seem fixed"** despite five straight rounds of real, hardware-confirmed protocol bugs fixed (stale-buffer-on-ZLP, missing-header, rigid-footer-count, footer-drain-before-sanity-check, log-durability regression — none of which recurred this session). Those were all genuine bugs and are legitimately fixed. But the user-visible symptom was never really "the code mishandles a good response" — it's "how often do we even get a chance to see a good response before giving up." At a ~14% blended per-attempt rate, `run_detect_cart_inner`'s old 3-attempt budget failed with a cart physically present roughly (1-0.14)³ ≈ 64% of the time; `poll_cart`'s old 2-attempt removal-confirmation failed (declared the cart "removed") roughly (1-0.14)² ≈ 74% of the time. Neither number moved no matter how correct the parsing became, because the bottleneck was never in the parsing.
- **Fix — retry budgets raised to match the measured baseline, not left as an open question.** This was flagged after test_6 as a latency/reliability tradeoff needing the tester's input, but a second data point (an even lower, not higher, number) makes waiting on that a worse trade than making a reasoned default and letting the tester tune it further:
  - `run_detect_cart_inner` (`source/main.c`): retry budget raised from 3 total attempts (2 retries, 500ms apart) to **`GBOP_DETECT_CART_ATTEMPTS` = 20 total attempts, 300ms apart**. At p=0.14, cumulative failure-with-cart-present drops from ~64% to ~4%; typical (geometric-expectation) latency ~2s, worst case ~6s. 300ms matches the interval already validated by the probe's own Test 5/6 sweeps (widened from 50ms to 300ms in test_4/test_5) rather than introducing a new untested pacing.
  - `poll_cart` (`source/main.c`): removal-confirmation budget raised from 1 retry (2 total attempts, no delay) to **`GBOP_POLL_CONFIRM_RETRIES` = 5 extra attempts (6 total), 200ms apart**. Not raised as high as `run_detect_cart_inner` deliberately — `poll_cart` runs continuously on a ~1s cadence from the menu loop, so a still-imperfect single-cycle confirmation is self-healing on the next tick at low cost, whereas over-lengthening every poll tick would make the background loop itself feel sluggish. A `GBOP_USB` result at any point in either loop still bails immediately as a transient device-side stall (not a removal signal), unchanged from the previous behavior.
  - Both loops preserve the existing behavior of treating `GBOP_USB` (EP OUT stall / device-side transient) differently from `GBOP_NOCART` (no cart / genuinely no response) — only the "how many `NOCART` results before we believe it" budget changed.
- **Not addressed by this fix, and worth tracking if it keeps recurring:** raising retry counts masks a low per-attempt rate but doesn't explain *why* the rate is this low and this variable session-to-session, given the Wireshark captures (same physical device+cart) show Epilogue Playback getting clean responses essentially every time. Candidate explanations not yet ruled in or out: Wii-side USB electrical/signal margin (port, cable, hub), IOS's own USB stack timing relative to the device's internal cart-probe cadence (distinct from the reopen-cycling theory already disproven), or something in the GB Operator's own firmware-side polling loop that Epilogue's Windows USB stack happens to align with better than IOS does. None of these are addressable by further Wii-side protocol-parsing changes — the current retry-budget fix is a mitigation, not a root-cause fix.

### Eighth hardware test (test_8, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_8/`, 2026-07-27) — the raised retry budget worked (Detect Cart succeeded on one attempt); a real corruption bug found in the header-optional fallback for ROM dump/header/save

The raised `GBOP_DETECT_CART_ATTEMPTS`/`GBOP_POLL_CONFIRM_RETRIES` budgets (test_7 fix) are confirmed working: the second "Detect Cart" this session (line 680) succeeded on retry 1 of 19 instead of exhausting the old 3-attempt budget. But the tester reported the enriched title was wrong and the subsequent ROM dump failed — both real, found and fixed.

- **Root cause: the "header is optional" fix (test_3/test_6) was applied inconsistently, and where it *was* applied to `gbop_read_rom_header_new()`, it didn't distinguish two very different situations that both look identical (a non-header, non-`C0DE` first packet):**
  1. The marker was dropped and the packet is genuinely the start of real stream data (test_3's confirmed case — non-zero cart-info bytes appeared immediately).
  2. The marker's *own* first packet (containing `C0 DE <seq><cmd><~cmd>`) was the one dropped, and what landed in its place is simply the marker block's zero-padded tail (`C0 DE <seq><cmd><~cmd>` is followed by 507 zero bytes — losing just that first packet leaves nothing but zero padding for up to 7 more reads).
  - The existing code treated both cases identically ("not a header → treat as data"), so case 2 silently fed 512 bytes of *marker padding* into `hdr_out` as if it were the real ROM header. Direct evidence, line 696-698: `hdr pkt[0] rd=64 [0..4]=00 00 00 00 00` then `hdr Got 512 bytes [0..7]=00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00` — the entire "header" is zero. The 512-byte match is not a coincidence: `NEWPROTO_MARKER_PACKETS` (8×64) is exactly 512 bytes, exactly `probe_size` — losing the marker's head packet and reading through its zero tail lands at *exactly* the requested 512 bytes, with the real ROM data (which starts immediately after the marker) never touched. This is why `game_code` came back empty (title stuck at cart-info's crude "PBPE0", never replaced by the real 4-char code from the actual ROM header) and why ROM size resolved to the generous unrecognized-game fallback (32768 KB) instead of Emerald's real 16384 KB — `rom_sizes_lookup_rom_kb` had no `game_code` to look up.
  - **Neither GBA/GB ROM data nor save data is ever a real 64-byte run of all zero at its very start** (Nintendo logo + entry branch occupy that region), so "the substitute packet is all-zero" is a reliable, cheap signal that it's marker padding, not real data — unlike "the substitute packet is non-zero," which is exactly test_3's already-confirmed recoverable case.
- **`gbop_dump_rom_new()` had the *opposite* problem — a full ROM dump `newproto_read_marker()` call never got the "header optional" treatment at all**, so a dropped-header ROM dump start always aborted outright (line 711: `ROM dump: old-firmware shape seen unexpectedly — aborting`, misleadingly named since nothing about old firmware was actually detected). This is strictly too conservative for the recoverable case (test_3-style, real data in hand) but was accidentally the *safe* behavior for the unrecoverable case (test_8-style, all-zero tail) — for a full multi-megabyte dump, silently splicing marker padding into the buffer is a correctness bug that can go unnoticed until the game crashes (the exact failure shape as the old-firmware `drain[0]` corruption bug, tests #11-12), so aborting was accidentally not wrong there, just not right for the recoverable case either.
- **Fix — `newproto_read_marker()` (the shared helper used by ROM dump, save read, and save write) now returns three distinct outcomes** instead of a binary matched/not-matched: `0` = real marker matched and drained; `1` = no marker, but the substitute packet is non-zero and safe to treat as real data (copied out via a new `first_out` parameter); `2` = no marker and nothing safely usable (either no genuine packet arrived at all, or it's all-zero) — callers must fail cleanly here, not guess.
  - `gbop_dump_rom_new()` and `gbop_read_save_new()`: on return `1`, splice the recovered packet directly into the caller's buffer as the first bytes of the stream (with accounting adjusted so the subsequent `newproto_stream_body()` call continues from the right offset) instead of losing it or aborting; on return `2`, fail cleanly.
  - `gbop_read_rom_header_new()`: rewritten to call the shared, now-three-way `newproto_read_marker()` instead of its own inline duplicate of the old, binary logic — removes ~35 lines of duplication and gets the fix directly instead of needing a second hand-applied copy.
  - `gbop_write_save_new()`'s two marker reads (header before pushing data, footer after): no recovery is meaningful here — the device isn't streaming payload at this point, just acknowledging the command — so both now pass `NULL` for `first_out` and treat return `1` the same as `2` (unchanged net behavior, just updated for the new signature).
- **Not yet hardware-verified**: this fix directly addresses the exact failure this log showed for the ROM header peek, and closes an equivalent theoretical gap for ROM dump/save read/write that had not yet been hit in a log (no dump/save attempt had reached a dropped-header moment before this session, since `gbop_dump_rom_new`'s stricter abort-on-any-mismatch behavior meant it was never in a position to *silently* accept bad data the way the header peek was) — next hardware test should confirm a repeat ROM dump attempt on this same flaky connection either succeeds cleanly or fails loudly, never silently with corrupt data.

### Ninth hardware test (test_9, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_9/`, 2026-07-27) — the test_8 fix confirmed working exactly as designed (fails loudly, no corruption); two new bugs found in what happens *around* that failure

The test_8 fix held: both ROM dump attempts this session hit the identical all-zero substitute-packet case and correctly failed with `"header missing and unrecoverable — aborting"` instead of silently writing 512 zero bytes into the ROM (line 616-617, 774-775). No corruption. But two real problems surfaced in the surrounding flow.

- **Bug 1 — a failed dump's 0-byte leftover file gets treated as a valid ROM cache "hit" in every later detect, for any cart.** This session's "Detect Cart" (line 599-608) skipped the real ROM-header-read path entirely and instead logged `ROM header from SD: sd:/apps/wii-gb-operator/roms/PBPE0_.gba` — reading test_8's own failed-dump leftover (0 bytes, since that dump failed before writing a single byte) as if it were a legitimate cached ROM. This is why `code=""` and `rom=32768KB` (the wrong, generous fallback) persisted into a THIRD session with no ROM header ever actually read.
  - **Root cause: `rom_cache_exists()` (`source/rom_cache.c`) compares `actual == expected` where `expected = info->rom_size_kb * 1024`, with no floor.** `try_enrich_info()` calls this *before* enrichment has run, at which point `info->rom_size_kb` is legitimately `0` on the new-firmware protocol (cart-info never populates size by design — see `rom_sizes.h`). `expected` is therefore `0`, and `0 == 0` matches a 0-byte file exactly. **This is not specific to this one file or this one game** — any dump that fails before writing its first byte (which, per test_8/test_9, is exactly what a dropped dump-start marker does) leaves a 0-byte file that will be treated as a valid cache hit for that game from then on, permanently short-circuiting real enrichment.
  - **Fix:** `rom_cache_exists()` now requires `expected > 0 && actual == expected` — a real ROM is never 0 bytes, so `expected == 0` (unresolved size) can never match anything, regardless of what's on disk. No cleanup of existing 0-byte junk files is needed: `rom_cache_dump()` always opens its output file in `"wb"` mode, so the next real dump attempt overwrites it unconditionally once this check stops short-circuiting that attempt from ever running.
- **Bug 2 — the ROM dump's opening handshake (send cmd 0x00, read the header marker) had no retry at all in two of its three call sites, and a weaker one in the third.** Given the dump-start marker read is subject to the same per-attempt connection reliability already measured for cart-info (~5-20%), a single failed attempt here is the *expected* outcome on this connection, not exceptional — the same statistical argument that justified raising the cart-info retry budgets in test_7 applies equally here, and this log shows it hitting in practice: both of this session's dump attempts failed on the very first try, with the dev-menu "Dump ROM" path (`source/main.c`, `choice==1`) having zero retry logic at all.
  - **Fix:** added `dump_rom_with_retry()` (`source/main.c`), a shared helper wrapping `rom_cache_dump()`: retries up to `GBOP_DUMP_START_ATTEMPTS` (15) times in place (close, 300ms, reopen — no fd-freshness requirement, since the failure mode here is a dropped packet, not a spent fd) before falling back to the existing 75-try fresh-fd wait (which targets the different, rarer "fd genuinely spent" failure mode the original `play_game` retry was written for). Safe to retry from scratch every time: every dump-start failure observed so far happens before a single ROM byte is written, and `rom_cache_dump()` always truncates its output file on open. Replaces the ad-hoc single-retry block previously duplicated only in `play_game`'s auto-install path; the frontend-menu and dev-menu "Dump ROM" call sites (`source/main.c` lines ~1982 and ~2164) had no retry before this and now share the same helper.
- **Not yet hardware-verified**: next test should show either a dump completing within the new retry budget, or — if it still fails every time — that the connection's dump-start success rate is even lower than cart-info's, which would be new information worth a dedicated USB probe test (a "Test 7: ROM dump-start reliability sweep," mirroring Test 5/6's cart-info sweeps) rather than inferring it from how many manual attempts happen to fail in a given session.
- **Follow-up (same day): the ROM header read itself had the identical gap.** Asked directly whether the test_9 fixes fully solve "ROM not recognized correctly" — they don't, not by themselves. `game_code` (the real 4-char code, which is what makes `rom_sizes.c`'s lookup return the *correct* size at all, independent of whether the GBA mirror-check ever runs) only comes from the ROM header read, and that read — inside `run_detect_cart_inner`'s "cartindex miss" branch — still only had a single attempt plus one fresh-fd-wait retry (2 total), the same "built for fd exhaustion, not packet loss" tool already found insufficient for cart-info and ROM dump.
