At the same measured ~5-20% per-attempt rate, 2 attempts still fail most of the time, so `code=""` and the wrong fallback size could keep recurring even with the previous two fixes in place. **Fix:** extracted `read_rom_header_with_retry()` (`source/main.c`), same shape as `dump_rom_with_retry()` — `GBOP_HDR_READ_ATTEMPTS` (15) in-place retries (close, 300ms, reopen) before falling back to the fresh-fd wait. Replaces the inline single-attempt block in `run_detect_cart_inner`.
- **Still a known, unaddressed gap: `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()` / `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` (the GBA size mirror-check) has no retry at all** — a single failed marker read there just logs "aborted (read error) — using reported size" and silently proceeds with whatever size was already known (correct, if the ROM header read above succeeded and the game is in `rom_sizes.c`'s table; the generous wrong fallback otherwise). Not fixed yet because its return value doesn't currently distinguish "didn't need to run" from "tried and failed" — retrying blindly would need that signal added first. Lower priority than the two fixes above because the mirror-check is a secondary safety net for games *not* in the lookup table; a working ROM-header-read fixes the common case on its own. Revisit if a future log shows a *known* game (already in `rom_sizes.c`) still reporting the wrong size after a successful header read — that would mean the mirror-check itself is the remaining culprit.

### Tenth hardware test (test_10, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_10/`, 2026-07-27) — real ROM corruption traced to the "recover non-zero data" fallback itself being unsafe; two protocol-level fixes, not just more retries

The ROM header-read retry fix (previous turn) is confirmed working: after 5 failed attempts (all-zero marker), attempt 6 got the genuine header (`7F 00 00 EA 24 FF AE 51` — the real Emerald entry branch + logo bytes) and correctly resolved `code=BPEJ rom=16384KB` (the true size, not the wrong 32768KB fallback) — a real, hardware-confirmed success of that fix. But the ROM dump itself, reported as completing (`[OK] Saved: ...POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba`), produced a file mGBA could not even recognise (`mCoreFindVF: unrecognised ROM`) — a **new and more serious bug than anything fixed so far**: the dump silently "succeeded" while writing a corrupt file, rather than failing loudly.

- **Root cause, traced exactly from the log (lines 1556-1561):** on the 4th `dump_rom_with_retry()` attempt, the marker-check's first packet came back as `13 72 A7 FC 9F...` — non-zero, so the test_8 "recover non-zero data as the start of the stream" logic (`m==1`) accepted it and spliced it into the ROM buffer. But `13 72 A7 FC 9F` is not a valid GBA ROM start (a real Emerald ROM begins with `7F 00 00 EA 24 FF AE 51`, confirmed moments earlier in this same log via the header read) — it was **stale, real leftover data from one of the three earlier failed attempts**, not genuinely fresh stream data. The very next read then matched the footer signature exactly, six packets later — again stale leftover, not a real end-of-stream signal — terminating the "dump" after only 512 bytes (64 recovered + 448 more accepted as real before the false footer). Every byte from offset 512 onward was then zero-filled by `newproto_stream_body`'s "footer already seen, pad the rest" branch, which exists specifically to handle a size-estimate that's too large — not to handle a *catastrophically wrong* footer position. The result: a 16MB file that is genuinely only the first 512 bytes of real data followed by 16MB of zeros, reported as a clean success.
  - **This is the same "IOS does not flush EP IN on close/reopen" root cause behind dozens of bugs elsewhere in this project, seen here for the first time compounding across *retries*:** each of `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s failed attempts sends a fresh `cmd 0x00` and may cause the device to start responding, but a failed marker check aborts without draining anything before the handle is closed for the next retry — so whatever the device already started sending sits queued, unconsumed, ready to be misread as fresh data by the *next* attempt. Test_8's "non-zero = safe to trust" heuristic (correct for its own motivating case — a genuinely dropped header immediately followed by fresh data, confirmed test_3) turns out to be **insufficient** once retries are involved: stale real data left over from a prior aborted attempt is just as non-zero as genuinely fresh data, and nothing in the byte content itself distinguishes the two.
  - **A second, related contamination confirmed in the same log** (lines 6019-6023, 6271-6282): a save-read's header check received a packet that WAS `C0 DE`-prefixed but with the wrong seq/cmd (`seq=01 cmd=04` — an actual **cart-info footer**, left over from an unrelated poll, not this save-read's own header at all). The old code only checked the 2-byte `C0 DE` prefix before accepting a marker as a match, logging the seq/cmd mismatch as a cosmetic warning but proceeding anyway — meaning it drained a completely unrelated exchange's marker block and then read whatever followed as if it were this call's real save data. It happened to work out this time (the save loaded fine in mGBA), but that was luck, not correctness, and the exact same acceptance would have silently spliced a *different* exchange's data into a save read under slightly different timing.
- **Fixes (both in `source/gb_operator.c`):**
  1. **`newproto_read_marker()` now requires an exact `seq`/`cmd`/`~cmd` match**, not just the 2-byte `C0 DE` prefix, before treating a packet as a genuine match for the *current* exchange. A `C0 DE`-prefixed packet that doesn't match is still drained (it's definitely marker-shaped, so consuming it reduces backlog for whatever reads next) but now correctly returns "failed," not "success." Affects all four callers of the shared helper (ROM dump, save read, save write ×2); `gbop_read_cart_info_new()`'s own separate inline marker check already required an exact match and was unaffected.
  2. **`gbop_dump_rom_new()` and `gbop_read_save_new()` no longer use the `m==1` "recover non-zero data" fallback at all** — they now require a fully-matched marker (`m==0`) or fail outright, the same strictness `gbop_write_save_new()`'s header/footer checks already had. Given a false-positive recovery here means *silently corrupting a multi-megabyte file that reports success*, while a false-negative just costs one more retry (already budgeted generously via `dump_rom_with_retry()`), failing cleanly is unambiguously the safer trade for these two operations specifically. `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` and `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` keep their existing recovery behavior — a false positive there produces wrong-but-visible metadata (caught by the existing size/cartindex checks and simply retried on the next detect), not a silent multi-megabyte corruption, so the tradeoff favors keeping the more forgiving behavior for those two.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Worth calling out plainly: these fixes make a bad dump **fail loudly and retry** instead of **silently succeeding with corrupt data** — a real and important improvement, but they do not by themselves guarantee the dump completes faster or more often. If `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s 15-attempt budget still isn't enough to get past a deep backlog on a given session, the practical experience will still be "dump failed, try again" rather than "dump silently wrong." The next log should show whether 15 attempts, now that each one is judged honestly, is enough — if not, the next lever is either raising that budget further or adding bounded draining inside the marker-check failure path itself (deliberately not attempted this round, to keep this fix narrowly scoped to the confirmed corruption bug rather than speculating further).
- **Known, deliberately-deferred residual risk:** `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` (the 512-byte peek used for enrichment) still uses the `m==1` recovery path and could in principle be fooled by the same kind of stale non-zero leftover data, producing a wrong-but-plausible game code instead of a corrupted file. No hardware evidence of this actually happening yet (this session's header-peek attempts all either failed cleanly on all-zero substitutes or succeeded with a genuinely correct header), and the failure mode is self-correcting (a wrong code just gets re-attempted on the next detect) rather than silently destructive, so it's left as-is pending direct evidence it's a real problem.

### Wireshark timing analysis (2026-07-27) — the device itself is not unreliable; Wii-side round-trip timing is the leading remaining suspect, and is now directly measurable

Prompted by "is there a way to learn what makes some tries successful," the `Firmware-V10_0_10-WireShark/*.pcapng` captures (already the basis for the whole new-protocol reverse-engineering) were re-examined specifically for **timing**, not just byte content, using `tshark.exe` (found at `D:/Games/Wii Mod Files/Wireshark/tshark.exe`; the real payload bytes are exposed via `usbcom.data.in_payload`/`usbcom.data.out_payload` — the Wireshark "USB Communications and CDC Control" heuristic dissector, triggered because the GB Operator's bulk interface reports `bInterfaceClass: CDC-Data (0x0a)`; per-packet timing via `frame.time_relative`/`frame.time_delta`). Analysis script and raw extraction were done ad hoc in Python/tshark, not committed to the repo.

- **The device responds fast and with zero flicker while a cart is physically stable.** Across all 94 cart-info exchanges in `insert_withdraw_GBA.pcapng`: the gap from the OUT command's completion to the first 512-byte IN response is consistently **~600-750 microseconds** (measured min 0.481ms, max 0.748ms, avg 0.607ms across 255 within-exchange IN-to-IN gaps), and the response payload is **byte-identical across dozens of consecutive polls** whenever the cart isn't being physically touched. Only 12 CART/NOCART state transitions occur across the whole 94-exchange, ~17-second capture, each isolated to a single exchange and separated from the next by multiple seconds — consistent with a human manually inserting/removing the cart during the recording, not any kind of per-poll instability. **There is no evidence anywhere in this reference capture of the kind of poll-to-poll flickering this project's own Wii-side logs show constantly, even with the cart never touched.**
- **Epilogue's own poll cadence is ~108ms between exchanges** — far slower than the ~700us the device is actually capable of. This is Epilogue's own choice (most likely a UI refresh rate), not something the device requires; the device is ready to respond again immediately.
- **The command bytes are verified byte-identical to what this codebase already sends.** Directly extracted the 64-byte cart-info OUT payload from the capture (`04 00 00 ... 00 b3 55 6b 81`) and computed CRC32-MPEG2 over its first 60 bytes independently in Python: the result (`0x816b55b3`) matches the capture's trailing 4 bytes read as little-endian, exactly as this project's own `gbop_make_pkt()` already does. Rules out any CRC/command-construction mismatch as an explanation for the difference in reliability.
- **No evidence of a "pre-armed read" advantage either.** Checked whether Windows/Epilogue's USB driver keeps a bulk-IN read persistently queued on the endpoint (which would let it catch a fast device response that a reactive "read-after-write" call might miss) by tracing IRP IDs across submit/complete pairs — found no indication of an early-queued read predating the command; the submit/complete shape looks like an ordinary sequential write-then-read, similar in structure to what this codebase already does.
- **Conclusion: the device and protocol are not the source of the remaining unreliability.** Epilogue gets a clean, deterministic, sub-millisecond response on effectively every single poll against the exact same hardware. The ~5-20% per-attempt success rate measured on Wii (test_6/7/9/10) is therefore most plausibly a Wii/IOS-side timing or scheduling issue — most likely the actual `USB_WriteBlkMsg`/`USB_ReadBlkMsg` round trip through IOS's syscall/IPC layer taking meaningfully longer than Windows's ~700us, giving the device's internal state more opportunity to have moved on (or its own response buffer to have been overwritten/reset) by the time the Wii's read call actually arrives. This is a real, testable hypothesis — but it cannot be tested from the PC-side capture alone, because there was previously no timing data on the Wii side to compare against it.
- **Fix enabling the next step: `lprintf()` now prefixes every log line with elapsed time since session start** (`source/log.h`, `gettime()`/`ticks_to_microsecs()` from `<ogc/lwp_watchdog.h>`; reference point `g_log_t0` captured as the very first statement in `main()`, `source/main.c`). Format: `[  1234.567ms] message`. This is the missing piece needed to actually test the round-trip-latency hypothesis above: a future hardware log can now show the real elapsed time between sending a command and receiving each response, directly comparable to the ~600-700us baseline established here. If Wii-side round trips turn out to consistently take, say, tens of milliseconds instead of under one, that would be strong, direct evidence for where to focus next (probably something in IOS's own scheduling latency, not any further protocol change); if they're already comparably fast, that would rule this specific theory out and point back toward something else (electrical/signal margin, or the GB Operator's own polling cadence relative to IOS's, as speculated but not confirmed in the test_7 write-up).
- **Not yet hardware-verified** — the timestamp prefix has not yet been exercised on real hardware. Next hardware test's log should be read with this specific question in mind: do successful cart-info reads show a *characteristically different* round-trip time than failed ones, and how does either compare to Epilogue's ~700us?

### Eleventh hardware test (test_11, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_11/`, 2026-07-27) — timestamps confirm a real, previously-invisible Wii-side latency pattern (though it doesn't cleanly explain success/failure by itself); the stale-garbage-data bug directly caught corrupting the ROM size for a third time

First hardware log with the new `lprintf()` timestamp prefix. Two real results.

- **A genuine, previously-invisible timing fact, confirmed by direct measurement: `USB_ReadBlkMsg`'s round trip on this Wii is bimodal — either ~1ms or ~11ms, never in between.** Measuring the gap from `cmd=0x04 tx=64` to the very next read across the whole session (dozens of samples): the fast mode clusters tightly at 0.97-1.02ms, the slow mode clusters just as tightly at 10.98-11.00ms (one outlier at 11.98ms) — never anything in between. Both are much slower than Epilogue's measured ~600-700us baseline (test_10-adjacent Wireshark analysis), though the fast mode is at least in the same order of magnitude. **Important, honest caveat: this bimodal split does NOT cleanly correlate with success or failure** — both the ~1ms and ~11ms modes produced both `REAL-CART` and all-zero/`NOCART` results in roughly similar proportion across the session. So while this is a real and newly-measurable fact about the Wii's IOS USB stack (likely a fixed internal scheduling quantum — worth further investigation for its own sake, e.g. whether it correlates with which of two IOS-internal code paths services the read), it does not by itself answer "what makes some tries succeed." That question remains open; this rules out one clean version of the round-trip-latency hypothesis (the timing split isn't the direct cause) without ruling out timing *entirely* — even the "fast" ~1ms mode is still ~1.4-1.7x slower than the device's own demonstrated capability, and there's no fast-vs-fast, one-succeeds-one-fails comparison in this log to test whether *some other* timing factor (e.g. jitter on the OUT side, not captured here) still matters.
- **The stale-garbage-data bug (test_10) was still open in `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` — and this log caught it red-handed, three times.** The exact same eight bytes (`13 72 A7 FC 9F 84 4D 73...`) that corrupted a ROM dump in test_10 reappeared **twice more in this session** (lines 635 and 710) as the "recovered" substitute for a missing ROM-header marker — and once even earlier, in test_10 itself. That's the same non-zero-but-wrong content showing up **three times across two separate app sessions**, which rules out "one-off dropped-header-immediately-followed-by-fresh-data" (test_3's original, correctly-recoverable case) and confirms it's a stable, repeatable stale value — plausibly the GB Operator device's or its firmware's own internal buffer/pointer not resetting between attempts, since it reproduced byte-for-byte across a full Wii session boundary. Trusting it (still allowed for the header peek specifically, deliberately left as-is after test_10 on the reasoning that a wrong-but-plausible code is "self-correcting") produced exactly the wrong-file-size symptom reported after this test: `code=""`, `rom=32768KB` (the generous unrecognized-game fallback, not Emerald's real 16384KB) reported as fact for the entire session, and once even a literal single garbage character (`title="="`) where a real title should be.
  - **Fix**: `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` now requires a strict marker match (`m==0`), the same tightening already applied to ROM dump and save read in test_10 — no more `m==1` "recover non-zero data" fallback anywhere in the three highest-consequence read paths. Only `gbop_read_cart_info_new()` still uses the forgiving recovery path; unlike the header peek, its own recovered-data case has not (yet) shown a repeatable-across-sessions stale value in any log, and a wrong cart-info read is cheap to detect and retry (the very next poll re-reads from scratch), unlike a wrong ROM header feeding an entire dump.
  - **On the "POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba" filename question**: that name is real and correct (built from a genuine successful enrichment — test_10's one working header read, before its own dump got corrupted) but this session couldn't reproduce it, because `[index] Entry missing: POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba` fired first (the cartindex still pointed at that file — presumably deleted after test_10 confirmed it was corrupt) and the fallback header re-read then hit the stale-garbage bug on every attempt this session, never once landing on real data the way test_10 eventually did. With the strict-marker fix above, a future session's header re-read should fail cleanly and retry (within the existing 15-attempt budget) rather than confidently reporting wrong data.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next log should show whether `code`/`rom_size_kb` either resolve correctly or the detect fails cleanly and retries — never the wrong-but-confident result seen here. Also worth another look at the bimodal ~1ms/~11ms timing split across a larger sample, specifically checking whether it correlates with *anything* observable (which retry attempt number, time since last reopen, etc.) even though it didn't correlate with success/failure directly in this one session.

### Twelfth hardware test (test_12, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_12/`, 2026-07-27) — the test_11 fix was too strong: it also rejected genuinely correct ROM headers; replaced with a real validity check instead of an outright ban

Confirmed the test_11 fix is working as designed — every failed header attempt this session logged a clean `"header marker mismatch/error"` instead of a confident wrong answer, exactly as intended. But across four separate detect cycles (60 total header-read attempts, all exhausting the 15-attempt budget), the header **never once resolved** — worse than test_10/test_11. Tracing the individual attempts found why: **test_11's fix threw out good data along with the bad.**

- **Direct evidence, line 847:** attempt 7 of one cycle received `pkt[0] rd=64 [0..4]=7F 00 00 EA 24` — this is not garbage, it's the cart's **genuine, correct** header start (`7F 00 00 EA 24 FF AE 51` is Pokemon Emerald's real Nintendo-logo/entry-branch bytes, independently confirmed multiple times elsewhere in this project, including a full clean marker-based read in test_10). Test_11's fix rejected it anyway (`m=1 — treating as failed read`), because it had removed the `m==1` recovery path entirely rather than distinguishing *which* non-zero substitute it was looking at.
- **The actual lesson from test_10/test_11 was narrower than what got implemented:** those tests proved that *raw non-zero content alone* is not a safe signal that recovered data is real (the same stale `13 72 A7 FC 9F...` value recurred three times and is clearly garbage) — not that *all* non-zero recovery is unsafe. Test_3's original motivating case (a dropped header immediately followed by genuinely fresh data) is still real and still happens, as this log's line 847 shows directly. Banning recovery outright overcorrected: it fixed the false-positive problem by introducing a false-negative problem just as costly (a session that never once resolves the ROM header, this log's user-reported "very unsuccessful" result).
- **Fix — a real validity check instead of a ban.** Added `header_checksum_valid()` (`source/gb_operator.c`): validates the assembled 512-byte buffer against the **same header checksum a real GBA BIOS or DMG/CGB boot ROM checks before it will run the cartridge at all** — GBA's complement checksum at offset 0xBD (sum of bytes 0xA0-0xBC, minus 0x19; GBATEK "Nintendo Logo & Header Checksum") or GB/GBC's running-subtraction checksum at 0x14D (bytes 0x134-0x14C; Pan Docs "Header Checksum"), accepting either match since this function serves both cart types without knowing which in advance. This is a **structural, game-independent, hardware-mandated** signal — nothing resembling it exists in a stale/garbage buffer by chance (≈1/256 odds per check), unlike "is it non-zero," which both a fresh real header and a stale garbage packet satisfy equally.
  - `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` now restores the `m==1` recovery path (accepts a non-zero substitute packet as the possible start of real data, matching test_8's original design) but gates final acceptance on `header_checksum_valid()` passing once all 512 bytes are assembled — recovering `7F 00 00 EA 24...` now succeeds (real header, checksum passes), while recovering `13 72 A7 FC 9F...` now correctly fails (garbage, checksum fails) instead of either being blindly trusted (test_8-10's bug) or blindly rejected alongside good data (test_11's overcorrection).
  - **Both checksum formulas were independently verified against this project's own already-captured hardware data before trusting them**, rather than relying on memory alone: extracted the real post-header ROM bytes from `rom_dump_GBA.pcapng` (Pokemon Emerald) and `rom_dump_GBC.pcapng` (Pokemon Silver) via `tshark`, computed both checksums in Python, and confirmed exact matches (GBA: computed `0x6D` == stored `0x6D`, alongside title "POKEMON EMER" / code "BPEJ"; GB/GBC: computed `0x2A` == stored `0x2A`, alongside title "POKEMON_SLV" / code "AAXE") before writing the equivalent C code.
  - `gbop_dump_rom_new()` and `gbop_read_save_new()` are **not** changed by this — they keep test_10's outright ban on `m==1` recovery, since a false-accept there means silently corrupting a multi-megabyte transfer (checked only once, at the very start), which is a categorically worse failure mode than the header peek's "wrong metadata, retried next detect" — and neither of those has produced a single hardware-confirmed instance of rejecting *genuinely good* recovered data the way the header peek just did. If a future log ever shows the ROM dump or save read rejecting real data unnecessarily, apply the same checksum-style validation there instead of reverting to unconditional trust.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next log should show the header resolving correctly (real `code`, real `rom_size_kb`) within the existing 15-attempt budget considerably more often than test_10-12's near-total failure — this is the fix most directly responsible for "the file size is wrong" and "very unsuccessful" reported across the last two tests.

### Thirteenth hardware test (test_13, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_13/`, 2026-07-27) — the checksum fix confirmed working (title enriched correctly); timing analysis directly answers "is it random"; ROM dump was still the strict/unrecoverable case and needed the same fix

- **The test_12 checksum fix is confirmed working on hardware:** `[hdr] Enriched: type=GBA title="POKEMON EMER" code="BPEJ" rom=16384KB ram=128KB"` (line 1059) — the real title, code, and correct size, not the wrong 32768KB fallback. The log also shows the checksum correctly *rejecting* bad recovered data along the way (`"recovered data failed header checksum — treating as failed read"`, lines 703/724/745) without blocking the eventual good read the way test_11's outright ban did. Both failure modes (reject-good, accept-bad) are gone; the fix does what it was designed to do.
- **Timing analysis, directly answering "is it random" — asked and checked with real data across two independent sessions now:** classified every first-read-after-command in this log by *outcome* (all-zero / genuine cart or ROM data / the known stale-garbage value / an unrecognized third value) and cross-referenced against the same bimodal ~1ms/~11ms latency split found in test_11. Result: **every outcome category shows both timing modes in similar proportion** — e.g. all-zero reads: 43 fast / 67 slow; genuine real data: 5 fast / 5 slow; the known stale-garbage value: 3 fast / 7 slow. There is no clean split where one timing mode reliably produces good data and the other reliably produces bad data. **This directly answers the question: no, the specific ~1ms-vs-~11ms Wii-side read latency is not what determines success or failure** — confirmed now with two sessions' worth of data, not a one-off. That doesn't mean the underlying phenomenon is unexplainable, only that *this specific, measurable* timing signal isn't the lever — whatever actually determines which of the three outcomes a given read gets is not something visible from the Wii-side software's own timestamps. A genuine answer would need either sub-microsecond bus-level instrumentation (a real logic analyzer or a repeat Wireshark-style capture, this time on the Wii's own USB traffic — not currently possible with this project's tooling) or ruling out electrical/signal-margin causes some other way; it is not further Wii-side software timing tuning.
- **A new, predictable consequence of test_12's fix, confirmed the same session: the ROM dump was still using the old, stricter, unrecoverable check from test_10 — and with the header peek's success rate now noticeably improved, the dump's comparatively worse odds became the obvious bottleneck.** Every single dump attempt this session (dozens, spanning multiple "Dump ROM" presses over minutes) failed immediately at offset 0 with `"ROM dump: header marker mismatch/error (m=1 or m=2) — aborting"` — including `m=1` cases, meaning some of those failures were plausibly the *same kind of genuinely-recoverable data* test_12 just proved exists for the header peek, being thrown away here for lack of an equivalent validity check.
  - **Fix:** `gbop_dump_rom_new()` now uses the same checksum-gated recovery as the header peek. On a dropped-header (`m==1`) result, the recovered packet is spliced into the start of the caller's buffer as before test_10's ban, `newproto_stream_body()` fills in the rest of the chunk (comfortably large — `rom_cache.c`'s `DUMP_CHUNK_SIZE` is 4096 bytes, far more than the 0xBD/0x14D offsets either checksum needs), and only *then* is `header_checksum_valid()` run against the fully-assembled chunk — accepted only if it passes. A stale garbage value (still failing the checksum, as confirmed for the header peek) is rejected exactly as before; genuinely recovered real data is now accepted instead of wastefully retried. `gbop_read_save_new()` is deliberately **not** changed the same way — a save file has no equivalent fixed-position hardware checksum to validate against (unlike a ROM header), so there is no safe way to apply the same technique there; it keeps test_10's strict, no-recovery behavior, a known asymmetry to revisit only if a future log specifically shows save reads suffering the same wasted-retry pattern the dump just did.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next log should show ROM dumps starting successfully far more often than this session's near-total failure, via either a clean marker match or a checksum-validated recovery — and, as important, should show it still failing cleanly (never silently) on genuinely stale/ambiguous data, same as before.

### Fourteenth hardware test (test_14, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_14/`, 2026-07-27) — a rough session (header never resolved, one T6 sweep hit 0/20), but it directly answered "can we just poll until success and then hold the connection?" using data already on hand, plus a new diagnostic capability added

This session's ROM header never resolved across three full detect cycles (all exhausting 15 attempts + fresh-fd fallback), and the final USB probe T6 sweep hit `OK=0 NOCART=20` — a reminder that the underlying per-attempt connection quality genuinely varies session to session and is still the dominant bottleneck, independent of any of the parsing/validation fixes made so far.

- **Answered directly: "once we get a success, can we poll with a budget until it happens and then just hold that connection, riding the good timing?"** Checked the USB probe's own Test 6 (which does exactly this — a single handle held open, no reopen, across 20 consecutive cart-info attempts) across every run in test_12/13/14: `OK` results **never once landed on two consecutive attempts**, in 7 total T6 runs (~140 attempts). Every single success was immediately preceded and followed by a failure on the very next attempt on the *same already-open, already-just-succeeded* handle (e.g. test_14 run 2: `O......O............`). This is direct, already-available evidence against the "ride the good connection" theory — if a success reflected some persistent, temporarily-good connection state, holding the handle open and immediately retrying should show short streaks of consecutive successes at least occasionally. It doesn't, in any of the 7 runs sampled. Whatever determines a given attempt's outcome does not appear to persist from one attempt to the very next one, even without a reopen in between — success looks like an independent, per-attempt event, not a state you can "catch and hold."
- **On building a USB sniffer into the dev menu:** true bus-level sniffing (seeing raw electrical/framing detail below what `USB_ReadBlkMsg` itself returns) is not possible from software running on the Wii — IOS's USB stack does not expose a promiscuous/monitor-mode capture API the way a PC's dedicated USB debug hardware or Linux's `usbmon` does; nothing below what the existing logging already sees is observable this way. The software-level equivalent, though, is a real, buildable, and immediately useful step: **the existing "first 5 bytes" previews throughout `gb_operator.c` weren't enough to tell whether an unexplained substitute packet has recognizable structure.** Added `log_full_packet()` (`source/gb_operator.c`) — hex-dumps the complete 64-byte contents of a packet — and wired it into the three spots that previously only logged `[0..4]=...`: `newproto_read_marker()`'s first-packet read (used by ROM dump, save read/write header/footer checks), and both of `gbop_read_cart_info_new()`'s own first-packet and data-packet reads. Every future log will now show full packet contents for these events, giving the same level of detail this project's Wireshark analysis had for the PC side, just from the Wii's own vantage point — enough to check, for instance, whether a future "unknown garbage" value is byte-for-byte identical to something seen before (as `13 72 A7 FC 9F...` has now been, four times), or whether it matches a completely different exchange's marker/footer shape that a 5-byte preview would have missed.
- **Not yet hardware-verified** — full-packet logging hasn't been exercised on real hardware yet; next log will be considerably more verbose in the specific spots it was added, by design.

### Fifteenth hardware test (test_15, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_15/`, 2026-07-27) — the ROM dump checksum fix confirmed rejecting bad data cleanly (never silently); the full-packet logging immediately paid off with a second distinct garbage value; and a real, long-standing display bug found in USB Probe Test 0

Session included the boot-with-cart-inserted, USB Probe, Detect Cart, and Dump ROM sequence requested. Header enrichment succeeded again (`title="POKEMON EMER" code="BPEJ" rom=16384KB`), but every ROM dump attempt this session still failed (0-byte file left on SD, included with this log — see below for whether that adds anything).

- **The test_13 ROM dump fix is confirmed doing exactly what it was designed to do: it never silently corrupted anything.** Every failed dump attempt logged either a clean marker-mismatch abort or, when data was recovered, an explicit `"ROM dump: recovered start-of-stream failed header checksum — aborting"` — the checksum gate is firing and rejecting bad data correctly, every single time it was exercised. The remaining problem is a lower-level one: the dump's own marker read essentially never got a *clean* match or *validatable* recovery this session, so the checksum never had a chance to say "yes" — only "no." That's a session-quality/attempt-budget question, not a sign the fix is wrong.
- **The new full-packet logging (added this session's predecessor) paid off immediately: it caught a *second*, previously-unseen "stale garbage" value, distinct from the well-known one.** Two consecutive dump-start retries recovered two different non-zero substitutes:
  - Attempt 3: `13 72 A7 FC 9F 84 4D 73 A3 CA 9A 61 58 97 A3 27 FC 03 98 76 23 1D C7 61 03 04 AE 56 BF 38 84 00 40 A7 0E FD FF 52 FE 03 6F 95 30 F1 97 FB C0 85 60 D6 80 25 A9 63 BE 03 01 4E 38 E2 F9 A2 34 FF` — the full 64 bytes of the now-familiar value (previously only its first 8 bytes were ever logged; this is the first time the complete content has been captured, confirming it's a genuinely fixed, stable 64-byte buffer, not just a matching prefix).
  - Attempt 4 (the very next retry, ~1 second later): `09 06 5A E0 62 46 10 78 00 01 45 46 0C 35 40 19 00 68 00 90 0B 4B 68 46 18 60 5C 60 0A 48 98 60 98 68 A4 19 89 1B B1 42 EC D8 10 78 00 01 40 19 00 68 00 90 6A 46 1A 60 5C 60 88 08 85 21 09 06` — a *completely different* value never seen in any prior log, and one that looks structurally like real ARM Thumb code (small values consistent with register loads/stores/branches — `46 10 78`, `4B 68`, `60 5C 60`, etc.), not random noise.
  - **This is a meaningful update to the working theory.** A single always-identical stale buffer (the original theory) would predict the *same* garbage value recurring within one session's back-to-back retries. Instead, two *different* garbage values appeared on consecutive retries of the same dump-start command. This fits a "device-side internal read pointer not resetting between attempts" theory better than a "one fixed leftover IOS packet" theory: if each new `cmd 0x00` doesn't actually restart the device's internal stream position, but instead continues wherever a previous attempt's stream left off, each retry would plausibly show *different* real ROM content from progressively different offsets — consistent with attempt 4's data looking like genuine code rather than noise. Not confirmed, but a concrete, evidence-backed refinement worth keeping in mind; the checksum fix is unaffected either way, since it only cares whether the *assembled* data is structurally valid, not why a bad substitute occurred.
- **A real, previously-unnoticed bug found and fixed: USB Probe's Test 0 has been mislabeling every genuinely successful new-firmware cart-info read as "no cart" this entire project.** `gbop_probe_hardware()`'s Test 0 required `rc == GBOP_OK && ti.rom_size_kb > 0` to print `"VALID"` — but on the new-firmware protocol, cart-info *never* populates `rom_size_kb` (by design; resolved later from the ROM header). Direct proof this session: T0[0ms]'s actual read (line 1289) shows the cart's real, correct `resp[]` bytes (`0A 00 30 01 01 18 11 31...`, the familiar Emerald pattern) — a genuine success — but the probe printed `"T0[0ms]: no cart (rc=0)"` anyway, purely because `rom_size_kb` was legitimately 0. Every prior test log's Test 0 section should be read with this in mind: `"no cart"` there does not necessarily mean the read failed, only that this specific display condition wasn't satisfied. **Fix:** condition simplified to `rc == GBOP_OK` alone — `gbop_read_cart_info`'s own success/failure already encodes the correct "resp[3:5] was non-zero" signal everywhere else in this codebase; the extra size check was a leftover old-firmware-era assumption never updated for the new protocol.
- **On the included 0-byte `POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba`, asked directly whether it adds diagnostic value: not much on its own** — it's empty, so there's nothing in the file itself to inspect. Its only informative property is confirming what the log already says: `rom_cache_dump()` never wrote a single byte this session, because every `gbop_dump_rom()` call failed before returning success (exactly the intended behavior of the checksum gate — no partial/corrupt data ever reached the file, unlike test_10's original corrupted-but-"successful" 16MB file). The log itself, now with full packet contents, is where all the actual new information came from this round.
- **Not yet hardware-verified**: the Test 0 fix and the working theory update. Next log's Test 0 section should show `"VALID"` whenever a cart is genuinely read, and it's worth continuing to watch whether further dump-start retries within one session keep producing *different* garbage values (supporting the drifting-pointer theory) or start repeating the same one (which would point back toward a fixed host-side buffer instead).

### Sixteenth hardware test (test_16, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_16/`, 2026-07-27) — MAJOR CORRECTION: the "known stale garbage" value was never garbage at all — it's real ROM content, and a precise, previously-invisible 7-packet gap is the actual bug

This session produced the first full-size (16,777,216-byte), non-obviously-corrupt ROM dump — clean marker match, steady progress logging every 512KB, footer seen right on schedule, `[OK] Saved`. The user reported it still doesn't run in mGBA on either Wii or PC. Direct byte-level comparison against the known-good reference already extracted from `rom_dump_GBA.pcapng` (verified earlier for the header checksum work) found the exact cause — and overturned a conclusion this document has relied on since test_10.

- **The dump is a byte-perfect copy of the real ROM, globally shifted 448 bytes early, from packet index 1 onward.** Packet-by-packet comparison against the Wireshark reference: packet 0 (file offset 0x00-0x3F) matches the reference exactly at offset 0; **every single packet from index 1 through the end of the file** (spot-checked at 0x1000, 0x100000, 0xF00000, 0xFFF000 — i.e. essentially the whole 16MB) matches the reference shifted forward by exactly 448 bytes (7×64) instead. The footer arrived exactly 448 bytes short of the requested total (`"footer seen at 16776768 bytes"`, vs. 16,777,216 expected) — the same 448-byte gap accounted for at both ends. This is not corruption in the sense of garbled bytes; it's **7 packets' worth of real, correct ROM content (real offsets 0x40-0x1FF) that never made it into the file at all**, with everything after sliding forward to fill the gap.
- **This directly proves the "known stale garbage" value tracked since test_10 was never garbage — it's the ROM's own real content at offset 0x40.** The value `13 72 A7 FC 9F 84 4D 73 A3 CA 9A 61...` — logged identically in test_10, test_11 (×2), test_12, and test_15, and treated every time as a stale/repeatable device-or-IOS-buffer artifact worth rejecting — is **exactly** what the Wireshark reference shows at real ROM offset 0x40. It recurred identically across sessions not because it was a fixed leftover buffer, but because it's *deterministic ROM content from the same cart*, read the same way every time. **The checksum-gated recovery fix (test_12/13) is still correct and necessary as a safety mechanism** — rejecting data that fails a real structural check is the right behavior regardless of *why* it fails — but the mental model behind it was wrong: those recovered attempts most likely kept failing the checksum not because they were "stale garbage," but because they were genuinely dropped-header cases hitting this *exact same 448-byte gap* before the checksum ever got a chance to see undamaged data. Fixing the gap should make the checksum-gated recovery path succeed far more often than it has so far, rather than needing to be revisited itself.
- **Root cause is not yet pinned down, and a wrong guess here would repeat the test_11 overcorrection mistake** — so a fix was deliberately not attempted this round; verbose diagnostic logging was added instead. Code inspection ruled out the obvious candidate: `newproto_stream_body()` (`source/gb_operator.c`) has no code path that reads a packet successfully and then discards it without writing it to the buffer — every non-footer, non-error, positive-length read is unconditionally copied and counted. Neither does `newproto_read_marker()`'s drain loop, which the log confirms ran exactly the expected 7 iterations (all logged `rd=64`, matching the known-correct 512-byte marker size independently verified against the same Wireshark reference). Since the loss lines up exactly with the transition from the marker-drain phase to the first real-data reads, but neither phase's own code shows an obvious discard bug, the most likely explanation is that 7 real packets were never actually delivered to a `USB_ReadBlkMsg` call at all at that point — genuinely lost in the IOS/USB layer rather than mishandled after the fact — but this is not confirmed.
- **Fix (diagnostic, not corrective): added per-read verbose logging with content preview to the two places that had none before.** `newproto_stream_body()` now logs every read (`rd` value + first 8 bytes) while `dev->np_given < NEWPROTO_EARLY_LOG_BYTES` (4096 bytes) — enough to fully cover the gap region with margin, without flooding the log for the rest of a 16MB dump. `gbop_read_rom_header_new()`'s own 512-byte accumulation loop got the identical treatment unconditionally (it's already short and bounded). Neither of these loops had ever logged per-read content before now — only the marker-check phase did. This is the direct next step toward seeing, read-by-read, exactly what happens (or doesn't) in the gap.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** The next ROM dump log should show, packet-by-packet with content, exactly what the reads between the first correct packet and the 448-byte-later resumption look like — whether reads simply don't happen there (supporting genuine transport-level loss) or something else is visible that code inspection alone couldn't find.

### Seventeenth hardware test (test_17, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_17/`, 2026-07-27) — a second, distinct ROM-dump corruption: the checksum gate itself produced a false accept; ROM dump reverted to strict-marker-only

The new verbose logging (test_16) immediately caught a second, different failure mode in the same session — this time in the checksum-gated recovery mechanism itself, not the still-unexplained gap.

- **The dump this time isn't shifted — it's a complete, full-size, entirely wrong file from byte 0.** Its first bytes (`F0 B5 05 1C 3E 30 01 78...`) don't match the real reference ROM at any offset tried. Tracing the log to the one successful `[cache] Dump started` (of 11 attempts that session) showed why: the marker read got a ZLP, retried, and the retry returned a **non-zero substitute packet — genuine Thumb-code-shaped bytes from *somewhere* in the ROM, not the real header at offset 0** (`m==1`, the "recover non-zero data" path added in test_13). The assembled chunk (this recovered packet + whatever `newproto_stream_body` read after it) then got checked against `header_checksum_valid()` — and it **passed**, specifically the GB/GBC checksum (confirmed by directly computing both checksums against the actual saved file: GBA check fails, GB check passes) — a **false positive**. The dump proceeded from there, streaming an entire 16MB "ROM" that is genuine cart content, just starting from the wrong offset entirely, accepted as correct because one of two 8-bit checksums happened to match by chance.
- **This is a real, structural flaw in using a single-byte checksum to gate something this consequential, not a one-off fluke.** An 8-bit checksum match happens by chance roughly 1-in-256 per format; checking two formats (GBA and GB/GBC, accepting either) roughly doubles that to ~1-in-128 per attempt. This session made 11 dump-start attempts before one "succeeded" — the probability of at least one false accept across that many tries is `1-(127/128)^11 ≈ 8%`, not a rare edge case. The header peek's own use of this same check (test_12) is a fundamentally different risk: a false accept there produces wrong-but-plausible *metadata* that gets caught and retried on the next detect. A false accept in the ROM dump produces a *complete, wrong 16MB file that reports success* — a categorically worse outcome that this same checksum strength cannot safely gate against.
- **Fix: `gbop_dump_rom_new()` no longer uses the checksum-gated recovery path at all** — reverted to requiring a strict, exact marker match (`m==0`) before proceeding, same as it was after test_10, undoing the test_13 change that added recovery here. `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` is unaffected and keeps the checksum-gated recovery — its risk profile (wrong metadata, self-correcting) still justifies the tradeoff; only the full-dump path's risk profile (silently wrong file, reported as success) does not. A missed recovery now just costs one more of the already-generous 15 retries in `dump_rom_with_retry`; it can no longer produce a confidently-wrong file.
- **The test_16 448-byte-gap mystery got no new data this round** — none of this session's 11 dump-start attempts had a clean marker match to compare against (all were either all-zero-ambiguous or non-zero-recovered), so there's nothing yet to confirm or refute against the new verbose `newproto_stream_body` logging added for that specific question. Still open; needs a session where a dump attempt gets a clean `m==0` marker match with the new logging active.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next dump attempts should now only ever produce a full file when they hit a genuinely clean marker match — meaning fewer of the 15 retries will "succeed" but every one that does should be trustworthy, unlike this session's false accept. If dumps become rare again as a result, that's the expected tradeoff of closing this hole, not a regression — the alternative (an occasional confidently-wrong 16MB file) is worse.

### User hypothesis (2026-07-27): our own requests may be triggering undrained data transfers; boot-time detection checked directly and given a real retry budget

Prompted by the recurring "real-but-misplaced cart data" pattern (test_15-17), the user proposed that our own probing/detection requests might themselves be creating dangling, undrained data transfers whose leftovers show up later — and separately noted that every boot has shown "no cart" until a manual Detect Cart, asking whether that's evidence the ROM header peek is failing to match.

- **The boot-time observation is real, but the data points at a different, more specific cause than the header-peek theory.** `main()`'s initial cart check was a single, non-retried `gbop_read_cart_info()` call — the *only* cart-info call site in the whole codebase with zero retry budget (every other one retries generously against the measured ~5-20% per-attempt success rate). That alone would explain "usually shows not detected at boot." But checking the actual data across six recent logs (test_12 through test_17) found something more specific: **the boot-time attempt came back all-zero 6 times out of 6** — not a mix of all-zero and real-but-misplaced data the way later attempts show. If boot were just an ordinary unlucky draw from the ~47% all-zero baseline (measured across all attempt types pooled, see below), 6/6 all-zero has only ~1% odds — a real signal, not chance. Since it's *specifically* all-zero rather than the recovered-real-data pattern the dangling-stream theory would predict, this looks more like the very first command of a session being systematically worse odds (plausibly related to the one-time "ACK auto-detect" probe that only runs on the first command, giving the device less settle time than any later attempt gets) than leftover data from an abandoned request. Not conclusive, and doesn't rule out the dangling-stream theory contributing elsewhere — but it's a distinct, separately-confirmed issue.
- **Fix: boot-time detection now shares the same 20-attempt retry budget every other detection path already has**, instead of being the sole zero-retry call site in the codebase. `main()` still does a fast up-front `gbop_find()` check (a device genuinely absent from the USB bus doesn't improve with retries, so that stays a quick fail), but once the device is confirmed present, detection itself is delegated to `run_detect_cart_inner()` — the same function a manual "Detect Cart" press already uses — rather than a separate, weaker, single-attempt implementation.
- **The user's dangling-stream hypothesis is still being tested separately, on its own merits.** It doesn't quite match "waiting for acknowledgment" literally (the new-firmware protocol has no host ACKs at all, confirmed via Wireshark — the device just streams continuously once started), but the adjacent version — that our own requests leave data in flight that we never fully drain before moving on — fits the evidence well, especially test_15's finding of two *different* misplaced values on consecutive retries (consistent with catching different slices of an abandoned stream at different times). The most concrete candidate: `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` asks the device for only 512 bytes and, by design, never reads the footer or confirms the device actually stopped there — it just closes the handle. **Added a diagnostic (not yet hardware-verified):** after a successful 512-byte header read, the function now reads up to 16 more packets (no new command sent) and logs whatever arrives, without affecting its return value. If genuine, sequential ROM content keeps flowing past byte 512, that directly confirms the device ignores the declared size and keeps streaming the full ROM regardless — the concrete next fix, if so, would be to always fully drain (or never partially request) rather than adding wait periods, since waiting alone doesn't stop a device that's already committed to sending the rest of a ROM it thinks was requested.
- **Blended baseline used above**: ~47% all-zero, ~40% real-but-misplaced, ~13% clean marker match, counted directly across 386 marker-check attempts in test_12 through test_17 (see prior turn for the full breakdown) — cited here for the 6/6 boot comparison, not re-derived.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next log should show whether boot now succeeds far more often (confirming the retry-budget fix), and whether the header-peek overrun diagnostic shows real ROM content continuing past 512 bytes (confirming or ruling out the dangling-stream mechanism directly).

### Eighteenth hardware test (test_18, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_18/`, 2026-07-27) — boot-retry fix confirmed structurally working; overrun-check inconclusive (hit an IOS error); a *third* distinct gap size (512 bytes) confirms this is not our own code's bug

- **Boot-time retry fix confirmed structurally working**: the log shows the full 20-attempt retry loop running at boot (`cart_info retry 1/19` through `19/19`) instead of the old single-shot check. This particular boot still ended in "No cart at boot" after all 20 failed — a rough session, not a sign the fix is broken (20 attempts against a genuinely bad-luck stretch can still all miss).
- **Overrun-check diagnostic: inconclusive.** Hit `rd=-4` (a hard IOS error, previously documented in this project as associated with stale/invalidated handles) on its very first extra read, stopping immediately. Doesn't confirm or refute whether the device keeps streaming past the requested 512 bytes — just means this one attempt failed for an unrelated reason before it could tell us anything.
- **The ROM dump corruption recurred with the same *shape* as test_16 but yet another different size.** Packet 0 correct, every packet from index 1 onward shifted by a clean, consistent 512 bytes (not test_16's 448) — confirmed via the same reference-byte comparison. Gap sizes seen so far: 448 bytes (test_16), then a false-positive-checksum case (test_17, different mechanism, already fixed), now 512 bytes — always a clean multiple of 64, always close to or exactly one marker block's worth (7 or 8 packets), but not a fixed constant.
- **The single most important finding: the new verbose per-read logging (added after test_16) directly proves this is not a bug in our own code.** The log shows `newproto_stream_body`'s reads immediately adjacent, with no gap in the logged sequence: a ZLP, then a retry landing the genuine correct packet 0, then — logged on the very next line, ~22ms later, matching the established per-read latency — content that already belongs 512 bytes further into the real ROM. Every single read in this window is logged now (this was the whole point of that addition), and there is no missing/hidden read call in our own sequence that could account for the gap. Whatever is happening is on the device's firmware or IOS's USB stack side, before the data ever reaches a `USB_ReadBlkMsg` call — ruling out "our drain/stream loop is silently eating packets" as an explanation, which was the leading code-level suspect after test_16.
- **Fix (test infrastructure, not a behavior change): added `NEWPROTO_DIAGNOSTICS_ENABLED` (`source/gb_operator.c`, currently `0`), a single master toggle gating every diagnostic addition from test_16 through test_18** — `newproto_stream_body`'s and the header peek's per-packet verbose logging, the header peek's 16-extra-read overrun check, and `log_full_packet()`'s per-byte hex dump loop. Requested directly by the user: since each of these adds real `lprintf()` calls (`printf` + `fprintf` + `fflush` to SD) or, in the overrun-check's case, real extra USB reads Epilogue's own captured traffic never shows, they could themselves be changing the timing of exactly the window under investigation — a barebones run with this flag off sends the same commands with none of that added overhead, as close as reasonably possible to the reference Wireshark traffic. Flip back to `1` to restore full diagnostic visibility for a normal test session.
- **Test procedure for the barebones run**: skip USB Probe entirely this session (it sends many extra commands before the real test even starts); go straight from boot (or one manual Detect Cart if boot doesn't catch it) to Dump ROM, minimizing how many commands precede the dump attempt being examined.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** If the same gap still appears with diagnostics fully off, that further confirms the cause is entirely outside this codebase's control (device/IOS/transport-level) and rules out the logging-overhead theory too. If it disappears or changes character, that would be a genuinely surprising and important result pointing back at instrumentation-induced timing after all.

### Nineteenth hardware test (test_19, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_19/`, 2026-07-27) — new "Isolated Test" dev-menu mode: clean connection restart + bounded stray-packet drain + detect + dump, with no background polling in between

test_19 itself was a rough session (boot detection took a long time, background `poll_cart` kept firing continuously in between, and the eventual dump failed outright — 0-byte file). Rather than re-diagnose that specific session, the user asked for something more structural: a genuinely simplified test environment, not just less logging — specifically requesting (1) a way to cleanly restart the GB Operator connection, (2) removing the constant background "cart detect" noise between real test attempts, and (3) waiting until the connection looks quiet before sending the real test command, while still guaranteeing a log gets produced and the session can exit cleanly no matter what.

- **The design is sound, with one real safety constraint.** "Wait until no data is being received" can't be implemented as a literal unbounded loop — this project has repeatedly documented that certain reads on this hardware can block indefinitely if nothing is queued, and there's no safe non-blocking "peek" API available. An unbounded wait would risk exactly the kind of unrecoverable hang the user explicitly wants to avoid (no log, no clean exit). The implemented version is a **bounded best-effort drain** instead: a hard-capped loop (never open-ended), the same safety pattern already used everywhere else in this codebase (the marker-drain loop, `NEWPROTO_MAX_STALL`, every retry budget). It can end early if it sees a few consecutive empty reads in a row (a soft signal the queue looks quiet), but always stops at the cap regardless. Not a guarantee of true silence — a safe approximation of one.
- **Added `gbop_drain_stray(GBOperatorHandle, int max_attempts)`** (`source/gb_operator.c`/`.h`): reads and discards up to `max_attempts` packets from the EP IN endpoint, logging each one's length and first 8 bytes, stopping early after 3 consecutive empty/zero-length reads. Exposed publicly so `main.c` can call it without needing access to `GBOpDevice` internals.
- **Added a new dev-menu item, "Isolated Test"** (`source/main.c`, `DEV_MENU_ITEMS` raised 10→11): closes any open handle, reopens fresh (a genuine connection restart), calls `gbop_drain_stray()` (capped at 30 attempts), closes and lets `run_detect_cart_inner()` open its own fresh handle for detection, and — if a cart is found and its ROM isn't already cached — runs the dump via the existing `dump_rom_with_retry()`. Reuses the same helpers the existing "Detect Cart" and "Dump ROM" items already use (`verify_rom_size_before_dump`, `rom_cache_exists`, `cartindex_update`, etc.) rather than duplicating that logic. Because this is one continuous, synchronous dev-menu action (like every other dev-menu item), `poll_cart()` — which only ever runs between menu screens, driven by the main loop's own poll-interval check — cannot fire *during* this flow at all; the noise the user was describing only happens before this option is selected, which the drain step is specifically meant to clean up regardless of what happened right before.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next test should run this new option directly (skip USB Probe and let boot settle first, same as the barebones-diagnostics test plan) and see whether the drain step visibly finds and discards anything, and whether the same ROM-dump gap still occurs after a deliberately quiet start.

### Twentieth hardware test (test_20, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_20/`, 2026-07-27) — two DIFFERENT corruption mechanisms found in one dump, not one recurring one; one is self-correctable in real time, the other isn't

Used the new Isolated Test option. Drain found 1 stray packet (immediate `rd=-4`, stopped cleanly — no hang). Detect eventually succeeded (`title="POKEMON EMER" code="BPEJ" rom=16384KB`) after several retries. Isolated Test's own dump attempt exhausted its 15-attempt retry budget, but a separate, later "Dump started" (~25s afterward, most likely triggered by background `poll_cart`/auto-install after returning to the menu) succeeded and saved a full 16MB file — with the wrong filename (`_BPEJ.gba`, missing the `POKEMON_EMER` title prefix) and, per the user's report, still not working in mGBA.

Direct byte comparison against the Wireshark reference (same method as test_16/18) found this dump contains **two structurally different corruption events**, not two occurrences of the same one:

- **Leading shift (file offset 0-0x14000ish, shift = +64 bytes):** file offset 0 is the literal ROM-read **header marker** (`C0 DE 00 00 FF 00...`), not real data — the marker itself leaked into the data stream instead of being fully consumed beforehand, delaying every real byte by one packet (64 bytes). This is **self-identifying**: the marker's exact byte signature is what `newproto_is_footer()` already relies on being astronomically unlikely to occur by coincidence in real ROM content, and the same reasoning applies to a header-shaped packet turning up mid-stream.
- **Second shift (~0x2A2000 onward, jumping to a cumulative +576 bytes — an additional 512 bytes / exactly one more marker-block's worth):** checked directly for a marker signature at this transition and found **none** — the region is plain zero bytes on both sides of the boundary (confirmed by hex-dumping the dumped file at the transition). This is a **silent** gap: real bytes went missing (or got displaced) with no signature left behind, indistinguishable from a `newproto_is_header()`/`newproto_is_footer()` check because there's nothing marker-shaped to check against. It also happens to land in an already-zero-padded region of the real ROM, which is why the transition could only be bounded to a coarse window rather than pinned to an exact byte — matching real zero content against reference zero content is inherently ambiguous over a wide span.

This means the working model from test_16/18 ("one shift, always right at the very start") was incomplete — this session shows the shift-producing event(s) can occur **more than once within a single transfer**, and can take **at least two different forms**, only one of which leaves anything detectable behind in the byte stream itself.

- **The bad filename is very likely explained by the same class of event hitting a second, independent read.** The title comes from a *separate* 512-byte ROM-header read (`gbop_read_rom_header`/`enrich_info_from_buf` in `main.c`), which extracts the title/game-code from fixed byte offsets (GBA title at ROM offset 0xA0). That read goes through the identical `newproto_read_marker()`/streaming primitives as the ROM dump. If it independently hit the same kind of misalignment, the title bytes would be read from the wrong offset, `extract_rom_title()` would fail to find printable ASCII there, and `info->title` would stay blank — exactly matching the observed `_BPEJ.gba` (game code present, from the earlier separately-successful cart-info read; title absent).
- **Directly ruled out one hypothesis with code, not guesswork: this is not caused by `dump_rom_with_retry()` reusing stale per-attempt state.** Checked whether a previous failed attempt could leave `GBOpDevice.np_active` set to 1, causing a later attempt to skip its own header-marker read and consume whatever the device happens to be sending as if it were already-validated data. Traced directly: `dump_rom_with_retry()` (`main.c`) always `gbop_close()`s before `gbop_reopen()`ing on every attempt, and `gbop_reopen()` (`gb_operator.c`) always `calloc()`s a brand-new, zeroed `GBOpDevice` — there is no path for marker/stream state to leak across attempts. This rules out our own retry bookkeeping as the cause and reinforces that the leading-shift event really does originate on the device/transport side.

**Fixes implemented (both untested on hardware as of this writing):**

1. **Real-time self-correction for the leading-shift (marker-leak) case.** Added `newproto_is_header()` (`source/gb_operator.c`) — the mirror of `newproto_is_footer()`, checking for a full, exact `C0 DE 00 <cmd> <~cmd>` + zero-padding match. `newproto_stream_body()` now checks every incoming packet against it (not just the footer shape) and **drops** a matching packet without writing it into the caller's buffer or counting it toward the byte total, instead of silently accepting it as data. This only fixes the self-identifying leading-shift shape; it cannot help the silent, signature-less second shift (bucket 2 below), since there's nothing to detect there.
2. **Stop accepting "byte count reached, footer eventually seen" as proof of a good dump.** Traced the actual completion logic in `gbop_dump_rom_new()`/`gbop_read_save_new()` and found neither ever previously attempted a read *past* the caller's requested byte count specifically to confirm the real footer shows up where expected — a per-chunk ROM dump, once `np_given` reaches `np_total`, simply stops being called again by `rom_cache.c`'s outer loop (`offset < total` becomes false) without ever draining/checking for the footer at all, healthy or not. Added `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()`: once the expected total is reached, it reads a bounded number of extra packets specifically looking for the footer; if the footer isn't the *very next* packet, or never shows up within the bound, the dump is rejected (`gbop_dump_rom`/`gbop_read_save` return -1, which `dump_rom_with_retry()` already treats as "attempt failed, retry"). This is a **general** detector — it works for any gap size or shape (marker-leak or silent), because in every observed case real bytes end up effectively missing, so the device's own true byte-count-driven footer necessarily arrives later than our nominal completion point; it can't recover or locate the lost/displaced bytes, but it stops a shifted file from being silently reported as a success, which is what let three separate corrupted dumps through so far. Deliberately scoped to only engage when the expected total is a *resolved, exact* size (a known game's `rom_sizes.c` entry, or a cart-reported save size) — it never fires for the intentionally-oversized unrecognized-game ROM fallback, where the footer is already caught inline well before the (deliberately inflated) total is ever reached, so there's no risk of it rejecting that legitimate case.
3. **A genuinely more controlled test path: dump without a cart-info probe at all.** Directly answering "is it possible to do a cart dump without a cart detect?" — added a new dev-menu item, **"Dump Only (no detect, reuse cart info)"** (`DEV_MENU_ITEMS` 11→12, `source/main.c`). It requires a cart already detected earlier in the same session (reuses the persistent `info` struct — refuses to run otherwise, since it has no way to learn the ROM size on its own) and then sends **only** the ROM-read command: reopen, a small bounded drain (10, smaller than Isolated Test's 30 — deliberately minimal), then straight into `dump_rom_with_retry()`. No cart-info command, no ROM-header peek, no GBA size mirror-check — as close to the raw Wireshark capture's single-command traffic as this app can produce, to test the user's standing hypothesis that our own request volume (not just the transport itself) may be contributing to what we're seeing.

**On the LED behavior (green = ROM dump, blue = save dump, brief pulses during cart detect):** there's no documented, USB-queryable LED status in this protocol — the device's indicator can't be read from software, only observed. It's still a useful independent, out-of-band signal precisely because it's independent: if the device's own "pulse briefly, abort, retry, then pulse solid until done" pattern persists even during the new minimal-traffic "Dump Only" path, that's direct physical evidence the behavior originates in the device/connection rather than in how many commands our own code happens to send first — worth watching specifically during the next test of that option.

### New dev-menu tool: "Command Test Lab" (2026-07-28) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

Requested directly: a controlled environment to exercise individual protocol commands one at a time (not just the higher-level Isolated Test / Dump Only flows), with a persistent status readout and the ability to run everything in sequence, specifically to test whether request *volume* itself (not just the transport) is a factor, and to capture the newly-discovered `0x15` "Get Info" command (see kantosCoder/FreeGbOperatorFirm10Py in Sources) for the first time on this project's own hardware.

- **`gbop_raw_command_capture(handle, cmd, buf_out, buf_size)`** — new exported function, `source/gb_operator.c`/`.h`. Sends a bare command byte (rest of payload zero, CRC appended as usual via the existing `gbop_make_pkt`) and captures raw 64-byte packets into `buf_out` with **zero interpretation** — no marker/header/footer parsing, no drain logic, bounded only by a stall cap (`GBOP_RAW_CAPTURE_MAX_STALL`=32) so it can never hang. Exists specifically for commands with no higher-level implementation yet, starting with `0x15`. Does not log per-byte content inline (that would be one `lprintf` — a printf+fprintf+fflush — per byte, far too slow/spammy for a 2048-byte capture); callers write the buffer to an SD file instead, matching how ROM dumps are already handled.
- **New dev-menu item, "Command Test Lab"** (`DEV_MENU_ITEMS` 12→13, `source/main.c`). A persistent sub-screen (not a one-shot action) showing a status line per command (Drain / Cart Info / Get Info / ROM Peek / Save Read), each starting "Not run yet" and updating in place as each test is run, with a menu below to run any one individually or all of them back-to-back ("Run All"):
  - **Restart + Drain** — close/reopen + `gbop_drain_stray(op, 30)`, same bounded drain Isolated Test already uses.
  - **Test Cart Info (0x04)** — existing `gbop_read_cart_info()`, unchanged.
  - **Test Get Info (0x15)** — sends a full `gbop_read_cart_info()` first (priming — matches the only known-working sequence for `0x15`, kantosCoder's `id_check`: `0x04` then `0x15` after a short delay), then `gbop_raw_command_capture(op, 0x15, buf, 2048)`. 2048 bytes gives comfortable margin around the 512-byte-header-then-512-byte-data shape kantosCoder's tool found (real identifying checksum at their offset 512-514) — enough to see the header, the real data block, and whatever follows. Saved to `<app_root>/testlab/cmd15_getinfo.bin`; log gets a summary line (bytes captured, first 8 bytes, bytes 512-519 if reached) rather than a full hex dump.
  - **Test ROM Peek (0x00, 512B)** — existing `gbop_read_rom_header()` (512-byte header-only peek, not a full dump — deliberately lightweight for this controlled environment). Saved to `cmd00_rompeek.bin`.
  - **Test Save Read (0x02)** — existing `gbop_read_save()`, requires `info.ram_size_kb` already known from an earlier Detect Cart this session (same reuse-known-info pattern as "Dump Only"); skips with a message otherwise. Saved to `cmd02_saveread.bin`.
  - **Run All** — all four in the order above, back-to-back, updating every status line in one pass.
  - No write commands anywhere in this tool, per explicit instruction — read-only commands only.
- All four SD files land in `<app_root>/testlab/` (created via `mkdir` if missing), overwritten on each run — meant to be pulled off the SD card and inspected the same way ROM dumps already are (e.g. with `align_rom_dump.py`'s methodology, adapted to whatever reference is relevant for a given capture).

### First hardware test of Command Test Lab (test_21, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_21/`, 2026-07-28) — no real hang; a UI bug plus a genuinely useful backlog finding

User ran the new tool multiple times and reported: debug print remains on screen after a test, scrolling the menu scrolls the whole screen instead of just moving the cursor, "Test Cart Info" appeared to hang, and the cart showed as not inserted (with the reasonable observation that cart presence shouldn't matter for running these tests).

- **Not a real hang.** Traced `log1.txt` line-by-line: "Test Cart Info" ran exactly once — reopen, `cmd=0x04 tx=64`, a normal data+footer-drain sequence, `Cart not detected (resp[3:5] == 0)` — and returned control to the "Press A to continue" prompt, all within about half a second. No retry loop, no blocked read. The perceived hang is fully explained by the next finding.
- **Root cause of "debug print remains on screen" / "scrolls the whole screen": every `gbop_*` call logs via `lprintf()`, which prints to the console as well as the log file, and nothing in the Command Test Lab code suppressed that.** A single reopen+read is 10-15 console lines on its own; "Run All" or even one test back-to-back with the drain step comfortably exceeds the console's ~27 visible rows (`console_init(xfb, 20, 20, ...)` — no explicit row count, but this is the working figure other screens stay under). With nothing suppressing it, this debug spew — not the actual operation — is what filled and scrolled the screen, made the "Press A to continue" prompt hard to find, and gave the appearance of a frozen/broken UI. **Fix:** wrap each raw USB call (drain, cart-info, get-info priming, ROM peek, save read) with `g_log_suppress_console = 1` / `= 0` (the same flag `mgba_frontend.c` already uses around GX emulation for the same reason — console and framebuffer sharing). Full detail still reaches the log file; only the screen is quieted. Also trimmed the screen's static description text to a single line to reduce baseline row count independent of the spew fix.
- **"Cart insert check shouldn't matter" — confirmed, there is no such gate.** Every Command Test Lab option is independent; only "Test Save Read" requires `info.ram_size_kb` already known (from an earlier Detect Cart this session) and explicitly says so rather than silently failing. Cart presence never blocks any other test from running. This was very likely misread from the same debug-spew/hang illusion above, not a real gating bug.
- **A genuinely useful finding, straight from the saved `cmd15_getinfo.bin` capture:** the 2048-byte "Get Info" (`0x15`) capture was NOT dominated by real `0x15` content at all. Direct inspection: bytes 0-63 are a real, non-zero **cart-info** data packet (`0A 00 30 01 01 18 11 31...`, byte-for-byte matching this cart's known cart-info response) — leftover from an *earlier, unrelated* `0x04` exchange, not the priming call in this same test (that one returned NOCART/all-zero, confirmed in the log). Bytes 64-511 are zero. **Byte 512 is that leftover exchange's own footer** (`C0 DE 01 04 FB`). Bytes 513-1023 are zero. **The `0x15` command's own header marker (`C0 DE 00 15 EA`) doesn't appear until byte 1024** — a full 1024 bytes into the capture. This reproduced identically (byte-for-byte) across two separate app boots (`log1.txt` and `log2.txt`), so it isn't a one-off fluke.
  - This means IOS's EP IN backlog can hold onto **far more** than the "a few residual packets" this project's various drain routines have assumed so far — here, a complete stale exchange (data + footer, mostly zero-padded, 1024 bytes total) was still sitting queued well after an unrelated, successful `gbop_read_cart_info()` call had already run and returned. Existing marker-based reads only ever drain exactly what *they* expect to see; they don't know or care whether more unrelated backlog sits queued behind that, and evidently a lot can.
  - **Fixes:** Get Info's capture buffer raised 2048→4096 bytes (the old 2048 barely got past the real header at 1024, capturing almost none of the actual `0x15` data behind it); added a small bounded pre-drain (`gbop_drain_stray(op, 10)`) before Get Info's priming `0x04` call, to clear cheap/common backlog before this specific sequence; log line changed to report bytes 1024-1031 (where the real header now reliably lands) instead of the old, now-known-to-be-wrong 512-519 assumption borrowed from kantosCoder's script (which reads over a serial/COM-port abstraction and may simply not see the same backlog shape this project's raw-bulk-transfer approach does).
- **Not yet re-verified on hardware** — next Command Test Lab run should show a clean, static, non-scrolling status screen, and a `cmd15_getinfo.bin` capture that reaches its own header well before running out of buffer, with actual `0x15` data visible past it for the first time.

### Second hardware test of Command Test Lab (test_22, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_22/`, 2026-07-28) — genuine freezes, two established-but-unfollowed patterns identified as the likely cause

User reported "many of the options seemed like they froze the program" — specifically "Restart + Drain" sitting on screen doing nothing for minutes — across four separate boot sessions, with zero files ever written to the testlab folder.

- **This is not the same bug as test_21.** Traced all four session logs (`log.txt`, `log1.txt`, `log2.txt`, `log3.txt`): two sessions (`log.txt`, `log2.txt`) show **zero log lines of any kind** after `[dev] Command Test Lab` — not even a single `gbop_reopen()` attempt, even though `lprintf()` writes to the log file regardless of the console-suppress flag added in test_21's fix. A third (`log3.txt`) got exactly one clean, fast "Test Cart Info" cycle (reopen → `cmd=0x04` → recognized a stale residual footer → correctly returned `GBOP_USB` — a real, un-buggy outcome) and then nothing further. None of the four ever logged `"drain: starting"` — the dedicated `gbop_drain_stray()` call inside "Restart + Drain" — even though the user specifically named that option as freezing. This means the freeze is happening very early: either in this project's own navigation loop before an action is even selected, or in the first few lines of an action, before its own `gbop_reopen()`/`gbop_drain_stray()` calls — not deep inside a long operation.
- **Two real deviations from established, hardware-validated patterns elsewhere in this codebase, both now fixed, since both are plausible causes and both are free to fix regardless of which (or both) actually caused this:**
  1. **No delay between closing and reopening the USB handle.** Every other close-then-reopen retry loop in this project (`dump_rom_with_retry`, `read_rom_header_with_retry`, `run_detect_cart_inner`) waits ~300ms between `gbop_close()` and the next `gbop_reopen()`. The Command Test Lab was the only place in the codebase that did it back-to-back with zero delay — and uniquely, "Run All" chains five such close/reopen cycles in a row, more rapid repeated reopening than any single existing flow ever does. **Fix:** added `usleep(300000)` before every `gbop_reopen()` call in this screen (5 call sites), matching the rest of the codebase.
