  2. **`log_commit_sd()` (fclose+fopen) was called up to twice per action**, and this is the only place in the app where a user can run action after action after action without the loop ever returning to the outer dev menu — "Run All" plus a few manual re-runs can rack up far more fclose/fopen cycles in one screen visit than this project's own documented heap-corruption hazard (tests 76-80, "never call `log_commit_sd()` inside `mgba_run()` except at 'Session ended'") has ever been exercised with elsewhere. **Fix:** per-action commits changed to `log_force_flush()` (fflush only, no fclose/fopen — still commits to the log file's stdio buffer, doesn't risk the FILE-struct churn); a single real `log_commit_sd()` now runs once, only when actually leaving the screen (on Back/B).
- **Not yet re-verified on hardware.** If the freeze persists after both fixes, that would point away from these two theories and toward something else — worth trying "Test Cart Info" alone first (cheapest, no chained reopens) before "Run All" on the next attempt, to narrow down whether a single action can still freeze on its own.

### Third hardware test of Command Test Lab (test_23, `logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_23/`, 2026-07-28) — no in-action hang found; the real problem was a 0-byte log from a session that never reached its one commit point, plus an unnecessarily strict Save Read

Two sessions this time: `log.txt` is **0 bytes** despite the user reporting drain/cart-info hangs; `log1.txt` (394 lines) shows a later session where the Test Lab was used successfully.

- **Every individual action traced in `log1.txt` completed in well under a second, correctly.** ROM Peek: reopen → `cmd=0x00` → all-zero header → cleanly recognized as a failed read (65291.420ms to 65291.464ms — 44 microseconds of actual processing). Cart Info: reopen → `cmd=0x04` → data + 4-packet footer-drain → correctly reported "Cart not detected" (75867.424ms to 75872.421ms — under 5ms). The multi-second-to-30-second gaps *between* these entries in the log are consistent with normal human navigation time (reading the result, pressing A, moving the cursor, picking the next option) — not the code stuck in a loop. Neither of the two actions the user specifically named ("drain and cart info") shows any evidence of hanging in this log.
- **The 0-byte `log.txt` is the actual finding.** This project's own `log_commit_sd()` documents that libfat may not write dirty sectors to the *physical* SD card until a real `fclose()` — `fflush()` alone (which `log_force_flush()` uses, and which `lprintf()` already calls on every single line regardless) only pushes data into libfat's own buffering, not necessarily to the card. test_22's fix limited real `log_commit_sd()` calls to once, only when leaving the Test Lab screen, specifically to avoid the fclose/fopen heap-cycling hazard from tests 76-80. The unintended consequence: if a session hangs or crashes *before* ever reaching that one exit-time commit — which the user's account says happened, even though this specific log can't show it — **nothing from that entire session ever reaches the physical SD card**, leaving a 0-byte file and zero forensic evidence right when it's needed most.
- **Fix: real `log_commit_sd()` restored before every action** (not `log_force_flush()`), placed *before* each risky operation begins rather than after — so whatever led up to a hang is guaranteed to already be physically on the SD card even if the operation itself never returns. tests 76-80's corruption was measured against `mgba_run()`'s heavy per-session churn (16MB ROM buffers, ~30+ cycles); a Test Lab visit is far lighter and involves far fewer cycles per visit, so the tradeoff favors forensic durability here — diagnosing the actual hang matters more right now than a bounded, likely-smaller-than-mgba_run() heap risk.
- **Save Read no longer requires a prior successful Detect Cart.** User asked directly: cart detection is often what's flaky in the first place, so requiring it as a prerequisite for testing the save-read command specifically defeats some of the point of an isolated command tester. When `info.ram_size_kb == 0`, the test now defaults to a GBA/128KB guess (matching the cart this entire investigation has used) instead of refusing to run, with the result string flagged `[default 128KB GBA]` / `(default size)` so a size-guessed result is never confused with a confirmed one. This is a diagnostic capture tool, not a save manager — inspecting whatever the device sends back for an untested size guess is exactly the point.
- **Not yet re-verified on hardware.** If a hang still reproduces, the per-action `log_commit_sd()` should now at least show exactly which action and which log line preceded it — try the single simplest action ("Test Cart Info" alone) first before "Run All", to isolate whether one action can hang in isolation.

### Command Test Lab: retry budget added, and a step back from the side-quest (2026-07-28) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

test_23's follow-up: the user confirmed the hang reproduced even running a single action alone (not just "Run All"), which changes the diagnosis. This is very unlikely to be a Test-Lab-specific bug — every other part of this app survives the exact same `gbop_reopen()`/`gbop_read_cart_info()`/etc. calls specifically *because* it wraps them in a 15-75-attempt retry budget (`run_detect_cart_inner`, `dump_rom_with_retry`, `read_rom_header_with_retry`). The Command Test Lab was the only place calling these functions completely unwrapped — one single attempt, fully exposed to this connection's own measured ~5-20% per-attempt success rate (USB Probe Test 5/6). A single unwrapped attempt was never a fair or representative test of these commands; most of the time it was simply going to get nothing, which reads as "hung" with no visible progress.

- **Fix:** every Command Test Lab action (Drain, Cart Info, Get Info, ROM Peek, Save Read) now retries up to `TESTLAB_ATTEMPTS` (15, matching `GBOP_HDR_READ_ATTEMPTS` elsewhere) with a 300ms gap before each reopen, stopping early on a definitive result (`GBOP_OK` for cart-info, `rc==0` for ROM peek/save read, `got>0` for Get Info). Save Read's buffer is now allocated once outside the retry loop instead of per-attempt.
- **Important, honest limitation stated directly in the code comment:** this only helps if a failing attempt actually *returns* (with an error) so the loop gets a chance to try again. If IOS genuinely blocks forever inside a single `USB_ReadBlkMsg` call — which this project has never found a way to interrupt from another thread or context (the X+Y abort limitation documented elsewhere applies here too) — no retry wrapper at any level can recover from that specific stuck call. This fix targets the "returns quickly but often with a failure" case, which is what the rest of this app's own retry budgets have always assumed and generally survived on.
- **Bigger-picture note, prompted directly by the user asking whether this was still a good use of test sessions:** three sessions (test_21, 22, 23) were spent on Command Test Lab bugs without re-touching the actual ROM-dump corruption fixes from test_20 (`newproto_is_header()` mid-stream drop, `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()` rejection) — which are the fixes that actually matter and have not been hardware-verified since they were written. The Command Test Lab is still worth having (it already produced the `0x15` discovery and the Get Info backlog finding) but is a side diagnostic, not the main line of work. See the testing sequence below for how the next session should be prioritized.

**Suggested next hardware test sequence** (priority order — stop and report back after any step that finds something unexpected rather than pushing through the rest):
1. **Isolated Test → Dump ROM**, cart inserted, fresh boot. This is the actual, still-unverified thing: does `newproto_is_header()`'s mid-stream drop plus `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()`'s rejection produce a clean dump now, or at least fail loudly instead of silently producing a shifted file? Compare the result against the Wireshark reference the same way test_16/18/20 did (or run the new `align_rom_dump.py` scratch script against it directly).
2. **If the dump is clean:** try loading it in mGBA to confirm it's actually playable — the thing this whole investigation has been chasing.
3. **If the dump still corrupts:** save the file and log; that's the priority finding for the next round, over anything else below.
4. **Only after 1-3**, if there's time/patience left: Command Test Lab, single actions first (Cart Info alone, then Get Info alone) to confirm the new retry budget actually restores basic usability before trying "Run All" again.

### test_24 (`logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_24/`, 2026-07-28) — the footer-confirm fix caught a real corruption for the first time; a hard crash traced to test_23's own fix; connection reliability itself was very poor this session

Two separate findings, from the two things the user ran: **Isolated Test → Dump ROM** (the priority #1 item from the previous testing sequence) and **Command Test Lab → Test Cart Info**.

- **The ROM dump story is actually good news buried in a bad outcome.** Traced all 16 dump attempts (15 from `dump_rom_with_retry` + 1 fresh-fd fallback) in the log. 14 of them failed immediately at the very first header-marker read (`header marker mismatch/error m=1/m=2`) — this is the same long-documented low per-attempt connection reliability (USB Probe Test 5/6's ~5-20% baseline) applying to the *opening* handshake specifically, nothing new. **But attempt #2 streamed all the way to the full 16384/16384 KB estimate and was then correctly REJECTED**: `"[newproto] stream(cmd=00) footer arrived 64 bytes LATE (1 extra packet(s)) — stream was misaligned, rejecting dump"`. This is `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()` (added test_20) working exactly as designed — this is the exact same 64-byte/1-packet shift class of corruption found in test_20's own dump, caught and rejected this time instead of silently written to disk as a "successful" file. The fix's specific, narrow goal — never again silently accept a shifted dump — is confirmed working on real hardware for the first time. The broader goal (an actual clean dump) still isn't met, because this session's connection was bad enough that nothing got both a clean start *and* a clean finish in the same attempt. The 0-byte `POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba` on disk is a side effect of `rom_cache_dump()` truncating the output file (`"wb"` mode) on every retry — the last (16th) attempt failed before writing anything, so that's what was left, not evidence of a corrupt-but-nonempty file.
- **The crash: traced to test_23's own fix.** "Test Cart Info" was run in a session that had already gone through a long detect-cart cycle (19 retries) and all 16 ROM dump attempts before ever reaching Command Test Lab — a long session with a lot of prior activity. test_23 had just changed every Test Lab action to call a *real* `log_commit_sd()` (fclose+fopen) before running, specifically to fix a 0-byte log from a hung session. That fix traded one real risk for a different, worse one: this project has hard, reproduced evidence (tests 76-90) that repeated fclose/fopen cycling corrupts dlmalloc's free list, and a later `malloc`/`free` following the poisoned pointer chain produces exactly this DSI/red-border crash shape. A long session already carries whatever fclose/fopen count it accumulated before Test Lab even opens; adding a real commit before *every single action* on top of that is a plausible way to cross whatever threshold tests 76-90 measured at ~30 cycles.
  - **Fix:** capped real `log_commit_sd()` calls to exactly two per Test Lab visit — one on entry, one on exit — regardless of how many actions run in between. Every per-action checkpoint (previously a real commit) is back to `log_force_flush()` (fflush only, no fclose/fopen; `lprintf()` already fflushes every line regardless). This also corrects a mistake in the test_23 changelog entry: the "commit once, on exit" commit was actually still *inside* the action loop (firing after every single action, not just on final exit) — genuinely fixed now, moved to after the loop.
  - **Priority ordering, stated directly this time:** a crash is strictly worse than a hang (recoverable by power-cycling) or a 0-byte log (still recoverable, just uninformative) — this always wins the tradeoff against better hang forensics, which was the reasoning test_23 got backwards.
- **Not yet re-verified on hardware.** Next test: confirm "Test Cart Info" no longer crashes after a long session; separately, the ROM dump problem is now squarely "the connection needs to stay clean through both the opening handshake AND the entire stream in the same attempt" — worth another Isolated Test → Dump ROM run specifically to see whether the 15-attempt budget can eventually land one attempt that's clean start-to-finish, now that a bad finish is guaranteed to be caught rather than silently accepted.

### User hypothesis tested directly against real ROM files (2026-07-28): "is the shift/gap defect actually a deliberate, layout-dependent reassembly scheme (effectively encryption)?" — NO, disproven with direct evidence; but a major, unexpected finding came out of testing it

User added the actual, independently-sourced ROM files (`Pocket Monsters - Emerald (Japan).gba`, `Pokemon - Silver Version (USA, Europe) (SGB Enhanced).gb`, plus their `.sav` files) directly into `logs/Firmware-V10_0_10-WireShark/`, and asked whether the recurring shift/gap corruption could be explained by the firmware reorganizing which ROM chunks get read in what order for speed, requiring the receiver to know the ROM's layout to reassemble it — i.e., the protocol would effectively be "encrypted" without that layout knowledge.

**Method:** extracted the raw IN-payload byte stream from `rom_dump_GBA.pcapng` and `rom_dump_GBC.pcapng` via `tshark` (same method as all prior Wireshark analysis), stripped the leading 512-byte header marker, and compared the result **directly against the independently-sourced real ROM files** — not against each other, which is what every previous comparison in this project (test_16 through test_24) had done. This is the first time an outside, independently-obtained copy of the actual game data was available to check against, rather than relying on the capture as its own ground truth.

**Result — hypothesis disproven:**
- The GBA capture (Pokemon Emerald) matches the real ROM byte-for-byte, in strict sequential address order, for the **entire 16MB file except one single, isolated 512-byte gap** at file offset ~0x2A5000 (~2.76MB in). Before that point: perfect match. After that point: the stream permanently reads exactly 512 bytes ahead of where it should relative to the real ROM (`stream[X] == real_rom[X+512]` for the rest of the file) — i.e., 512 bytes of real ROM content never arrived, and everything after slides forward to fill the gap. This is the **identical shape** (a clean, marker-block-sized, signature-less shift) as multiple corruptions already found in this project's own Wii-side dumps (test_16's 448-byte gap, test_18's 512-byte gap, test_20's two-stage shift) — except this one is in `rom_dump_GBA.pcapng` itself, the capture of the **official Epilogue Playback software's own transfer**, not anything Wii-side.
- The GBC capture (Pokemon Silver) matches the real ROM **perfectly, with zero shifts anywhere**, for the entire 2MB file.
- No reordering, permutation, or rearrangement was found anywhere in either file — every byte that matches, matches at its correct real-ROM address (accounting for the one shift). A deliberate layout-dependent reassembly scheme would need to show chunks appearing out of address order, or *some* structure the receiver has to know to undo; instead every discrepancy found (in this project's entire history of looking at this, now including the official reference) has the same "N contiguous bytes are simply absent, rest slides over" shape, which is the signature of intermittent data loss, not reorganization.

**What this actually means:**
1. **The protocol itself is not "encrypted" or layout-dependent** — it's a plain sequential byte stream in real ROM address order, full stop. Confirmed now against independently-sourced ROM data, not just self-referential comparison.
2. **The shift/gap defect is real, and it is not Wii-specific, not IOS-specific, and not a bug in this project's own code** — the exact same class of defect appears in the manufacturer's own official software's own capture. This is the strongest evidence yet that the root cause sits below the application layer entirely (the device's firmware/hardware, or the USB transport under load), independent of which host is talking to it.
3. **Open question, not yet answered:** why does this happen on nearly every Wii-side attempt, but only once in ~18MB of combined official reference capture? One data point can't establish Epilogue's own true rate, but the gap between "constant on Wii" and "rare here" is large enough to still be worth understanding — the leading theory remains this project's existing timing work (Wii-side USB round trips measured consistently slower than Epilogue's ~600-700us baseline), i.e. a timing-sensitivity difference rather than a protocol misunderstanding.
4. **No code change follows from this directly** — the existing strategy (detect and reject a misaligned dump via `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()`, confirmed working on hardware in test_24; retry until a clean attempt lands) is already the correct response to "occasional, unpredictable data loss with no structure to decode." There is no reassembly key to find, because there is no reassembly scheme.

### Two new Wireshark captures (2026-07-28): the 512-byte gap is deterministic and content-triggered, not random packet loss; cart-swap capture reveals adaptive polling and a small-scale version of the same shift

User captured `rom_dump_GBA_4x.pcapng` (the same Emerald ROM dumped 4 times back-to-back, directly answering the earlier "get a real baseline gap rate" ask) and `cart_swap_emerald2ruby_repeated.pcapng` (repeated Emerald↔Ruby cart swaps).

**The 4x repeat is the single most important data point this investigation has produced.** All four dumps were extracted and diffed against the real ROM independently. Every one of the four shows **the exact same 512-byte gap, at the exact same byte boundary** (binary-searched to the exact transition point: between stream offset `0x2A27B0` and `0x2A2800` — identical to the byte in all four dumps, not just "the same neighborhood"). This rules out random transport packet loss as the mechanism: a true random-loss event would not land on the identical byte four separate times. This is a **deterministic, content-triggered** event — something about this specific ROM's data at this specific address (real-ROM offset ≈0x2A5000, ~2.76MB in) consistently causes the device/firmware to drop exactly 512 bytes, every single time this ROM is read, regardless of host. The real ROM content at that address is repetitive/structured (tile or graphics-adjacent data, including a run of `FF FF FF FF` immediately before the drop) rather than program code — consistent with a content-sensitive hardware/firmware quirk (e.g. something in the read path mishandling a specific byte pattern) rather than a timing-sensitive one.
- **This likely explains why this project's own Wii-side dumps (test_16, 18, 20) kept showing a shift in the same ~2.7-2.8MB neighborhood** — not coincidence, but the same deterministic, address-tied event firing regardless of which host reads the cart.
- **Important strategic implication, not yet acted on:** the existing strategy (retry until an attempt streams cleanly) assumes failures are independent random events that a fresh attempt might avoid. If this specific gap is deterministic per-ROM-content, **no amount of retrying will ever produce a clean dump of this cart** — every attempt that gets far enough to reach ~2.76MB will hit the identical drop. This reframes the open question from "how do we get a lucky clean run" to "do we need a fundamentally different recovery" (e.g. a targeted re-read of just the known-bad window, if the protocol ever supports an offset parameter — not currently established) for carts that trigger this. Flagging this for discussion, not implementing anything yet — the right response depends on whether this generalizes to other games/ROM regions or is specific to whatever byte pattern sits at this one address in this one ROM.

**Cart-swap capture — two useful behavioral characterizations, plus a correction to earlier documentation:**
- **Epilogue's poll rate is adaptive**: ~500ms between cart-info polls while a cart is stably identified, dropping to the previously-documented ~108ms cadence once the cart reads back as not-present (searching more eagerly). Both rates were visible in the same capture for the first time, confirming they're two modes of the same client, not two different capture sessions behaving differently.
- **A distinct, repeatable "transient" response appears during the physical instant of a cart swap**: `30 00 00...` (all zero except a single leading `0x30` byte), clearly different from both the clean cart-present response and the clean NOCART response (which both retain the fixed `...0A 00 0A 02 00 00 01 00` tail — see below). This looks like the same class of small-scale misalignment as the 512-byte ROM gap, just at cart-info scale: `0x30` is the real GBA type marker normally sitting at `resp[2]`, appearing here at `resp[0]` — i.e., real response content landing 2 bytes early, the identical *shape* of defect as the large-scale ROM shifts, just triggered by the physical contact bounce of a cart swap rather than ROM content. Further evidence this class of misalignment is a general, low-level transport characteristic of this device, not something confined to long streaming reads.
- **Correction to prior documentation:** the "New Firmware Protocol" section previously described `resp[5..7]` (`18 11 31` for the Emerald capture) as possibly "a fixed non-zero value... recurred byte-for-byte across every external tester's log," floated as a firmware/protocol-revision identifier. This capture disproves that: Ruby's cart-info response shows **different** bytes at the same position (`resp[3..7] = 00 00 19 00 00` vs Emerald's `01 01 18 11 31`), confirmed directly against a real, identified Ruby response (title bytes decode to "PAXV0", matching Ruby's real game code AXV_). This field **does vary by cart** and was never a universal constant — likely per-cart identifying data after all, just not the ROM/RAM size field. What **is** confirmed as a genuine fixed constant, independent of both cart identity and cart presence, is `resp[26..33]` (`0A 00 0A 02 00 00 01 00`) — identical across Emerald, Ruby, and the pure NOCART response alike, meaning it's a fixed part of the response frame itself (protocol/firmware version trailer, most likely), never cart data.

### Third capture (2026-07-28): `rom_dump_GBA_emerald_ruby_firered.pcapng` — the "pause to let Playback build the ROM" hypothesis directly disproven; gap position is per-ROM, not universal, and not guaranteed every time

User captured Emerald, Ruby, and FireRed dumped in sequence in one file, added real `Pokemon - Ruby Version (USA).gba` and `Pocket Monsters - Fire Red (Japan).gba` to diff against, and proposed a specific mechanism for the gap: that it's a deliberate protocol pause giving Epilogue Playback time to build/process the ROM file while the transfer is paused (i.e. bytes are delayed, not lost).

**Diffed all three games against their real ROMs independently:**
- **Emerald: one gap, at the identical byte offset found in all four `rom_dump_GBA_4x.pcapng` repeats** (real-ROM offset ≈0x2A5000) — a fifth consecutive confirmation this exact address is a hard, reproducible trigger for this specific ROM.
- **Ruby: zero gaps.** Full 16MB, byte-perfect match, no shift anywhere. Confirms the phenomenon is not guaranteed on every dump of every game.
- **FireRed: two gaps**, close together (~16KB apart, at real-ROM offsets ≈0x1D3000 and ≈0x1D7000) — a new pattern (multiple drops in one dump) not seen before, at a completely different address than Emerald's.

This on its own already tells us the gap position is **tied to something in each specific ROM's own data, not a fixed byte-offset/timing checkpoint universal across any GBA transfer** — if it were a fixed elapsed-time or fixed-byte-count checkpoint in the protocol, every game (all three are the same 16MB size) would show it at the same position. They don't.

**Direct test of the "pause for processing" hypothesis: disproven.** Pulled the exact per-packet frame timing (not just byte content) at all three gap locations from the raw capture. At every single one — Emerald's gap, and both of FireRed's — the inter-packet delta is a perfectly uniform ~0.55-0.63ms, identical to the steady-state rate everywhere else in the transfer, with **no pause, stall, or anomalous delay of any kind** at the exact frame where the content jumps. A deliberate "hold the transfer open while the software catches up" mechanism would necessarily show up as a large time gap between packets right at that point (however Playback implemented it, host-side buffering still requires the wire to go quiet while it works) — instead, data keeps arriving at full speed throughout, just silently different from that point on. This rules out a deliberate pause/backpressure mechanism as the explanation; whatever causes the drop does so without slowing or halting the byte stream at all, more consistent with the device's own internal read-address counter silently skipping forward than with any kind of intentional handshake or processing delay.

**Net picture after three independent capture sessions (4x-repeat, this one, plus the original single dumps):** the defect is real, reproducible for a given ROM (Emerald: 5/5 so far, always the same byte), sometimes entirely absent (Ruby: 0/1), sometimes multiple in one transfer (FireRed: 2), always silent (no marker, no footer, no error code), and always at full transfer speed with no timing signature to detect it by other than the content itself. Nothing here yet points at *why* a given ROM's data at a given address triggers it — that remains open.

### Streaming throughput fix (2026-07-28) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

User's concern: a GBA ROM dump takes 5+ minutes, and increasing the retry budget (the standing recommendation from the previous entry) would only make that worse. Direct math on test_24's own progress log settled where the time actually goes: ~9.3 seconds per 512KB once a dump is genuinely streaming (~290s for the full 16MB) — with **zero retries involved in that figure**. The same 512KB in the Wireshark reference capture (Epilogue on Windows) takes well under a second. That ~16x gap is the real cost, not the handshake retries this whole investigation has been focused on.

**Likely cause:** `newproto_stream_body()` (`source/gb_operator.c`) read exactly one 64-byte packet per `USB_ReadBlkMsg()` call — a full 16MB dump is 262,144 individual IOS round trips. This project's own earlier timing work measured Wii-side `USB_ReadBlkMsg` latency as bimodal ~1ms/~11ms per call, far above Epilogue's measured ~600-700us on Windows — and the Windows captures themselves show 512-byte "transfers" per USB event, not 64-byte ones, meaning its stack already coalesces multiple packets per call while this project's implementation never did.

**Fix:** `newproto_stream_body()` now requests up to `NEWPROTO_READ_CHUNK` (4096) bytes per `USB_ReadBlkMsg()` call instead of a fixed 64, using a new aligned static buffer (`s_stream_rx`). Since a batched read can now contain several packets at once, marker detection (header-leak drop, footer confirm) is checked at every 64-byte-aligned position within whatever came back, not just once against the whole read — a short tail smaller than one packet can't contain a marker and is copied through as plain data, same as before. The request size is always capped to the caller's remaining buffer space, so `rd` can never exceed what's left in `buffer` regardless of how much of a given read gets dropped as a marker. If the footer is found mid-batch, the scan loop stops immediately rather than continuing to treat trailing bytes in that same read as data (the existing pad-with-zero safety net handles the rest).

**Scope:** this is the shared streaming primitive for both ROM dump and save read, so both should benefit. Save write and the marker-drain/footer-confirm helper loops (`newproto_read_marker()`, `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()`) were deliberately left at one-packet-at-a-time reads — they're bounded, one-time costs (at most ~8-64 reads), nowhere near the actual hot path, not worth the added complexity.

**What to test:** any ROM dump (Isolated Test → Dump ROM is the simplest). If per-call IOS overhead really is the dominant cost, total dump time should drop by something in the neighborhood of the same multiple the read size increased by (64x fewer calls) — not a promise of an exact factor, since there may be other floors (actual USB wire bandwidth, device-side pacing), but it should be a clearly noticeable difference from the ~5 minute baseline, not a marginal one. Also worth confirming dumps still complete correctly (no new corruption introduced by the batching) and that the existing detection (header-leak drop, footer-confirm rejection) still fires correctly on a corrupted attempt if one occurs.

### poll_cart debounce + auto_detect_cart setting (2026-07-28) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

User reported the background cart poll firing "very frequently," each time disabling controls for a noticeable stretch. Traced `poll_cart()` (`source/main.c`) directly: `*was_absent` (cart gone) was already debounced via `GBOP_POLL_CONFIRM_RETRIES`, but the "cart *changed*" signal had no debounce at all — a single successful cart-info read whose bytes merely differed from the last confirmed state immediately triggered the full, blocking `run_detect_cart_inner()`. On a connection with a measured ~5-20% per-attempt error rate, an occasional successful-but-noisy read (not a real physical swap) is exactly the kind of thing that would trip this often.

- **Fix 1 — debounce "changed" the same way "absent" is already debounced.** `poll_cart()` now requires the *same* differing `raw_resp` to show up on two consecutive ~1s polls before returning "changed" (new `static uint8_t s_pending_change[60]` / `s_pending_change_valid` inside the function). A genuine swap costs one extra poll cycle (~1s) of latency to confirm; a one-off noisy read no longer triggers anything. Reappearing after a confirmed absence is *not* additionally debounced here — that path is already gated by `GBOP_POLL_CONFIRM_RETRIES` on the way into `*was_absent`, and a real reinsertion should be picked up immediately.
- **Fix 2 — new setting, `auto_detect_cart`** (`source/settings.h`/`.c`, `settings.ini`), **defaulted to 0 (disabled) for now**. When off, `poll_cart()` still runs and still tracks presence/absence for the UI (so "cart removed" still shows correctly), but a sensed "changed" result never triggers `run_detect_cart_inner()` on its own — only explicit actions do (menu "Detect Cart", "Detect Cart Swap", Isolated Test, Dump Only all call `run_detect_cart_inner()` directly and are unaffected by this setting). Requested directly for testing: the user plans to use manual Detect Cart before each dump attempt rather than rely on the background poll. Set `auto_detect_cart=1` in `settings.ini` to restore automatic re-detection.
- **Scope check:** confirmed via `grep` that `run_detect_cart_inner()` has 5 call sites total — boot-time initial detect (unconditional, one-time, not gated), the one poll-triggered call (now gated), and three explicit manual actions (dev-menu Detect Cart, frontend Detect Cart Swap, Isolated Test) — all correctly left unconditional.
- **Not yet re-verified on hardware** — next session: confirm controls no longer freeze from background polling with `auto_detect_cart=0`, and that manual Detect Cart / Isolated Test / Dump ROM still work exactly as before.

### poll_cart follow-up (test_25, 2026-07-28) — the debounce fix above wasn't the whole story: an un-gated 5-retry confirm-absence loop was the actual remaining freeze source

test_25 (`logs/Post Firmware Update Test/test_25/`) confirmed `auto_detect_cart=0` loaded correctly and confirmed `run_detect_cart_inner()` never fired via the poll path (no `"[poll] Cart change — running auto-detect"` anywhere in the log) — the fix above worked exactly as designed. But the user still reported "auto detect still fired in the background" with controls frozen, and the log shows why: repeated cycles of

```
[poll] cart_info fail — retrying (1/5)
...
[poll] cart_info fail — retrying (5/5)
[poll] Cart removed
```

each spanning ~4.5 seconds (e.g. lines 552-608, `108150.125ms` to `112665.139ms`), recurring every few seconds throughout the log on this session's unreliable connection. This is `poll_cart()`'s **"confirm absence" retry loop** (`GBOP_POLL_CONFIRM_RETRIES`=5, `usleep(200000)` between each of 5 full reopen+read cycles) — a completely separate code path from the "confirm changed" debounce fixed above, and it was never gated by `auto_detect_cart` at all. It runs synchronously on the main thread with no input polling inside it, so each 4.5s cycle is a real, felt control freeze — indistinguishable to the user from "auto detect firing," even though the expensive full re-detect genuinely never ran.

- **Fix:** when `g_settings.auto_detect_cart == 0`, `poll_cart()` now skips the 5-retry confirm-absence loop entirely and concludes "no cart" from a single failed read. This is safe specifically because nothing expensive follows from a wrong "removed" conclusion when auto-detect is disabled — the only consequence is the on-screen cart status label flickering more readily on a bad connection, not a blocking retry sequence. `rc == GBOP_USB` (device-side transient stall, not a removal signal) is still handled the same way as before either way — it returns 0 immediately without touching `*was_absent`, since a stall was never evidence of removal in the first place.
- **Scope:** the small 1-retry `gbop_reopen()` fallback at the very top of `poll_cart()` (device not found at all) was left untouched — it's already just 2 attempts, not 5, and is a much smaller cost than the confirm-absence loop this fix targets.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next session should show the ~4.5s "cart_info fail — retrying" cycles gone entirely from the log when `auto_detect_cart=0`, replaced by single fast fail/recover checks.

### test_25's ROM dump itself, examined directly (2026-07-28) — a real, silent corruption bug found in the exact same dump the speed fix produced; a structural blind spot in the footer-confirmation logic, not a new device quirk

Asked directly whether anything was learned from the ROM dump test_25 itself produced (`POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba`, 16,777,216 bytes, logged `"[OK] Saved"`). Diffed it against the independently-verified-correct reference (`Pocket Monsters - Emerald (Japan).gba`, same SHA1 as an independently-sourced file, see the entry above) using the same method as test_16/18/20.

- **The dump is corrupt — a clean, single, 512-byte shift, but at the very START of the file this time (not mid-file like every prior instance).** `dump[0:4] = FF FF FF FF`, not the real reset vector (`7F 00 00 EA`); from byte 4 onward, every sampled offset (200 random positions, zero mismatches) satisfies `dump[X] == ref[X+512]`. This is the real ROM's genuine content, just uniformly displaced by exactly one marker-block (512 bytes) for the entire file — the real ROM's first 512 bytes (the actual header, logo, reset vector) were never delivered as data at all, and the last 512 bytes of the dumped file are zero (`dump[-64:]` all zero, vs. the real ROM's actual tail which is `0xFF`-padded flash). The log confirms this exactly: `"[newproto] stream(cmd=00) footer seen at 16776704 bytes"` — precisely 512 bytes short of the requested 16,777,216 — immediately followed by `"[OK] Saved"` with no rejection.
- **Root cause: a structural blind spot in `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()`, not a new device behavior.** That function (added test_20) was designed to catch a footer arriving LATE — i.e. checked only once `np_given >= np_total`, reading a few bounded extra packets to confirm the real footer is right there and rejecting if it isn't. It never considered the mirror-image case: the footer arriving EARLY (`np_footer_seen` going true while `np_given` is still below `np_total`). Traced why this matters: once `newproto_stream_body()` sees the footer, its pad-fill branch zero-fills the *per-call* `written` counter for every subsequent chunk request (so `rom_cache.c`'s outer loop believes each remaining chunk completed normally) but **never again advances `np_given`** — meaning if the footer arrives early, `np_given` can structurally never reach `np_total` afterward, so the `if (np_given >= np_total)` gate that guards the entire late-footer check is never satisfied, and `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()` is never even called. The dump completes "successfully" purely because the outer chunk-counting loop reached its expected offset — with zero bytes-received validation ever happening. Three prior corrupted-but-"successful" dumps (tests 10, 16, 18, 20) already showed the same shift shape mid-file; this is the same underlying blind spot, just never previously formalized as "early vs. late footer" or given its own check.
- **Fix is not "reject every early footer"** — an early footer is the *expected, correct* outcome for `rom_sizes.c`'s deliberately-oversized fallback guess on an unrecognized game (a real, smaller ROM legitimately ending before the generous guess) — see the existing "Do Not: Refactor rom_cache.c" entry. Rejecting unconditionally would break that legitimate case. Added a `rom_size_confirmed`/`ram_size_confirmed` int pair to `CartInfo` (`gb_operator.h`), set in `main.c`'s `enrich_info_from_buf()` based on whether `rom_sizes_lookup_rom_kb()`/`_ram_kb()` actually found a table entry (confirmed=1) or the caller fell back to the generous guess (confirmed=0). New `newproto_check_early_footer()` (`gb_operator.c`) runs immediately after every `newproto_stream_body()` call in both `gbop_dump_rom_new()` and `gbop_read_save_new()`, regardless of the `np_given >= np_total` gate: if the footer arrived early against an *unconfirmed* (fallback-guess) size, it's accepted exactly as before (logged, not rejected); if it arrived early against a *confirmed* (exact table-lookup) size, the dump/save read is now rejected outright (`dev->np_active = 0; return -1;`), which `dump_rom_with_retry()`/the save-read retry paths already treat as "attempt failed, retry" — the same safe failure mode every other corruption-detection fix in this project falls back to.
- **Emerald (BPEJ) is a `rom_sizes.c`-confirmed entry (16384 KB, CONFIRMED marker)**, so this exact scenario — the one that just slipped through silently — would now be caught and rejected instead of saved. Not yet re-verified on hardware; next dump attempt on this cart should either complete genuinely clean or fail loudly and retry, never silently produce a front-shifted file again.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.**

### Rejected-dump diagnostics: preserve partial bytes + log the actual "extra" packet content (2026-07-28) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

Prompted directly: since the early/late-footer rejection fixes now correctly refuse to save a corrupt dump, they also mean a corrupt dump's actual bytes are no longer available to compare against the reference Wireshark captures the way test_25's (pre-fix) silently-saved file was — that comparison is what found the front-of-stream 512-byte gap in the first place. Two additions, both diagnostic-only (no behavior change to what gets accepted/rejected):

- **`source/rom_cache.c`**: `rom_cache_dump()`'s failure path now preserves a copy of the just-written partial file before it can be destroyed. The reason this was previously lost: `dump_rom_with_retry()` (`main.c`) reopens the same output path with `fopen(..., "wb")` on every retry attempt, truncating whatever the previous attempt wrote — and since the early/late-footer checks only ever fire once `np_given` has nearly or fully reached `np_total`, a rejected attempt can represent almost the entire real ROM (e.g. test_25/26/27's recurring case: 16,776,704 of 16,777,216 bytes, 99.997% streamed) right before it gets silently wiped by the next attempt's truncate. Gated on `offset > 0` (skips the far more common case: a marker-mismatch failure that aborts at offset 0 before a single chunk is written — not interesting, would just spam the debug path) and `!aborted` (a user-initiated X+Y abort isn't a protocol corruption case). Copies to `<roms_dir>/_debug_last_rejected.bin`, overwritten each time — logged as `"[cache] Preserved N bytes of rejected dump for diagnostics: ..."`.
- **`source/gb_operator.c`**: `newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()` previously only logged that a non-footer packet appeared while scanning for the late footer (`"footer arrived N bytes LATE"` — a byte count, no content). It now calls `log_full_packet()` (the same full-64-byte hex dump helper added back in test_14) on every non-footer packet encountered during that scan, so a future "64 bytes LATE (1 extra packet)" rejection (seen in both test_26 and test_27) leaves the actual content of that extra packet in the log — previously invisible.
- **Open question this is aimed at, stated directly by the user's line of questioning:** every prior Wireshark-based finding (the 4x-repeat Emerald capture, the Emerald/Ruby/FireRed capture) showed the device's own content-triggered gap landing *mid-file* at a fixed-but-cart-specific address (~0x2A5000 for Emerald) — but every Wii-side dump examined so far (test_25's saved file) shows the gap at the very *front* of the stream instead, immediately after the header. Same gap size (512 bytes), different location. That mismatch hasn't been resolved — it's not yet established whether the Wii-side front-of-stream gap is the *same* phenomenon as the Windows-side mid-file gap (just triggering at a different point for some reason specific to the Wii's own read timing) or a *different* bug specific to how this codebase's own header-drain-then-stream handshake works. A preserved `_debug_last_rejected.bin` from a future early-footer rejection, diffed against the reference the same way test_25's file was, is the direct way to keep collecting evidence on this.
- **Not yet hardware-tested.**

### Speed pass: cut fixed delays and per-attempt logging overhead (2026-07-29) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

test_34 confirmed the pre-stream settle delay from last session showed no benefit (persistent-handle attempt, which always gets it, still 0/8) and, while investigating, surfaced a much bigger fixed cost: **1,152 `gbop_reopen()` calls in that one session**, each one running through `GBOP_D2X_SETTLE_US` — a 250ms unconditional sleep once the connection is detected as d2x-cIOS-style (which happens almost immediately and then sticks for the rest of the session). That's up to **4.8 minutes of pure sleep** in a single session, and — directly relevant — this delay's own justification was already disproven by this project's own research *before* the new-firmware protocol rebuild even started (test_21: the bug it was built to fix reproduced under plain IOS58 with no cIOS involved at all). It's been sitting in every reopen since, on a completely different protocol, never re-validated.

User asked directly what could be cut given reliability is fundamentally about retrying until a naturally clean exchange happens (test_34's conclusion) rather than something a code fix can force on the first attempt. Four changes, all reducing fixed cost with no behavior change to the protocol logic itself:

- **Removed `GBOP_D2X_SETTLE_US` (250ms) entirely from `gbop_reopen()`** (`source/gb_operator.c`). `s_ack_read_size` itself is untouched (still used for old-firmware ACK framing size detection elsewhere) — only the sleep gated on it is gone.
- **Removed `NEWPROTO_PRESTREAM_SETTLE_US` (20ms)** from both `gbop_read_rom_header_new()` and `gbop_dump_rom_new()` — last session's experiment, no measurable benefit in its one hardware test (0/8 persistent-handle successes, same as baseline).
- **Shrank the inter-retry pacing from 300ms to 100ms** (new shared `GBOP_RETRY_PACING_US`, `source/main.c`) in the three retry loops this session's slowness came from: `run_detect_cart_inner()`'s cart-info retry, `read_rom_header_with_retry()`'s retry, and `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s retry. No specific evidence 300ms was ever required for correctness; more attempts fit in the same wall-clock time.
- **Cut per-attempt logging overhead directly answering "does printing the lines make it take longer?" — yes, and two concrete instances were fixed:**
  1. `log_full_packet()` (`source/gb_operator.c`) was still making 2 `lprintf()` calls (each a printf + fprintf + fflush) even with `NEWPROTO_DIAGNOSTICS_ENABLED` off — a `"(N bytes):"` label and a bare newline, with zero actual hex content between them. Now skips entirely when diagnostics are disabled instead of emitting two empty-handed log lines on every single marker read in every attempt.
  2. `newproto_read_marker()`'s marker-block drain loop was logging every single padding packet unconditionally (up to 6 extra `lprintf()` calls per marker match, each almost always just `"rd=64"` with no new information). Now gated behind `NEWPROTO_DIAGNOSTICS_ENABLED`, replaced with one summary line (`"drained N padding packet(s)"`) when diagnostics are off — same forensic fact (how many packets drained), far fewer log/fflush calls.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next session should show noticeably less wall-clock time per retry cycle with the same underlying attempt-by-attempt behavior — worth comparing total session duration against test_34's for a similar number of attempts, not expecting any change to the success rate itself.

### ROM Dump Reconciliation — multi-attempt overlap stitching implemented (2026-07-29) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

Full design plan at the time of implementation is preserved in the session's plan file; summarized here. Implements the user's insight (refined through direct discussion): since a rejected dump attempt's captured bytes are always genuine, correct, deterministic ROM content forming one contiguous run from *some* true-ROM start offset (usually 0, but front-shifted in test_25's case), two attempts whose gaps land in different places can — if we can prove where they overlap — cover the whole ROM without needing a single lucky fully-clean stream.

- **Honesty check done before implementing, not after:** grepping every early-footer rejection across `Post Firmware Update Test/test_25` through `test_34` (10 real gap events) found **9 of 10 report the exact same shortfall** (512 bytes missing, footer at byte 16,776,704 of 16,777,216), much closer to the deterministic, content-triggered pattern already confirmed on the PC/Epilogue side (the 4x-repeat Wireshark capture) than to independent per-attempt noise. This means two attempts will *frequently* reproduce the identical gap and gain nothing from stitching — the tool is explicitly designed to recognize and report this (`RECONCILE_REDUNDANT`) as a distinct, expected outcome rather than a generic failure, and to support capturing more than 2 attempts (up to 4 slots) since a single deterministic gap may mean any given pair doesn't complement each other.
- **Safety framing governing every design choice**: a wrong alignment that passes validation would silently produce a corrupted "verified" ROM — exactly the failure mode this project has spent enormous effort eliminating elsewhere (test_10, test_17). Every step rejects and reports a clean failure rather than guessing, interpolating, or accepting a "probably fine" match.
- **`source/rom_cache.c`/`.h`**: extracted `rom_cache_stream_chunks()` — the per-4096-byte-chunk streaming loop `rom_cache_dump()` already had — as a shared, reusable primitive. `rom_cache_dump()`'s own behavior is unchanged (pure extraction); the new reconciliation capture path reuses the identical loop instead of duplicating it.
- **`source/gb_operator.c`/`.h`**: `header_checksum_valid()` (previously `static`) is now exposed, reused by reconciliation as the anchor-selection and final-gate check — the same hardware-mandated checksum a real GBA BIOS/DMG boot ROM validates before it'll boot a cartridge.
- **New `source/rom_reconcile.h`/`.c`**: `rom_reconcile_capture()` streams one attempt to a numbered scratch file. `rom_reconcile_align_and_merge()` implements the core algorithm on exactly 2 attempts at a time:
  1. Refuse immediately if `info->rom_size_confirmed` isn't set (need a real, trustworthy total).
  2. Free fast path: either attempt already complete + header-valid → done, no reconciliation needed.
  3. **Anchor selection never assumes shift-0 from capture order** (test_25 proved that's unsafe) — `header_checksum_valid()` is checked on *each* attempt's own start; whichever validates is the anchor; if neither validates, `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR` (no way to bootstrap a trustworthy zero-point).
  4. Cheap direct comparison at shift-0 first — definitive if it matches (stronger proof than a sample search), and expected to be the common real-world result per the finding above; if the union still doesn't cover the total, this is `RECONCILE_REDUNDANT` (both start at 0, no complementary gain possible) rather than a generic gap.
  5. Only if shift-0 genuinely fails: sample-search up to 8 candidate 256-byte windows (skipping degenerate/repetitive ones), requiring a **unique** match in the other buffer before considering it.
  6. **Zero-tolerance full-overlap validation** — any single mismatch across the entire claimed overlap region (minimum 4096 bytes) rejects the candidate; this is the actual proof, not the uniqueness check.
  7. Coverage check: union of the two validated ranges must reach `[0, total)` with no hole, or `RECONCILE_GAP` with the exact missing range reported — never filled with zeros or a guess.
  8. Merge: anchor's buffer is allocated at the full `total` size up front, so only the non-overlapping tail of the other buffer needs copying in — peak RAM stays at 2×`total` (e.g. ~32MB for a 16MB GBA ROM), not 3×.
  9. Final gate: `header_checksum_valid()` on the assembled result (defense in depth; should be unreachable given step 6, but never skipped).
  - `d` (the non-anchor attempt's shift) is always computed in a signed 64-bit type and validated (`d >= 0`, `d + L_other <= total`) before ever being used as a buffer offset — a sign error or spurious match must never silently wrap into a huge unsigned value and become an out-of-bounds `memcpy`.
- **Dev-menu screen replaces "Command Test Lab"** (index 12, per direct user feedback that tool hadn't been a productive line of testing) — up to 4 capture slots + "Align & Reconcile" (tries every pair among captured slots, stopping at the first `RECONCILE_OK`, logging every pair's outcome including redundant ones) + "Back". Same heap-safety discipline Command Test Lab learned the hard way (test_23/24): `log_commit_sd()` at most once on entry and exit, `log_force_flush()` for every action in between.
- **Scope: GBA only** for this first implementation — GBC (2MB, no variable-size lookups) is expected to behave consistently but untested with this failure mode.
- **Not yet hardware-tested.** First test should confirm captures produce correct short-prefix files; second should confirm two same-cart captures mostly land on `RECONCILE_REDUNDANT` per the finding above (that's success, not failure, of the tool); only a pair with genuinely different gaps (e.g. a front-shift alongside a tail-cut) should produce `RECONCILE_OK` — verify its SHA1 against the known-good Emerald reference if that's the cart under test.

### First hardware test of ROM Reconcile (`Rom Stitching Test/test_1`, 2026-07-29) — no success with 2 captures; direct byte analysis found the gaps were NOT the same, but neither attempt had a usable anchor; two enhancements added, NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

Capture speed was fine (rapid A-presses across 4 slots worked). "Align & Reconcile" tried every pair and failed all of them with `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR`. Direct byte comparison of all 4 captured files against the SHA1-verified Emerald reference (same method as test_16/18/20) found a third, previously-uncatalogued gap shape:

- `attempt_0`/`attempt_3`: correct for real-ROM bytes [0:64), then the file's remaining bytes match the reference starting at real offset 576 — i.e. real bytes [64:576) (512 bytes) are missing, an **internal, non-edge gap immediately past the entry vector**, distinct from every previously-seen front-shift (test_25) or tail-cut (test_33/34) shape.
- `attempt_1`/`attempt_2`: a clean, full-length 512-byte front-shift (matches the reference shifted by 512 across the entire captured length) — the same shape as test_25's corruption, i.e. real bytes [0:512) are missing entirely.
- **Neither shape gives any attempt an intact header/logo region**: `attempt_0/3` only has 64 real bytes before the internal gap swallows the rest of the logo; `attempt_1/2` never captured real offset 0 at all. `header_checksum_valid()` (which needs bytes through offset 0xBD) correctly failed on all 4 — this was not a false negative, there genuinely was no complete header anywhere in this specific dataset. Confirmed via `grep` that the `"sample search took"` timing line never appears anywhere in the log — every pair was rejected at the anchor-selection step, before any content-comparison code ever ran.
- **Timing root cause identified**: each `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR` pair cost ~3.5 seconds — not from the (never-reached) sample search, but purely from `fread()`-ing two ~16MB files into RAM before the anchor check (which itself only needs ~200-300 bytes) ever ran.
- **The union of attempt_0-shape and attempt_1-shape data still wouldn't have reached `RECONCILE_OK` even with a perfect anchor mechanism**: `attempt_0` covers real [0:64) ∪ [576:total), `attempt_1` covers real [512:total); their union is [0:64) ∪ [512:total), still missing real [64:512) — a 448-byte hole neither attempt's data contains. This session's specific 4 captures could not have been fully reconciled by any algorithm; a 5th capture with a different shape was always going to be needed.

**Two enhancements implemented in response (both in `source/rom_reconcile.c`), NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED:**

1. **Partial-front anchor validation via the fixed Nintendo logo, answering the user's direct question ("is there not enough data in the first 64 bytes... or do you need the first 512?").** `header_checksum_valid()` needs the full header region and can't help a 64-byte-only front. But every licensed cart is hardware-required to carry the identical, universal logo bytes — so an exact partial-prefix match against a known-correct copy of those bytes is strong, independent evidence on its own, without needing the full checksum region. Added `GBA_LOGO[156]`/`GB_LOGO[48]` + `logo_prefix_match_len()` + `anchor_score()` (2=full checksum, 1=partial logo match ≥32 bytes, 0=neither). **The logo bytes were deliberately not typed from memory** — they were extracted programmatically from four independently-sourced GBA ROM files already in this repo and cross-verified byte-identical across all four, with the extracted sequence's CRC32 (`0xD0BEB55E`) matching vendor/mgba's own `LOGO_CRC32` constant exactly; the GB/GBC logo was extracted from the Pokemon Silver reference file and cross-checked against a real, independently-recomputed passing header checksum on that same file. See the new GBATEK/Pan Docs Sources rows for the full verification chain.
   - **Honest limitation, traced through by hand before implementing**: this fix would **not** have salvaged test_1's specific 4 attempts — `attempt_0/3`'s logo region is itself corrupted by the internal gap (only the first 60 of 156 logo bytes are genuinely present before the gap swallows the rest), and `attempt_1/2` never captured the logo region at all. It's a real, general improvement for the common case of a short-but-clean front capture; it doesn't turn this specific dataset into a success.
   - **The arithmetic "distance to the end" safety check the user proposed (idea 1 from the prior message) was validated by hand and confirmed already effectively in place**: the sample-search step already rejects any implied shift where `d < 0 || d + other_len > total` (this was written into the original implementation, not new). Worked through concretely: this check is *exactly* what makes it safe to only ever check position 0 for the logo/header, rather than needing to scan the whole buffer and worry about confusing the real header with Emerald's second, embedded copy at ROM offset ~0x912458 (an embedded GBA multiboot payload) — any candidate start offset implied by matching that distant copy would require a deeply negative `S`, which the bound rejects outright. Scanning the whole buffer for the logo was considered and *not* implemented, because the arithmetic bound proves it would only ever validly resolve to position 0 anyway (same result, more cost) — see the session's own by-hand trace in conversation for the full derivation.
2. **Speed: the anchor check no longer requires loading full-size buffers.** `rom_reconcile_align_and_merge()` now reads only a small (≤512-byte) prefix from each file first and runs `anchor_score()` against that; if neither scores above 0, it returns `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR` immediately, without ever allocating or `fread()`-ing the full total-sized buffers. This directly targets the measured ~3.5s/pair cost from this test (which was pure file I/O, not algorithm cost) — the common "no anchor" case should now resolve in well under a millisecond of actual I/O instead.
3. **"Marked jump points" (user's other idea, from the earlier stream-jump discussion, folded in here rather than dropped):** a small session-local hint list (`s_known_gaps[]`, up to 8 entries) now records the boundary offset from every `RECONCILE_REDUNDANT`/`RECONCILE_GAP` result. Future sample searches in the same session try these remembered offsets first, before falling back to blind even-spacing — since the dominant real-world case is a deterministic, address-tied gap that recurs at the same position for a given cart (confirmed independently on the PC/Epilogue side via the 4x-repeat Wireshark capture, and via the 9-of-10 test_25-34 tally), a gap position seen once this session is a good bet to seed future searches with. This changes only which candidates get tried first — every candidate, known-gap-sourced or not, is still subject to the identical uniqueness check and zero-tolerance full-overlap validation as before, so it cannot make a wrong alignment more likely to pass, only a right one faster to find.
- **Not yet hardware-tested.** Next session should re-run "Align & Reconcile" and check: (a) whether the cheap precheck visibly speeds up `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR` resolution, (b) whether a capture with a genuinely clean 64+ byte front (no internal gap) now gets accepted as an anchor via the logo-partial path (score=1) where it previously couldn't validate at all, and (c) whether a 5th capture attempt, aimed specifically at covering real offset [64:512), would finally let this session's own 4 files reconcile in combination with one of them.

### Second hardware test (`Rom Stitching Test/test_2`, 2026-07-29) — a "success" that wasn't stitching at all: two independent full clean dumps

`log.txt` showed no alignment ever ran — slot 0 and slot 1 both eventually landed a fully complete, footer-confirmed 16MB stream on their own (slot 0 needed 5 attempts, slot 1 needed roughly 15), and "Align & Reconcile" simply hit the existing free-fast-path shortcut (`RECONCILE_OK` via `attempt_0.bin already complete and valid`) without ever reaching anchor selection or the sample search. SHA1 of both captured files and the reconciled output all matched the independently-verified reference (`d7cf8f15...`). Real, useful confirmation that the capture-with-retry loop can land a clean dump on its own, but not a test of the stitching mechanism at all.

### Third hardware test (`Rom Stitching Test/test_3`, 2026-07-29) — "significantly worse": every pair hit `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR`; exact gap layout mapped; a genuinely new corruption shape found (packet transposition); a real scoring bug found in the anchor check

All 4 captures were short (16,773,120 bytes, 4096 short of total) and every one of the 6 possible pairs failed at `RECONCILE_NO_ANCHOR`. Direct byte-level resolution against the reference (walking each attempt as a sequence of contiguous real-ROM segments) found:
- `attempt_0`/`attempt_1`: identical to each other — a clean, full-length 512-byte front-shift (`data[0:] == ref[512:16773632)`), same shape as test_25/test_1's attempt_1/2.
- `attempt_3`: correct for real [0,64), then real [64,512) (448 bytes) missing, resumes cleanly from real 512 onward — same shape as test_1's attempt_0/3.
- `attempt_2`: the messiest capture found anywhere in this project so far — correct for real [0,64), then the two 64-byte chunks belonging to real [512,576) and [576,640) arrive **in swapped order**, followed by a **separate 64-byte gap** (real [640,704) missing entirely), before resuming cleanly from real 704 onward. A packet transposition plus an adjacent gap, not just a missing chunk — a new failure shape, not previously catalogued.
- **Union of every combination still leaves real [64,512) (448 bytes) uncovered** — no observed attempt, across test_1 or test_3, has ever contained that specific range. Confirmed as a real, repeated pattern (not a small-sample coincidence) once tallied across both sessions.
- **Root cause of the `NO_ANCHOR` result for attempt_2/attempt_3 specifically**: `logo_prefix_match_len()` (as first written) compared the *entire* available precheck buffer (up to the full 156-byte logo) in one all-or-nothing pass — since content beyond byte 64 in both captures is corrupted/reordered rather than continuing the logo, the comparison failed outright and credited 0 matched bytes, even though the first 64 bytes were genuinely, exactly correct. **Fixed** (see below) to score the longest matching prefix instead of requiring the whole window to agree.
- **Traced by hand whether this fix alone would have salvaged test_3's dataset: it wouldn't have.** Being an anchor means the algorithm treats the *entire* buffer as offset-0-consistent; attempt_2/attempt_3 both violate that internally (a real gap partway through). A relaxed score would let one of them become an anchor, and the sample search would likely converge on a self-consistent (but wrong, in the sense of not reflecting the true header layout) shift against another attempt — but the final `header_checksum_valid()` gate on the assembled output would almost certainly still catch and reject it (`RECONCILE_HEADER_FAILED`), since the merged bytes at the header-region file offsets would actually be unrelated game content. The existing defense-in-depth gate holds either way; it just means an attempt with an internal gap can only safely anchor the bytes *before* that gap, a limitation not accounted for in the original single-(S,L)-pair design.

### User pushback + real data: the "peek and abort" idea is the same operation as cart detection's existing ROM-header read — and per-attempt cost is dominated by USB reopen overhead, not streaming duration (2026-07-30)

User correctly identified that a proposed new "cheap peek, abort, retry" diagnostic would just be reimplementing `gbop_read_rom_header()` (cmd 0x00 requesting only 512 bytes), already used by `read_rom_header_with_retry()`/`run_detect_cart_inner()` for cart detection, and already measured extensively across dozens of sessions. Checked directly rather than assumed:
- **1,831 total cmd=00 handshake attempts logged across all `Post Firmware Update Test` sessions; only 48 (~2.6%) got a clean `C0 DE` marker match.** Of those classified specifically: 6 were 512-byte header-read peeks, and 5 of those 6 (83%) went on to fully validate (real game code, real title) — confirming a clean marker, when it happens, very reliably means the whole front is intact, not just a lucky partial match. Of 17 classified as full-dump-starts with a clean marker, only 2 reached completion within the log window — most hit a *separate*, already-documented later corruption deeper in the stream, unrelated to the front.
- **Real per-attempt timing pulled directly from logs**: both `read_rom_header_with_retry` and `run_detect_cart_inner`'s retry loops cost **~650ms–1000ms per attempt** (e.g. `61661.156ms → 62318.148ms → 62975.147ms`, consistently 650-1100ms apart), and reconcile's own failed-dump-start attempts (test_3) show the *same* ~350ms-1000ms per-attempt cost. **Both are bottlenecked by the same USB-reopen + IOS-round-trip overhead, not by streaming duration** — a failed marker check aborts in ~25ms either way (directly measured). Conclusion: gating reconcile behind the existing header-read function would NOT have sped up the dominant failure case (marker fails outright) — that idea was retracted. The real, harder lever (cutting the ~650-1000ms/attempt reopen overhead itself) is a problem this project has already investigated extensively (persistent-handle experiments, reopen-delay removal) without a decisive win.

### Fast front-shift/gap check added directly inside the ROM dump stream (2026-07-30) — NOT YET HARDWARE-TESTED

User's refined proposal, after the above: rather than a separate peek-then-retry cycle (redundant with cart detection, as established above), check the very front **inside the actual dump stream itself**, the instant enough bytes have arrived, and abort immediately on a mismatch — going back to a fresh handshake rather than continuing to stream for the ~40s a 16MB dump takes. This is different from the retracted peek idea: it doesn't add a new round-trip cost, since `gbop_dump_rom_new()`'s first read after a clean marker match already has to happen regardless — this just inspects that first packet's content before committing to the rest of the stream.

- **`source/gb_operator.c`/`.h`**: moved the Nintendo logo tables (`GBA_LOGO[156]`, `GB_LOGO[48]`, previously private to `rom_reconcile.c`) to `gb_operator.c` as the canonical, shared location, exposed via `gbop_logo_prefix_match_len(CartType, buf, avail)` and `GBOP_LOGO_MIN_BYTES` (32). **Fixed the scoring bug found in test_3 analysis** along the way: the function now returns the *longest matching prefix*, not an all-or-nothing full-window match — an attempt genuinely correct for its first 64 bytes and then diverging (attempt_2/attempt_3's shape) is now credited for those 64 bytes instead of scoring 0. `rom_reconcile.c`'s `anchor_score()` now calls this shared function instead of keeping its own private duplicate.
- **`gbop_dump_rom_new()`**: immediately after a clean marker match (`m==0`) and before committing to the full `newproto_stream_body()` loop, for **GBA only** (the logo lands entirely within the first 64-byte packet, right after the 4-byte entry vector — GB/GBC's logo doesn't start until byte 0x104, well past packet 0, so this can't apply there yet), reads exactly the first data packet and checks it against `gbop_logo_prefix_match_len()`. Below `GBOP_LOGO_MIN_BYTES` (32) matched → abort immediately (`dev->np_active = 0; return -1;`), letting the caller's existing retry loop (`dump_rom_with_retry`/`reconcile_capture_slot`) cycle back to a fresh handshake in a fraction of a second instead of after a ~40s stream. At or above threshold → the already-read packet is spliced into the caller's buffer (`stream_buf`/`stream_size` adjusted so the shared tail — progress log, `newproto_check_early_footer`, completion check — runs exactly once, unduplicated) and streaming continues normally.
- **Directly targets the recurring front-shift shape** (real bytes [0,512) missing, confirmed identically in test_1's attempt_1/2 and test_3's attempt_0/1) — this shape gets a genuinely clean marker match (protocol framing is fine) but the device's own internal read pointer starts from the wrong address; this check catches that mismatch in one packet instead of a full stream.
- **Scope decision**: this check runs inside `gbop_dump_rom_new()` itself, not just the reconcile capture path — it applies to every GBA dump (normal caching included), since a front-shifted dump is never useful as a real ROM either way; there is no case where continuing such a stream to completion would help.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.** Next session should show `"front check passed (N logo bytes matched)"` or `"first packet doesn't match known ROM logo ... aborting early"` firing almost immediately after most dump-start attempts, and — the actual goal — a much higher rate of reaching a genuinely clean, useful capture per unit of wall-clock time, since front-shifted attempts should now cost a fraction of a second instead of tens of seconds each.

### Fourth hardware test (`Rom Stitching Test/test_4`, 2026-07-30) — front-check confirmed working for normal dumps; reconcile captures show no front-check log line at all (diagnostic gap identified and fixed); a real, always-paid reopen cost found and fixed

Two things asked directly: (1) what pre-dump checks exist and which can be cut for speed, (2) why does reconcile still show no matching pairs — is the front-check not running there?

- **Front-check confirmed working on real hardware for the normal "Dump ROM" path**: `"[newproto] ROM dump: front check passed (60 logo bytes matched)"` appears once, on the attempt that went on to become the successful, complete `POKEMON_EMER_BPEJ.gba`. First direct hardware confirmation the check fires and correctly credits a genuine clean front.
- **A real, always-paid cost found in `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` (`main.c`), called before every normal "Dump ROM"/`play_game` dump (NOT before reconcile captures, which don't call it at all)**: it unconditionally did `gbop_close()` + `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` after calling `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()`, **even when that function's own internal gate decided no verification was needed at all** (an anomalous cart-info byte pattern check — confirmed in this exact log: `resp[26]=0x00`, failing the gate, so no mirror-check streaming happened) — the log shows two back-to-back `reopen:` sequences immediately after pressing "Dump ROM" for exactly this reason. **Fix**: `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()` (`gb_operator.c`/`.h`) gained an `int *out_did_stream` out-parameter, set to 1 only when the function actually entered its streaming loop (both gates passed). `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` now only pays for the close+reopen-wait-fresh cycle when `did_stream` is true; otherwise the original handle is reused untouched. This removes one full reopen cycle from every single "Dump ROM"/play_game dump attempt whose cart doesn't show the doubling-quirk byte pattern.
- **Reconcile captures show zero `"front check passed"` / `"doesn't match known ROM logo"` log lines anywhere, despite a confirmed clean marker match** (`"drained 7 padding packet(s)"` at line 1123, followed immediately by normal streaming progress lines with nothing in between) for the capture that became `attempt_0.bin` (16,773,120 bytes, later rejected via `"footer arrived EARLY... 512 bytes missing"` — a different, already-documented corruption shape, not a front problem). Traced the code and found a genuine diagnostic blind spot: the branch in `gbop_dump_rom_new()`'s front-check where the very first post-marker read isn't a clean, exactly-64-byte data packet (a ZLP, an oversized/coalesced read, or a footer/header-shaped packet) had **no logging at all** — it silently falls through to normal streaming, making "check ran but hit this edge case" indistinguishable from "check never ran." **Fix**: added explicit logging to that branch (`"front check skipped — first read after marker was rd=%d..."`) and to the `buffer_size`/type precondition itself, so the next test conclusively shows which case is occurring instead of requiring guesswork from a silent gap. Not yet re-verified on hardware — this is a diagnostics fix, not a behavior change to the check's own logic.

### Fifth hardware test (`Rom Stitching Test/test_5`, 2026-07-30) — root cause of the "reopen runs so much" complaint found and fixed: wrong recovery tool for an ordinary failure

New front-check diagnostic logging (added after test_4) paid off immediately: `"front check skipped — first read after marker was rd=0..."` confirms a ZLP consistently arrives right after the marker drain, both during normal "Dump ROM" attempts AND during reconcile captures — the check genuinely does run for reconcile (contrary to the working theory after test_4), it's just tripping on this ZLP every time. Not yet fixed (needs a small tolerance for a leading ZLP before giving up) — flagged for next session.

The bigger finding, directly answering "why does reopen run so much": traced a single, uninterrupted burst of **~75 back-to-back `reopen: OpenDevice`/`ep_out=01 ep_in=81` pairs**, ~70-80ms apart, same fd (`-434241503`) every single time — about 5-6 seconds of pure waste in one spot. Root cause: `gbop_verify_gba_rom_size()`'s own gate fired this session (it hadn't in test_4 — the anomalous byte pattern varies session to session), its internal ROM-read failed at the marker stage (the ordinary ~97%-of-attempts outcome, confirmed at the log line right before: `header marker mismatch... ROM size verification aborted`), and `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` responded by reaching for `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` — a mechanism built for a genuinely spent/stale fd, retrying up to 75 times waiting for the fd to change. On this hardware the fd never cycles at all, so it burns the entire budget every time this fires, for a failure that is not meaningfully different from any other single failed marker read `dump_rom_with_retry()` already recovers from instantly with a plain reopen.

- **Fix**: `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` (`main.c`) now tries a fast, plain `gbop_reopen()` first — matching `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s own established pattern of fast retries before ever reaching for the expensive fresh-fd wait — and only falls back to `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` if that fast reopen itself fails to produce a working handle.
- **This directly closes the gap between "Dump ROM" and a manual reconcile capture attempt** the user asked about: reconcile never calls `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` at all (confirmed via `rom_cache_exists()`/verify call-site grep — reconcile skips both), which is why manual capture presses already felt fast; "Dump ROM" was paying this one-time but very expensive tax on top of an otherwise equally-lean automatic retry loop. With this fixed, "Dump ROM"'s automated loop should now cost about the same per attempt as manually mashing "Capture Attempt" would, just automated.
- **Full step-by-step comparison, Dump ROM vs. reconcile capture** (for reference): both funnel through the identical `gbop_dump_rom_new()` (same front-check, same marker handling) — the only structural differences are (1) Dump ROM checks `rom_cache_exists()` first (reconcile always captures fresh, no SD check), (2) Dump ROM calls `verify_rom_size_before_dump()` once per press (reconcile never calls it), (3) Dump ROM retries automatically up to 15 times per press with a fast reopen between each (reconcile does one attempt per button press, retried manually by the user).
- **Not yet hardware-verified.**

### Sixth hardware test (`Rom Stitching Test/test_6`, 2026-07-30) — a second, separate reopen burst found and fixed at the root; mid-stream gap detection question answered

Confirmed the previous fix (fast reopen before wait-fresh in `verify_rom_size_before_dump`) is working: the mirror-check's gate fired this session, its internal read failed immediately, and only a *single* fast reopen followed — no burst. But the user correctly reported reopen still runs a lot after a failed attempt overall: found a **second, separate** ~2.4s burst (30 back-to-back reopens, same fd every time) from `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s **own inline fresh-fd-wait loop** (`GBOP_DUMP_FRESH_FD_TRIES`, reached after all 15 fast in-place retries fail) — a near-duplicate of `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` written directly in `main.c`, not a call to the shared function, so the earlier fix never touched it.

- **Root-level fix, not another one-off patch**: `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` (`gb_operator.c`) now tracks `s_fd_known_unstable`, a session-wide flag set the first time its own 75-try budget fully exhausts without the fd ever changing — real, directly-observed evidence (not a guess) that this connection never cycles the fd. Exposed via `gbop_fd_known_unstable()`. Once set, `gbop_reopen_wait_fresh()` itself skips straight to one fast reopen on all future calls, **and** `dump_rom_with_retry()`'s own separate inline loop in `main.c` now checks the same flag before starting its 30-try loop, skipping it entirely once established. Confirmed directly from test_6: every single reopen across the whole session (~200+) showed the identical fd — one full exhaustion anywhere is already strong, well-supported evidence for the rest of the session, not a fluke worth re-discovering on every subsequent call site.
- **Not yet hardware-verified.**

### Mid-stream gap detection — what already exists, and what isn't possible without a reference (2026-07-30)

Asked directly whether a 512-byte gap occurring mid-stream can be detected and the attempt rejected, rather than only discovering it after the fact.

- **This is already happening, just not early.** Every "short" reconcile capture examined so far (test_1/3/4/5, all landing at 16,773,120 instead of 16,777,216) already triggered the existing `newproto_check_early_footer()`/`newproto_confirm_footer_immediate()` rejection internally — that rejection is *why* `gbop_dump_rom()` returns failure and the capture ends up short at all. Reconcile's capture wrapper deliberately *keeps* whatever was written before that rejection (the whole point of the feature) rather than discarding it the way normal caching does. So the correctness guarantee already exists — a gap of this kind cannot silently produce a "successful" file — but it doesn't save time, since the rejection only fires once the footer actually shows up, which can be most of the way through a 16MB stream.
- **A general, real-time, content-based mid-stream check (for an arbitrary attempt with no reference) isn't possible with information currently available.** Checked against everything already established in this project: the observed gaps (packet transposition, repeating cycles, the dominant [64,512) hole) leave no marker/footer-shaped signature at the boundary in most cases (`newproto_is_header()`/`newproto_is_footer()` already scan every packet throughout the whole stream and would already catch that specific case), and a timing-based heuristic was already directly disproven earlier in this project (the Wireshark timing analysis found perfectly uniform inter-packet delays straight through a known gap, no pause or anomaly of any kind). Without a reference ROM, there is nothing to compare against.
